# Daily Summary: 2025-09-11

### M-BRe: Discovering Training Samples for Relation Extraction from Unlabeled Texts with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07730v2)
- **Authors**: Zexuan Li, Hongliang Dai, Piji Li
- **Abstract**: For Relation Extraction (RE), the manual annotation of training data may be prohibitively expensive, since the sentences that contain the target relations in texts can be very scarce and difficult to find. It is therefore beneficial to develop an efficient method that can automatically extract training instances from unlabeled texts for training RE models. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been adopted in various natural language processing tasks, with RE also benefiting from their advances. However, when leveraging LLMs for RE with predefined relation categories, two key challenges arise. First, in a multi-class classification setting, LLMs often struggle to comprehensively capture the semantics of every relation, leading to suboptimal results. Second, although employing binary classification for each relation individually can mitigate this issue, it introduces significant computational overhead, resulting in impractical time complexity for real-world applications. Therefore, this paper proposes a framework called M-BRe to extract training instances from unlabeled texts for RE. It utilizes three modules to combine the advantages of both of the above classification approaches: Relation Grouping, Relation Extraction, and Label Decision. Extensive experiments confirm its superior capability in discovering high-quality training samples from unlabeled texts for RE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "M-BRe: Discovering Training Samples for Relation Extraction from Unlabeled Texts with Large Language Models" proposes a framework called M-BRe to automatically extract training instances for Relation Extraction (RE) from unlabeled texts using Large Language Models (LLMs). The framework addresses two main challenges: (1) the difficulty of LLMs to comprehensively capture the semantics of every relation in multi-class classification, and (2) the high computational overhead of using binary classification for each relation individually. M-BRe combines three modules: *Relation Grouping* (partitioning relation types into distinguishable groups), *Relation Extraction* (using LLMs for multi-class classification within groups and binary classification for individual validation), and *Label Decision* (determining the final labels based on confidence scores). The authors demonstrate through experiments on standard RE benchmarks that M-BRe can effectively discover high-quality training samples from unlabeled texts, improving the performance of conventional RE models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a relatively novel approach to leveraging LLMs for RE training data generation by combining multi-class and binary classification strategies in a staged manner. The Relation Grouping module is a key contribution, aiming to optimize LLM performance by partitioning the relation space intelligently. This is a smart attempt to mitigate the LLM's weaknesses in large multi-class classification settings while improving efficiency. The method avoids direct text generation, and instead classifies existing text, which can lead to better quality examples, as the context is from real sentences.

*   **Significance:**  The work addresses a crucial problem in RE: the scarcity of labeled training data. By providing a method to automatically generate training data from unlabeled text, the paper has the potential to lower the cost and increase the availability of RE models for various domains. The reported experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements compared to using manually labeled samples alone, and comparable to other related methods, which is a strong indicator of its potential. The framework is modular and relatively easy to implement.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The method attempts to address an important and real problem.
    *   The design of M-BRe is well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   Experiments are thorough, covering several standard datasets and model variations.
    *   The ablation studies and analysis of grouping strategies offer valuable insights.
    *   The code is publicly available, increasing reproducibility and adoption.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on LLMs means the quality of the generated training data is limited by the LLM's inherent knowledge and biases.
    *   While the paper shows improvements over baselines, the analysis of the generated data quality itself is somewhat limited. Metrics like precision and recall on the generated data, even against a small set of gold examples, would strengthen the argument.
    *   The "long-tail issue" remains, although it is acknowledged in the Limitations section. The ability of the framework to address the minority class problem could be tested using other methods such as oversampling.
    *   The performance improvement after the use of the LLM generated data is inconsistent. The quality variability of the generated training samples introduces noise.
* **Rigorous Rationale**:
The paper is assigned a score of 7. The novelty of the idea is interesting, but there are some limitations in its approach. Although the paper combines existing components and LLMs into a working framework, the framework is relatively simple and has limited potential.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Factuality Beyond Coherence: Evaluating LLM Watermarking Methods for Medical Texts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07755v1)
- **Authors**: Rochana Prih Hastuti, Rian Adam Rajagede, Mansour Al Ghanim, Mengxin Zheng, Qian Lou
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) adapted to sensitive domains such as medicine, their fluency raises safety risks, particularly regarding provenance and accountability. Watermarking embeds detectable patterns to mitigate these risks, yet its reliability in medical contexts remains untested. Existing benchmarks focus on detection-quality tradeoffs, overlooking factual risks under low-entropy settings often exploited by watermarking's reweighting strategy. We propose a medical-focused evaluation workflow that jointly assesses factual accuracy and coherence. Using GPT-Judger and further human validation, we introduce the Factuality-Weighted Score (FWS), a composite metric prioritizing factual accuracy beyond coherence to guide watermarking deployment in medical domains. Our evaluation shows current watermarking methods substantially compromise medical factuality, with entropy shifts degrading medical entity representation. These findings underscore the need for domain-aware watermarking approaches that preserve the integrity of medical content.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Factuality Beyond Coherence: Evaluating LLM Watermarking Methods for Medical Texts":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the critical gap in evaluating LLM watermarking methods specifically within the medical domain.  Current evaluation benchmarks primarily focus on detection quality and general text coherence, neglecting the vital aspect of factual accuracy, especially concerning medical terminology and potential hallucinations. The authors propose a medical-focused evaluation workflow that jointly assesses factual accuracy and coherence, using GPT-Judger and human validation to introduce the Factuality-Weighted Score (FWS).  FWS is a composite metric designed to prioritize factual accuracy over coherence, guiding the deployment of watermarking in sensitive medical contexts. Their evaluation reveals that existing watermarking methods often compromise medical factuality, particularly due to entropy shifts that degrade the representation of medical entities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its domain-specific focus on medical text and the prioritization of factual accuracy in watermarking evaluation. While watermarking itself isn't new, and GPT-Judger-based evaluations are becoming more common, the explicit focus on medical factuality and the introduction of FWS to reflect this priority represents a novel contribution. The entropy analysis to explain the factuality degradation is also a valuable insight.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work is substantial. As LLMs are increasingly deployed in medical applications (e.g., diagnosis assistance, patient education), ensuring factual accuracy and provenance becomes paramount. By demonstrating the potential for existing watermarking methods to degrade medical factuality, the authors raise crucial safety concerns and highlight the need for domain-aware watermarking strategies. The FWS metric offers a practical tool for evaluating and comparing different watermarking methods based on their ability to maintain factual integrity. The analysis of medical entity degradation provides practical insight for medical domain-aware watermarking development.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of evaluating watermarking methods in the medical domain and the limitations of existing benchmarks.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The FWS metric is well-defined and justified. The experimental setup using GPT-Judger and human evaluation strengthens the validity of the results.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The study provides actionable insights for developing domain-aware watermarking approaches that preserve medical factuality.
    *   **Code and Data Availability:**  The availability of code and data enhances reproducibility and allows other researchers to build upon their work.
    *   **Rigorous Analysis:** The entropy analysis and the detailed examination of entity hallucinations provide a solid foundation for understanding the causes of factuality degradation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Tasks:** While the three chosen tasks are representative of medical applications, the scope could be expanded to include other important tasks such as clinical note generation or medical report summarization.
    *   **Dependence on GPT-Judger:**  The GPT-Judger evaluation, while useful, is subject to biases and limitations inherent in LLM-based evaluation. Further human validation would strengthen the claims.
    *   **Limited exploration of mitigation strategies:** While the paper convincingly demonstrates the problem, it dedicates limited space to proposing and evaluating specific solutions for mitigating factuality degradation. They could explore and evaluate various mitigation strategies.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the design and evaluation of watermarking methods for medical text generation. It may lead to the development of domain-aware watermarking techniques that balance detectability with the preservation of critical factual information. This would be vital for safe and responsible deployment of LLMs in medicine.

**Justification for Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7.5** to this paper.

*   The core idea – evaluating watermarking *specifically* for medical factuality – is highly relevant and timely. The introduction of FWS provides a useful metric.
*   The problem is clearly defined, and the methodology is sound, including GPT-Judger and human validation.
*   The paper offers actionable insights regarding the types of errors watermarking methods introduce and emphasizes the necessity of domain-aware methods.
*   The paper also presents an analysis of entropy shifts in medical entities due to watermarking, providing actionable insights for model training and watermarking strategy development.
*   However, it's important to note that the reliance on GPT-Judger introduces some uncertainties. Exploring more mitigation strategies would improve the paper further.

Overall, the paper makes a substantial contribution to the field by highlighting the importance of factuality in evaluating watermarking methods for medical text and provides a framework for future research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### A Survey of Long-Document Retrieval in the PLM and LLM Era
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07759v1)
- **Authors**: Minghan Li, Miyang Luo, Tianrui Lv, Yishuai Zhang, Siqi Zhao, Ercong Nie, Guodong Zhou
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of long-form documents presents a fundamental challenge to information retrieval (IR), as their length, dispersed evidence, and complex structures demand specialized methods beyond standard passage-level techniques. This survey provides the first comprehensive treatment of long-document retrieval (LDR), consolidating methods, challenges, and applications across three major eras. We systematize the evolution from classical lexical and early neural models to modern pre-trained (PLM) and large language models (LLMs), covering key paradigms like passage aggregation, hierarchical encoding, efficient attention, and the latest LLM-driven re-ranking and retrieval techniques. Beyond the models, we review domain-specific applications, specialized evaluation resources, and outline critical open challenges such as efficiency trade-offs, multimodal alignment, and faithfulness. This survey aims to provide both a consolidated reference and a forward-looking agenda for advancing long-document retrieval in the era of foundation models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the survey paper on Long-Document Retrieval (LDR):

**Summary:**

This survey provides a comprehensive overview of the field of long-document retrieval (LDR), covering the evolution of techniques from classical lexical methods and early neural models to modern pre-trained language models (PLMs) and large language models (LLMs).  It categorizes methods into key paradigms: passage aggregation, hierarchical encoding, efficient attention, and LLM-driven re-ranking and retrieval. The paper also reviews domain-specific applications (law, biomedicine, web search), specialized evaluation resources, and outlines open challenges such as efficiency trade-offs, multimodal alignment, and faithfulness.  The authors aim to provide both a consolidated reference and a forward-looking agenda for researchers working on LDR.

**Critical Evaluation:**

This survey is a valuable contribution to the field of information retrieval, particularly concerning the specialized area of long-document retrieval. Here's a detailed breakdown of its strengths and weaknesses:

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensiveness:** The survey thoroughly covers a wide range of approaches to LDR, spanning different eras and model types. It's well-organized and provides a structured taxonomy that helps readers understand the evolution of the field.
*   **Clear Categorization:**  The paper's organization around key paradigms (Holistic, Divide-and-Conquer, Indexing-Structure-Oriented, and Long-Query Retrieval) is helpful for understanding the different strategies used to tackle the challenges of LDR.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The inclusion of domain-specific applications provides context and motivates the need for specialized LDR techniques. Reviewing existing datasets and evaluation methods assists researchers and practitioners. Highlighting efficiency techniques has clear practical application.
*   **Forward-Looking Perspective:**  The authors identify critical open challenges and outline promising directions for future research. This helps to guide future research efforts and promote innovation.
*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The survey provides a clear and well-articulated definition of the long-document retrieval problem, including its unique challenges and differences from standard ad-hoc retrieval.
*   **LLM Era Focus:** The detailed discussion of LLMs as re-rankers and retrievers, including their strengths and limitations in the context of LDR, is timely and valuable given the rapid advancements in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Depth vs. Breadth:**  While the survey covers a lot of ground, it might lack deep dives into the technical details of specific models or algorithms.  A reader might need to consult the original papers for more in-depth understanding. While it is challenging to provide such details in a survey format, deeper discussions of algorithmic complexity and scaling behaviors would provide further utility.
*   **Evaluation Metric Criticism:** The survey is critical of existing evaluation metrics, however further articulation of what specifically new evaluation methods could take into consideration could be of benefit.
*   **Multimodal Coverage:** Although the survey mentions multimodal aspects of long documents, this area could be expanded upon. A more detailed discussion of techniques for handling images, tables, and other non-textual elements would be beneficial.
*   **LLM Hallucinations:** While hallucination is mentioned as a problem with LLMs in LDR, the survey could go deeper into techniques and existing literature on mitigating or detecting these issues.
*   **Index Structure Detail:** The discussion of indexing structures is high-level. A more detailed analysis of specific indexing techniques and their performance trade-offs would strengthen the survey.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The survey's novelty lies in its comprehensive and structured treatment of the *specific* problem of long-document retrieval, distinguishing it from broader surveys on information retrieval or LLMs. It consolidates a scattered body of research and provides a clear roadmap for navigating the field. The significance is high as it addresses a fundamental challenge in information access and provides valuable guidance for researchers and practitioners in a rapidly evolving area. It provides a timely synthesis that helps structure existing knowledge and guide future development.

**Score: 8.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper is an excellent, timely, and well-structured survey that fills a gap in the literature by focusing specifically on long-document retrieval. It provides a valuable overview of the field, categorizes existing approaches effectively, and identifies important open challenges. While it could benefit from more in-depth technical details and expanded coverage of certain areas (e.g., multimodal aspects, hallucination mitigation), its comprehensiveness, clear organization, and forward-looking perspective make it a significant contribution. The survey's high practical relevance and potential to guide future research justify the high score.

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 8/10

### What Were You Thinking? An LLM-Driven Large-Scale Study of Refactoring Motivations in Open-Source Projects
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07763v1)
- **Authors**: Mikel Robredo, Matteo Esposito, Fabio Palomba, Rafael Peñaloza, Valentina Lenarduzzi
- **Abstract**: Context. Code refactoring improves software quality without changing external behavior. Despite its advantages, its benefits are hindered by the considerable cost of time, resources, and continuous effort it demands. Aim. Understanding why developers refactor, and which metrics capture these motivations, may support wider and more effective use of refactoring in practice. Method. We performed a large-scale empirical study to analyze developers refactoring activity, leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to identify underlying motivations from version control data, comparing our findings with previous motivations reported in the literature. Results. LLMs matched human judgment in 80% of cases, but aligned with literature-based motivations in only 47%. They enriched 22% of motivations with more detailed rationale, often highlighting readability, clarity, and structural improvements. Most motivations were pragmatic, focused on simplification and maintainability. While metrics related to developer experience and code readability ranked highest, their correlation with motivation categories was weak. Conclusions. We conclude that LLMs effectively capture surface-level motivations but struggle with architectural reasoning. Their value lies in providing localized explanations, which, when combined with software metrics, can form hybrid approaches. Such integration offers a promising path toward prioritizing refactoring more systematically and balancing short-term improvements with long-term architectural goals.
- **Summary**: This paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to understand developers' motivations for refactoring code in open-source Java projects. The study analyzes a large dataset of refactoring activities, using LLMs to identify motivations expressed in version control data and comparing these findings with motivations reported in the existing software engineering literature. The authors also investigate the correlation between software metrics and refactoring motivations. The results suggest that LLMs can effectively capture surface-level motivations, but struggle with architectural reasoning, highlighting the potential of hybrid systems that combine LLM reasoning with contextual project data.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a novel approach to studying refactoring motivations by leveraging LLMs on a large scale. The use of LLMs to extract and categorize motivations from commit messages and code diffs is a relatively new application in software engineering research, contrasting with more traditional methods based on keyword searches or static analysis tools.

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-Scale Empirical Study:** The study analyzes a substantial dataset of Java projects, providing a broader perspective than smaller-scale or survey-based studies.
*   **LLM-Driven Approach:** The application of LLMs for motivation extraction is a significant methodological contribution.
*   **Comparison with Prior Work:** The comparison of LLM-generated motivations with those reported in previous literature provides valuable insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in this context.
*   **Identification of Hybrid Approach:** The conclusion that hybrid systems, combining LLM reasoning with contextual project data, offer a promising path for improving refactoring prioritization is a key takeaway.
*   **Taxonomy of Motivations**: The detailed taxonomy of refactoring motivations provides insight into real-world drivers of refactoring activity.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Architectural Understanding by LLMs:** The study acknowledges that LLMs struggle with architectural reasoning, which limits their ability to understand refactoring motivations related to long-term design goals. This suggests a reliance on surface-level code cues.
*   **Weak Correlation Between Metrics and Motivations:** The weak correlation between software metrics and motivation categories suggests that existing metrics may not be effective in capturing the underlying reasons for refactoring. This necessitates more sophisticated or alternative metrics.
*   **Context Dependence:** The reliance on local code context limits the understanding of broader project-level influences. The paper highlights this limitation and points towards the need to embed more project-wide context into the LLM input.
*   **Java-Specific:** The focus on Java projects limits the generalizability of the findings to other programming languages.
*   **RMT Detection Accuracy**: Though RMT is near 100% accurate, some limitations will occur.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   It highlights the potential of LLMs for automating tasks in software engineering, such as motivation extraction and code understanding.
*   It identifies the limitations of LLMs in capturing architectural reasoning, motivating the development of hybrid systems that combine LLM reasoning with other sources of knowledge.
*   It provides a refined catalogue of refactoring motivations and insights into the factors that drive developers to refactor code.
*   It opens avenues for further research into the development of more effective software metrics for capturing refactoring motivations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by applying LLMs to the problem of understanding refactoring motivations and empirically evaluating their capabilities and limitations. The scale of the study and the comparison with prior work strengthens the findings. While the limitations related to architectural understanding and weak metric correlations are acknowledged, they also point toward directions for future research, indicating its potential for meaningful influence. While the novelty of using LLMs is noteworthy, similar applications are emerging, which limits the paper's individual pioneering impact. The specific study on Java also somewhat limits its general applicability. The meticulous experimental setup and relatively sound methodology also contribute to the reliability of its findings and its usefulness in the software engineering community.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### AgentSentinel: An End-to-End and Real-Time Security Defense Framework for Computer-Use Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07764v1)
- **Authors**: Haitao Hu, Peng Chen, Yanpeng Zhao, Yuqi Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have been increasingly integrated into computer-use agents, which can autonomously operate tools on a user's computer to accomplish complex tasks. However, due to the inherently unstable and unpredictable nature of LLM outputs, they may issue unintended tool commands or incorrect inputs, leading to potentially harmful operations. Unlike traditional security risks stemming from insecure user prompts, tool execution results from LLM-driven decisions introduce new and unique security challenges. These vulnerabilities span across all components of a computer-use agent. To mitigate these risks, we propose AgentSentinel, an end-to-end, real-time defense framework designed to mitigate potential security threats on a user's computer. AgentSentinel intercepts all sensitive operations within agent-related services and halts execution until a comprehensive security audit is completed. Our security auditing mechanism introduces a novel inspection process that correlates the current task context with system traces generated during task execution. To thoroughly evaluate AgentSentinel, we present BadComputerUse, a benchmark consisting of 60 diverse attack scenarios across six attack categories. The benchmark demonstrates a 87% average attack success rate on four state-of-the-art LLMs. Our evaluation shows that AgentSentinel achieves an average defense success rate of 79.6%, significantly outperforming all baseline defenses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "AgentSentinel" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AgentSentinel, a real-time security framework designed to protect computer systems from potential threats arising from the use of Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents that can autonomously operate tools. AgentSentinel intercepts sensitive operations, performs security audits correlating task context with system traces, and halts execution if necessary. The paper also introduces BadComputerUse, a benchmark of diverse attack scenarios to evaluate the framework.  Evaluation demonstrates AgentSentinel's effectiveness in detecting and preventing attacks compared to baseline defense methods, achieving a 79.6% defense success rate.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its end-to-end, real-time security framework specifically designed for computer-use agents. While individual components, like system tracing and security audits, are not entirely new, their integration into a cohesive system tailored for the unique security challenges posed by LLM-driven agents is significant. The BadComputerUse benchmark is also a valuable contribution, providing a standardized way to assess security vulnerabilities and defenses in this emerging domain. It is worth noting that tool use detection techniques have existed, however the paper's combination to prevent novel threats is the differentiator here.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical security concern in the rapidly evolving field of LLM-based agents. As these agents gain more control over computer systems, the potential for misuse (intentional or unintentional) increases dramatically. AgentSentinel offers a practical approach to mitigate these risks.  The paper's findings are relevant to researchers and practitioners working on LLM security, agent design, and system security. It highlights the limitations of existing security measures and motivates the development of more specialized defense mechanisms. The framework's emphasis on passively tracing each tool invocation is a major strength which avoids interfering with proper agent execution.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** AgentSentinel provides a holistic approach to security, addressing vulnerabilities across various components of computer-use agents.
    *   **Real-Time Defense:** The framework's real-time interception and auditing capabilities enable timely threat mitigation.
    *   **Context-Awareness:** Integrating task context with system traces improves the accuracy and effectiveness of security audits.
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation using the BadComputerUse benchmark, demonstrating AgentSentinel's superior performance compared to baseline defenses.
    *   **Pragmatic Design:** The modular architecture of AgentSentinel and its client-server design principles suggest it's possible to integrate into existing agent systems with minimal modification.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependence on LLM Performance:** The effectiveness of AgentSentinel's LLM-based auditor is dependent on the underlying LLM's reasoning capabilities.  If the LLM has blind spots or biases, the auditor may miss certain threats.
    *   **Potential for False Positives/Negatives:** While AgentSentinel outperforms baselines, it still exhibits non-zero false positive and false negative rates.  Further research is needed to minimize these errors. A false-positive here is detrimental to the agent and user, whereas a false-negative can compromise their safety.
    *   **Overhead:** Even with optimizations like query caching, real-time security audits inevitably introduce some performance overhead. The paper doesn't fully quantify the impact on agent responsiveness, although it's addressed partially.
    *   **Limited Scope of Trace Probes:** The paper admits that the selection of 16 trace probes may not be exhaustive. Expanding trace coverage could improve threat detection but also increase overhead.

*   **Future Directions:** The paper opens up several avenues for future research:
    *   Improving the robustness and accuracy of the LLM-based auditor, e.g., by incorporating ensemble methods or adversarial training.
    *   Developing more sophisticated methods for analyzing system traces to detect subtle or complex threats.
    *   Exploring adaptive security policies that dynamically adjust the level of protection based on the risk profile of the task and the agent's behavior.
    *   Quantifying the performance overhead of AgentSentinel more precisely and developing techniques to minimize it.
    *   Exploring integration with other security mechanisms, such as sandboxing or privilege separation.

*   **Justification:** Given the novelty of the approach (real-time defense in computer-use agents), the critical importance of the problem, and the comprehensive evaluation, the paper represents a notable contribution to the field. Although limitations exist (primarily concerning the potential for false alarms, overhead, and dependence on base LLMs), it provides a solid foundation for future research and development. The modular design of AgentSentinel allows for a pragmatic, composable approach, making it suitable for integration into existing systems.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are LLMs Enough for Hyperpartisan, Fake, Polarized and Harmful Content Detection? Evaluating In-Context Learning vs. Fine-Tuning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07768v1)
- **Authors**: Michele Joshua Maggini, Dhia Merzougui, Rabiraj Bandyopadhyay, Gaël Dias, Fabrice Maurel, Pablo Gamallo
- **Abstract**: The spread of fake news, polarizing, politically biased, and harmful content on online platforms has been a serious concern. With large language models becoming a promising approach, however, no study has properly benchmarked their performance across different models, usage methods, and languages. This study presents a comprehensive overview of different Large Language Models adaptation paradigms for the detection of hyperpartisan and fake news, harmful tweets, and political bias. Our experiments spanned 10 datasets and 5 different languages (English, Spanish, Portuguese, Arabic and Bulgarian), covering both binary and multiclass classification scenarios. We tested different strategies ranging from parameter efficient Fine-Tuning of language models to a variety of different In-Context Learning strategies and prompts. These included zero-shot prompts, codebooks, few-shot (with both randomly-selected and diversely-selected examples using Determinantal Point Processes), and Chain-of-Thought. We discovered that In-Context Learning often underperforms when compared to Fine-Tuning a model. This main finding highlights the importance of Fine-Tuning even smaller models on task-specific settings even when compared to the largest models evaluated in an In-Context Learning setup - in our case LlaMA3.1-8b-Instruct, Mistral-Nemo-Instruct-2407 and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Are LLMs Enough for Hyperpartisan, Fake, Polarized and Harmful Content Detection? Evaluating In-Context Learning vs. Fine-Tuning" investigates the effectiveness of Large Language Models (LLMs) in detecting hyperpartisan, fake, polarized, and harmful content. It comprehensively compares fine-tuning (FT) and in-context learning (ICL) approaches across multiple models, datasets, and languages. The study examines different ICL strategies (zero-shot, few-shot with various selection methods, codebooks, and chain-of-thought) and finds that FT generally outperforms ICL, even with the largest models. The results highlight the importance of task-specific fine-tuning, even for smaller models. The paper also analyzes the performance variations across different model architectures (encoder-only vs. decoder-only) and languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a strong contribution by providing a systematic and extensive benchmark of LLMs for detecting different types of problematic content. While individual studies have explored LLMs for specific tasks like fake news detection, this work stands out due to its breadth and multilingual scope. The comparison of FT and various ICL techniques across different model architectures and languages is a novel and valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The finding that FT often outperforms ICL, even with larger models, is significant. It challenges the assumption that the sheer scale of LLMs obviates the need for fine-tuning. This result has practical implications for researchers and practitioners who need to detect problematic content, suggesting that targeted fine-tuning on smaller, task-specific datasets remains a relevant strategy. The paper's focus on multilingual content detection is also important, as most prior studies have been limited to English or U.S.-centric data.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study considers a wide range of models (Llama3, Mistral, Qwen, BERT variants), adaptation techniques (FT, ICL with various prompting strategies), datasets (10 datasets), and languages (5 languages).
    *   **Multilingual Focus:** The inclusion of non-English datasets is crucial for assessing the generalizability of LLMs for content detection in diverse linguistic contexts.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The experiments appear well-designed and executed, with clear descriptions of the methods and hyperparameter settings. The use of techniques like DPP for few-shot example selection adds rigor to the evaluation.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The findings provide practical guidance on selecting the appropriate adaptation strategy for different content detection tasks and languages.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Model Selection:** While the range of models is good, there could be more exploration with other model architectures, perhaps more recent models that were not available when the experiments were run. The budget limitation as mentioned in the limitation section can also be considered.
    *   **Overemphasis on ICL Prompt Engineering:** Some of the ICL approaches, especially around chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting, might benefit from a more in-depth analysis of why they don't work well. Is it simply a matter of prompt engineering, or are there fundamental limitations of CoT in these specific tasks?
    *   **Dataset Limitations:** Some of the datasets are based on older data, which might not fully reflect the current landscape of online misinformation.
    *   **Generalizations:** It may be challenging to generalize results across vastly different cultural/regional political environments; additional care in contextualizing multilingual results is warranted.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by highlighting the importance of fine-tuning and providing a valuable benchmark for future research on LLMs for content detection. The findings can inform the development of more effective and robust content moderation systems.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper addresses important ethical considerations, acknowledging the potential for misuse of the developed models and emphasizing responsible research practices.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's strengths clearly outweigh its weaknesses. It provides a comprehensive and rigorous evaluation of LLMs for content detection, yielding valuable insights that challenge conventional wisdom and offer practical guidance. While there are some limitations in the model selection and exploration of CoT, the paper's contributions are substantial and warrant a high score. It fills a gap in the literature by offering a comprehensive benchmarking across different ICL strategies, models, and multilingual contexts. The experiments are extensive, well-documented, and offer actionable takeaways for practitioners and researchers alike.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Query Expansion in the Age of Pre-trained and Large Language Models: A Comprehensive Survey
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07794v1)
- **Authors**: Minghan Li, Xinxuan Lv, Junjie Zou, Tongna Chen, Chao Zhang, Suchao An, Ercong Nie, Guodong Zhou
- **Abstract**: Modern information retrieval (IR) must bridge short, ambiguous queries and ever more diverse, rapidly evolving corpora. Query Expansion (QE) remains a key mechanism for mitigating vocabulary mismatch, but the design space has shifted markedly with pre-trained language models (PLMs) and large language models (LLMs). This survey synthesizes the field from three angles: (i) a four-dimensional framework of query expansion - from the point of injection (explicit vs. implicit QE), through grounding and interaction (knowledge bases, model-internal capabilities, multi-turn retrieval) and learning alignment, to knowledge graph-based argumentation; (ii) a model-centric taxonomy spanning encoder-only, encoder-decoder, decoder-only, instruction-tuned, and domain/multilingual variants, highlighting their characteristic affordances for QE (contextual disambiguation, controllable generation, zero-/few-shot reasoning); and (iii) practice-oriented guidance on where and how neural QE helps in first-stage retrieval, multi-query fusion, re-ranking, and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG). We compare traditional query expansion with PLM/LLM-based methods across seven key aspects, and we map applications across web search, biomedicine, e-commerce, open-domain QA/RAG, conversational and code search, and cross-lingual settings. The review distills design grounding and interaction, alignment/distillation (SFT/PEFT/DPO), and KG constraints - as robust remedies to topic drift and hallucination. We conclude with an agenda on quality control, cost-aware invocation, domain/temporal adaptation, evaluation beyond end-task metrics, and fairness/privacy. Collectively, these insights provide a principled blueprint for selecting and combining QE techniques under real-world constraints.
- **Summary**: This paper provides a comprehensive survey of query expansion (QE) techniques in the age of pre-trained language models (PLMs) and large language models (LLMs). It synthesizes the field by proposing a four-dimensional framework for query expansion: point of injection, grounding and interaction, learning and alignment, and KG-augmented. It categorizes PLMs/LLMs relevant to QE and articulates their characteristic affordances, as well as practical guidance on where and how neural QE helps in retrieval-related tasks. The survey maps applications across various domains and distills design grounding and interaction, alignment/distillation, and KG constraints as robust remedies to topic drift and hallucination. Finally, it outlines an agenda for future work including quality control, cost-aware invocation, domain/temporal adaptation, and fairness/privacy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual techniques discussed are not novel as they are drawn from existing research, the paper's novelty lies in its comprehensive synthesis and structured organization of the field. The proposed four-dimensional framework offers a useful lens through which to analyze and categorize different QE techniques in the PLM/LLM era. This framework isn't necessarily groundbreaking, but provides a beneficial way to understand the design space. The paper also synthesizes research on traditional QE and compares them to PLM/LLM-based techniques. However, a weakness is that much of the "survey" involves simply listing existing papers and categorizing them according to the framework. There's not a ton of in-depth critical analysis beyond this categorization.

*   **Significance:** The paper is highly significant for the information retrieval community. By providing a structured overview of the rapidly evolving landscape of QE techniques with PLMs/LLMs, it helps researchers and practitioners to navigate the complex design space and identify relevant methods for their specific needs. It also identifies key challenges and future directions that can guide future research efforts. The clear articulation of architectural affordances by model family is a valuable resource. The practical guidance tying methods to deployment concerns is also a very useful contribution. The discussion of open challenges is also useful, setting the stage for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Comprehensive coverage of the field
    *   Well-defined framework for analyzing QE techniques
    *   Clear articulation of architectural affordances of different model families
    *   Practical guidance on deployment concerns
    *   Identification of key challenges and future directions
    *   Rigorous comparison between classical and PLM/LLM-based QE

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited in-depth critical analysis beyond categorization of papers.
    *   The four-dimensional framework, while useful, is not groundbreaking.
    *   Focus is heavily on summarizing existing work, with less emphasis on identifying genuinely new, previously unseen patterns.
    *   Could benefit from more rigorous and detailed evaluation metrics beyond simple success metrics to determine where to apply the techniques.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to become a key reference for researchers and practitioners working on query expansion in the era of PLMs/LLMs. It provides a valuable foundation for future research and can help to accelerate the development of more effective and efficient QE techniques. However, its actual impact will be determined by how actively the framework is adopted and applied in subsequent work, and how well it continues to be maintained and updated with the rapid advancement of the field.

**Rationale:**

Given the rapid pace of development in the field of PLMs/LLMs, synthesizing the landscape of QE is essential. However, the paper's contribution is primarily in the organization and summarization of existing work, rather than in groundbreaking novel insights. The framework and guidance are practical and helpful, enhancing its value. Nevertheless, limitations such as the lack of in-depth analysis and reliance on known concepts lead to a moderately high rating.

### Dual Knowledge-Enhanced Two-Stage Reasoner for Multimodal Dialog Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07817v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaolin Chen, Xuemeng Song, Haokun Wen, Weili Guan, Xiangyu Zhao, Liqiang Nie
- **Abstract**: Textual response generation is pivotal for multimodal \mbox{task-oriented} dialog systems, which aims to generate proper textual responses based on the multimodal context. While existing efforts have demonstrated remarkable progress, there still exist the following limitations: 1) \textit{neglect of unstructured review knowledge} and 2) \textit{underutilization of large language models (LLMs)}. Inspired by this, we aim to fully utilize dual knowledge (\textit{i.e., } structured attribute and unstructured review knowledge) with LLMs to promote textual response generation in multimodal task-oriented dialog systems. However, this task is non-trivial due to two key challenges: 1) \textit{dynamic knowledge type selection} and 2) \textit{intention-response decoupling}. To address these challenges, we propose a novel dual knowledge-enhanced two-stage reasoner by adapting LLMs for multimodal dialog systems (named DK2R). To be specific, DK2R first extracts both structured attribute and unstructured review knowledge from external knowledge base given the dialog context. Thereafter, DK2R uses an LLM to evaluate each knowledge type's utility by analyzing LLM-generated provisional probe responses. Moreover, DK2R separately summarizes the intention-oriented key clues via dedicated reasoning, which are further used as auxiliary signals to enhance LLM-based textual response generation. Extensive experiments conducted on a public dataset verify the superiority of DK2R. We have released the codes and parameters.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DK2R, a Dual Knowledge-Enhanced Two-Stage Reasoner for multimodal task-oriented dialogue systems. It addresses limitations in existing systems by: (1) incorporating unstructured review knowledge (user reviews) in addition to structured attribute knowledge, and (2) leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) more effectively. DK2R employs a novel probe-driven knowledge type filtering mechanism to dynamically select relevant knowledge types and decouples intention reasoning from response generation through a two-stage process. The model first extracts both structured attribute and unstructured review knowledge from external knowledge bases, then uses an LLM to evaluate the utility of each knowledge type by analyzing LLM-generated provisional probe responses. Finally, it summarizes intention-oriented key clues via dedicated reasoning, which are further used as auxiliary signals to enhance LLM-based textual response generation. Extensive experiments on the MMConv dataset demonstrate the superiority of DK2R over existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the integration of *unstructured review knowledge* alongside structured knowledge within a multimodal dialog system and the probe-driven knowledge type filtering mechanism. This is a worthwhile advancement as most prior work focuses solely on structured knowledge or uses LLMs as simple knowledge integration black boxes. The two-stage reasoning approach to decoupling intention and response is also a novel contribution, although the benefits might be incremental.

*   **Significance:**  The significance is moderate. Task-oriented dialog systems are a mature field, and many papers incrementally improve upon existing architectures.  Demonstrating that unstructured review knowledge can boost performance is valuable, as is a method to automatically select the most useful knowledge type for a given context. The experiments show clear improvements over a range of strong baselines, bolstering the paper's claim.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method.
    *   The experimental setup is thorough and comprehensive, with comparisons against numerous baselines and ablation studies.
    *   The inclusion of both quantitative and qualitative analysis provides a strong validation of the method's effectiveness.
    *   The ablation studies provide useful insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   The authors have released their code and parameters, promoting reproducibility and further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The gains, while statistically significant, might be considered incremental relative to the complexity of the approach. A simplified baseline that directly concatenates all available knowledge with LLM, then tunes the LLM with LoRA is a worthwhile comparison point.
    *   The reliance on a captioning model (BLIP) to transform visual inputs into textual representations, although common, introduces a potential bottleneck and might limit the system's ability to fully exploit visual information. Using raw visual features directly may improve the system further.
    *   The choice of LLama3 for the backbone is a decent one, however, there might be benefit in having other more competitive LLMs tested to see what gains and robustness there may be.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in multimodal dialog systems by highlighting the importance of incorporating diverse knowledge sources and using LLMs in a more principled and explainable way. The probe-driven knowledge filtering approach could be adapted for other knowledge integration tasks. Releasing the code and parameters will likely encourage others to build upon their work.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **7**.  While the paper introduces valuable novelties in knowledge integration and decoupling reasoning within multimodal dialog, and its experiments demonstrate improvement, these improvements are likely incremental rather than revolutionary. Furthermore, limitations exist within the LLM backbones used. The core contributions are significant enough to warrant attention and encourage further research.

### LLMs in Wikipedia: Investigating How LLMs Impact Participation in Knowledge Communities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07819v1)
- **Authors**: Moyan Zhou, Soobin Cho, Loren Terveen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are reshaping knowledge production as community members increasingly incorporate them into their contribution workflows. However, participating in knowledge communities involves more than just contributing content - it is also a deeply social process. While communities must carefully consider appropriate and responsible LLM integration, the absence of concrete norms has left individual editors to experiment and navigate LLM use on their own. Understanding how LLMs influence community participation is therefore critical in shaping future norms and supporting effective adoption. To address this gap, we investigated Wikipedia, one of the largest knowledge production communities, to understand 1) how LLMs influence the ways editors contribute content, 2) what strategies editors leverage to align LLM outputs with community norms, and 3) how other editors in the community respond to LLM-assisted contributions. Through interviews with 16 Wikipedia editors who had used LLMs for their edits, we found that 1) LLMs affected the content contributions for experienced and new editors differently; 2) aligning LLM outputs with community norms required tacit knowledge that often challenged newcomers; and 3) as a result, other editors responded to LLM-assisted edits differently depending on the editors' expertise level. Based on these findings, we challenge existing models of newcomer involvement and propose design implications for LLMs that support community engagement through scaffolding, teaching, and context awareness.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, followed by a justified novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper "LLMs in Wikipedia: Investigating How LLMs Impact Participation in Knowledge Communities" explores the effects of large language models (LLMs) on editor participation in Wikipedia. Through interviews with 16 Wikipedia editors who use LLMs, the authors investigate how LLMs influence content contribution, strategies for aligning LLM outputs with community norms, and how other editors respond to LLM-assisted contributions. The study finds a participation divide based on expertise: experienced editors leverage LLMs to expand their contributions and improve content quality, while newcomers often rely on LLMs to fill knowledge gaps but struggle with aligning content to community norms, leading to negative feedback. The authors argue that LLMs disrupt the traditional learning pathways for newcomers, shifting focus from peripheral tasks to editorial judgment and exacerbating a participation gap. They propose design implications for LLMs to scaffold learning, teach community norms, and personalize assistance based on user expertise.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Relevant and Timely Topic:** The study addresses a very current and important topic: the impact of AI, specifically LLMs, on collaborative knowledge production. Given the rapid adoption of LLMs, understanding their effects on communities like Wikipedia is crucial.
*   **Solid Methodology:** The qualitative approach, using semi-structured interviews, allows for in-depth exploration of editor experiences and perceptions. The use of thematic analysis provides a systematic way to analyze the interview data.
*   **Clear Findings:** The paper identifies a clear expertise-based participation divide, highlighting how LLMs affect experienced editors and newcomers differently. The identification of specific strategies editors use to align LLM output with community norms is also valuable.
*   **Practical Design Implications:** The proposed design implications are well-reasoned and grounded in the empirical findings. The suggestions to scaffold learning, teach community norms, and personalize assistance are relevant and actionable.
*   **Connection to Existing Literature:** The paper effectively situates its findings within the context of prior work on human-AI collaboration, communities of practice, and the digital divide.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The sample size of 16 participants, while typical for qualitative research, limits the generalizability of the findings to the broader Wikipedia editor population. The authors acknowledge that it's unclear how prevalent the observed patterns are.
*   **Potential Sampling Bias:** The recruitment methods (posting on WikiMedia pages, searching for LLM users) might have introduced bias towards editors who are more open to using LLMs or more active in the community. The absence of perspectives from editors who explicitly avoid LLMs is a limitation.
*   **Limited Scope of Norms:** The paper focuses mainly on content-related norms. It could benefit from exploring the impact on other aspects of community engagement, such as interpersonal interactions or power dynamics.
*   **Lack of Quantitative Data:**  The paper makes strong claims about participation divides.  Although the qualitative data supports these claims, presenting even basic summary statistics of the interview participants' experiences or perceptions would strengthen the conclusions.  How many newcomers received negative feedback, compared to experienced editors?  How often did editors reject LLM-generated text outright versus modifying it extensively?
*   **Limited focus on AI risks and biases** The study focuses more on the shift in workload rather than highlighting potential biases that these LLMs can perpetuate through knowledge creation.

*Novelty and Significance:*

The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on the impact of LLMs on editor *participation* (not just content generation) within a large knowledge community like Wikipedia. While other work has examined AI-assisted writing or editing, this study specifically addresses the social dynamics and learning processes within a community context. The identification of the participation paradox and the disruption of traditional learning pathways are significant contributions. The design implications provide a concrete path forward for developing LLMs that better support community engagement.

However, some aspects are incremental.  Human-AI collaboration has been researched extensively, and while applying it to Wikipedia with LLMs is new, the overall patterns of expertise-based adoption and the need for scaffolding/training aren't entirely surprising.

Overall, the paper makes a valuable contribution by providing a nuanced understanding of how LLMs are reshaping knowledge production in Wikipedia and by identifying key challenges and opportunities for future development.

**Score: 7.5**

*Justification:*

A score of 7.5 reflects the balance between the paper's strengths and weaknesses. The topic is highly relevant, the methodology is sound, and the findings are clearly presented and supported by evidence.  The implications are well-articulated and practically useful. However, the limited generalizability of the findings, potential sampling bias, and lack of quantitative evidence slightly reduce the overall impact.

The paper is more than simply an incremental advancement, but it also doesn't completely revolutionize understanding of the topic. The insights regarding expertise-based participation and the disruption of learning pathways are meaningful and add substantial insight to previous work.

The practical design implications are likely to influence future research and development in the field of AI-assisted knowledge production.  Because of the strengths of the study, its impact on the field is expected to be high, even if generalizability is limited.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Certainty-Guided Reasoning in Large Language Models: A Dynamic Thinking Budget Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07820v1)
- **Authors**: João Paulo Nogueira, Wentao Sun, Alonso Silva, Laith Zumot
- **Abstract**: The rise of large reasoning language models (LRLMs) has unlocked new potential for solving complex tasks. These models operate with a thinking budget, that is, a predefined number of reasoning tokens used to arrive at a solution. We propose a novel approach, inspired by the generator/discriminator framework in generative adversarial networks, in which a critic model periodically probes its own reasoning to assess whether it has reached a confident conclusion. If not, reasoning continues until a target certainty threshold is met. This mechanism adaptively balances efficiency and reliability by allowing early termination when confidence is high, while encouraging further reasoning when uncertainty persists. Through experiments on the AIME2024 and AIME2025 datasets, we show that Certainty-Guided Reasoning (CGR) improves baseline accuracy while reducing token usage. Importantly, extended multi-seed evaluations over 64 runs demonstrate that CGR is stable, reducing variance across seeds and improving exam-like performance under penalty-based grading. Additionally, our token savings analysis shows that CGR can eliminate millions of tokens in aggregate, with tunable trade-offs between certainty thresholds and efficiency. Together, these findings highlight certainty as a powerful signal for reasoning sufficiency. By integrating confidence into the reasoning process, CGR makes large reasoning language models more adaptive, trustworthy, and resource efficient, paving the way for practical deployment in domains where both accuracy and computational cost matter.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Certainty-Guided Reasoning (CGR), a novel approach to dynamically manage the "thinking budget" of large reasoning language models (LRLMs). CGR employs a "critic" model to periodically assess the certainty of the LRLM's reasoning process. If the certainty exceeds a predefined threshold, the reasoning process terminates early. Otherwise, the LRLM continues to reason until the target certainty is met or the token budget is exhausted. This adaptive mechanism balances efficiency and reliability. The authors demonstrate CGR's effectiveness on math problem-solving datasets (AIME2024, AIME2025), showing improved accuracy and reduced token usage.  They also introduce a "Grade" metric that penalizes incorrect answers given with high certainty, and they perform multi-seed experiments to show the stability of the CGR approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The core idea of using a certainty measure to dynamically allocate reasoning resources is a useful and potentially impactful. The inspiration from GANs (generator/discriminator) provides a fresh perspective. The introduction of a new grading metric addresses some of the downfalls of using accuracy alone in the evaluation.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides solid empirical evidence. The experiments on AIME datasets, a challenging benchmark, strengthens the claim. The multi-seed experiments are crucial in demonstrating the robustness of CGR, mitigating concerns about the randomness inherent in LLM inference.
    *   **Practical Implications:**  The paper clearly highlights the practical benefits of CGR: reduced computational cost and adaptable performance based on different safety/efficiency priorities.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow. The pseudocode provides a clear understanding of the algorithm's implementation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Complexity of the "Critic" Model:** While the authors mention exploring different "critic" models (including a secondary model solely for analysis), the details and the impact of this critic model's design are underexplored. The performance of CGR is likely highly dependent on the effectiveness of the certainty estimation, which is essentially delegated to the critic. A deeper analysis of different critic architectures and calibration techniques would be beneficial.
    *   **Generalizability:** While the AIME datasets are challenging, they are limited to math problems.  The effectiveness of CGR in other reasoning domains (e.g., coding, commonsense reasoning) is not demonstrated and may not directly translate.
    *   **Certainty Threshold Calibration:** The paper states an emprirical analysis was done to find the correct value for the threshold, but this analysis is not described in detail. How does this translate to other datasets, tasks, and models? How do we find the right thresholds for our use case?
    *   **Lack of ablation studies:** The authors do not include ablation studies that would show the effectiveness of each individual component in the CGR system, which lowers the importance of the work.

*   **Significance:**

    The paper addresses a crucial challenge in deploying LRLMs: balancing accuracy with computational cost. CGR offers a promising direction by enabling more efficient and adaptive reasoning. The multi-seed experiments and the "Grade" metric contribute to a more thorough and realistic evaluation of reasoning systems.

    The work could influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore confidence-based mechanisms for controlling LLM behavior. However, the limitations regarding the critic model's design and the generalizability of the results need to be addressed in future work.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of LLM efficiency. The core idea is novel and well-executed, supported by strong empirical evidence. However, the lack of deeper analysis of the certainty estimation process and limited generalizability slightly diminishes the paper's potential impact.
