A better score would require more technical nuance, more comprehensive evaluations in the field, and a more thorough consideration of the legal and ethical issues.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 5/10

### Feature Space Analysis by Guided Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07936v1)
- **Authors**: Kimiaki Shirahama, Miki Yanobu, Kaduki Yamashita, Miho Ohsaki
- **Abstract**: One of the key issues in Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) is the black-box nature of their internal feature extraction process. Targeting vision-related domains, this paper focuses on analysing the feature space of a DNN by proposing a decoder that can generate images whose features are guaranteed to closely match a user-specified feature. Owing to this guarantee that is missed in past studies, our decoder allows us to evidence which of various attributes in an image are encoded into a feature by the DNN, by generating images whose features are in proximity to that feature. Our decoder is implemented as a guided diffusion model that guides the reverse image generation of a pre-trained diffusion model to minimise the Euclidean distance between the feature of a clean image estimated at each step and the user-specified feature. One practical advantage of our decoder is that it can analyse feature spaces of different DNNs with no additional training and run on a single COTS GPU. The experimental results targeting CLIP's image encoder, ResNet-50 and vision transformer demonstrate that images generated by our decoder have features remarkably similar to the user-specified ones and reveal valuable insights into these DNNs' feature spaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenge of understanding the feature space within deep neural networks (DNNs), particularly in vision-related domains. The authors propose a novel decoder that generates images whose features closely match user-specified features. This is achieved by guiding the reverse image generation process of a pre-trained diffusion model, minimizing the Euclidean distance between the feature of a generated image and the desired feature. A key advantage of their approach is that it's training-free and can analyze feature spaces of different DNNs without additional training, even running on a single COTS GPU. The paper presents experimental results on CLIP's image encoder, ResNet-50, and vision transformers, demonstrating the effectiveness of the decoder in generating images with similar features and revealing insights into these DNNs' feature spaces. They find things like CLIP encoder's low sensitivity to anatomy, limited information contained by ResNet50, and ViT's main object focus.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of a guided diffusion model with a loss function based on the Euclidean distance between image features for analyzing DNN feature spaces. While guided diffusion models and feature inversion techniques exist, the authors emphasize the guarantee that the generated images' features closely match a user-specified feature, which they claim is missed in previous work. This level of control allows for a more rigorous and interpretable analysis of the feature space. The method being training-free and usable across different architectures without further retraining is a significant practical advantage. A second notable advantage is that the method eliminates the need for specialized handling of noisy images.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from addressing the "black box" problem of DNNs. By providing a tool to visualize and understand which image attributes are encoded into specific features, the paper potentially paves the way for:

    *   Improved DNN design:  Knowing how a network represents information can inform architectural choices and training strategies.
    *   Enhanced interpretability:  The approach contributes to making DNNs more transparent and trustworthy.
    *   Adversarial example analysis:  The decoder could be used to generate images that are difficult to distinguish, aiding in the development of more robust networks.
    *   More targeted image editing and manipulation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of understanding DNN feature spaces and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Technically Sound Approach:** The guided diffusion model is well-motivated and explained, and the implementation details are provided.
    *   **Practical Advantages:** The training-free nature and low computational requirements make the decoder accessible to a wider range of researchers.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experiments cover different DNN architectures and datasets, providing evidence of the decoder's generality.
    *   **Interesting Insights:** The identified characteristics of the feature spaces (e.g., CLIP's anatomical insensitivity) are valuable and could stimulate further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** While being training-free, the image generation using diffusion models is still computationally intensive, limiting the scale of analysis that can be performed. This is discussed in the paper, but it remains a practical limitation. The analysis requires several steps and can take hours.
    *   **Subjectivity in Interpretation:** Interpreting the visual cues from generated images is somewhat subjective. While the authors attempt to ground their interpretations in the properties of the DNNs, there's still room for bias.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** The paper focuses more on the practical application of the decoder than on a deep theoretical analysis of the feature space itself. A stronger theoretical foundation could enhance the paper's long-term impact.
    *   **Limited Novelty Compared to Prior Work:** It is not necessarily *fundamentally new* but more of an improvement/adaptation of existing techniques. It builds on existing work related to guided diffusion and feature inversion rather than creating entirely new paradigms.

* **Score:** 7

**Justification:** The paper presents a valuable tool for analyzing DNN feature spaces. The novelty lies in the specific combination of existing techniques, the guarantee of feature matching, and the training-free aspect. The significance is substantial, as it addresses a key challenge in deep learning interpretability. The paper is well-written and experimentally supported. However, the computational cost, the subjectivity in interpretation, and the limited theoretical analysis slightly temper the overall score. A higher score would require a more substantial theoretical contribution or a more disruptive shift in the methodology for feature space analysis. The score of 7 acknowledges the practical value and the contribution to the field while also recognizing its incremental nature and the potential for improvement.


- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Guided Reasoning in LLM-Driven Penetration Testing Using Structured Attack Trees
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07939v1)
- **Authors**: Katsuaki Nakano, Reza Feyyazi, Shanchieh Jay Yang, Michael Zuzak
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have driven interest in automating cybersecurity penetration testing workflows, offering the promise of faster and more consistent vulnerability assessment for enterprise systems. Existing LLM agents for penetration testing primarily rely on self-guided reasoning, which can produce inaccurate or hallucinated procedural steps. As a result, the LLM agent may undertake unproductive actions, such as exploiting unused software libraries or generating cyclical responses that repeat prior tactics. In this work, we propose a guided reasoning pipeline for penetration testing LLM agents that incorporates a deterministic task tree built from the MITRE ATT&CK Matrix, a proven penetration testing kll chain, to constrain the LLM's reaoning process to explicitly defined tactics, techniques, and procedures. This anchors reasoning in proven penetration testing methodologies and filters out ineffective actions by guiding the agent towards more productive attack procedures. To evaluate our approach, we built an automated penetration testing LLM agent using three LLMs (Llama-3-8B, Gemini-1.5, and GPT-4) and applied it to navigate 10 HackTheBox cybersecurity exercises with 103 discrete subtasks representing real-world cyberattack scenarios. Our proposed reasoning pipeline guided the LLM agent through 71.8\%, 72.8\%, and 78.6\% of subtasks using Llama-3-8B, Gemini-1.5, and GPT-4, respectively. Comparatively, the state-of-the-art LLM penetration testing tool using self-guided reasoning completed only 13.5\%, 16.5\%, and 75.7\% of subtasks and required 86.2\%, 118.7\%, and 205.9\% more model queries. This suggests that incorporating a deterministic task tree into LLM reasoning pipelines can enhance the accuracy and efficiency of automated cybersecurity assessments
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a guided reasoning pipeline for LLM agents in cybersecurity penetration testing.  It improves upon existing self-guided LLM approaches by incorporating a deterministic task tree (STT) built from the MITRE ATT&CK Matrix. This STT constrains the LLM's reasoning to predefined tactics, techniques, and procedures, filtering out ineffective actions and grounding the agent in proven penetration testing methodologies. The authors evaluate their approach using three LLMs (Llama-3-8B, Gemini-1.5, and GPT-4) on 10 HackTheBox exercises. Results demonstrate significant improvements in subtask completion rates and efficiency compared to a self-guided LLM baseline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the explicit incorporation of a deterministic task tree derived from a well-established cybersecurity framework (MITRE ATT&CK) to guide the reasoning of an LLM agent during penetration testing. While using LLMs for penetration testing isn't entirely new, constraining the reasoning process with a predefined structure, rather than relying solely on self-guided exploration, is a significant contribution. Previous work often struggles with hallucinations and unproductive actions from the LLM, and the STT addresses this directly. However, it's important to note that the idea of using structured knowledge to guide LLM reasoning exists in other domains, so the novelty is primarily in its *application* to penetration testing and the specific implementation using the MITRE ATT&CK framework.

*   **Significance:** The results are compelling and demonstrate a practical improvement in both the accuracy and efficiency of LLM-driven penetration testing.  The fact that the STT approach significantly improves the performance of smaller models (Llama-3-8B, Gemini-1.5) is particularly significant, as it makes automated penetration testing more accessible and potentially reduces reliance on expensive, large LLMs. The reduction in model queries is also valuable, reducing API costs and improving response times.  However, the paper also identifies limitations, such as difficulty in identifying complex CVEs and the limited understanding of advanced features in exploitation tools. These limitations temper the overall impact but also provide clear directions for future research. While the approach works effectively, it also brings the downside of potentially hindering the LLM to leverage its ingenuity to identify novel attacks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined approach.
    *   Rigorous experimental evaluation with a relevant benchmark (HackTheBox).
    *   Demonstrated improvements in both accuracy and efficiency.
    *   Open-source release of code, enhancing reproducibility and adoption.
    *   Identified limitations provide clear directions for future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The dependence on MITRE ATT&CK inherently limits the agent's ability to discover techniques *outside* of that framework. This is a trade-off between control and exploration.
    *   The identified limitations regarding complex CVEs and advanced tool usage require further investigation to address.
    *   The study could benefit from an analysis on the reasoning paths, showing how different tactics and techniques within the STT are actually used.
    *   It may be better to use a smaller tree with broader tactics and techniques to still allow the LLM to explore new vulnerabilities while still guiding it with the framework.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more practical and reliable LLM-based tools for cybersecurity assessment. The STT approach could be adopted by other researchers and practitioners to improve the performance of LLM agents in other complex, structured tasks. Also, it offers a generalizable design pattern of combining the strength of LLMs with known frameworks.
Score: 7

**Rigorous Rationale:**

A score of 7 reflects a well-executed paper that presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of LLM-driven cybersecurity.  The strengths lie in the clear problem definition, effective solution, strong empirical results, and open-source availability. While the approach addresses critical limitations of self-guided LLMs, the dependence on the MITRE ATT&CK framework, the identified limitations, and potential constraints on discovering novel vulnerabilities prevent a higher score. The paper demonstrates tangible improvements, but the future impact depends on addressing these limitations and exploring the trade-offs between structured guidance and open exploration in LLM reasoning. The results, and open source code, are certainly promising, but further developments will be needed to overcome these limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### ImportSnare: Directed "Code Manual" Hijacking in Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07941v1)
- **Authors**: Kai Ye, Liangcai Su, Chenxiong Qian
- **Abstract**: Code generation has emerged as a pivotal capability of Large Language Models(LLMs), revolutionizing development efficiency for programmers of all skill levels. However, the complexity of data structures and algorithmic logic often results in functional deficiencies and security vulnerabilities in generated code, reducing it to a prototype requiring extensive manual debugging. While Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) can enhance correctness and security by leveraging external code manuals, it simultaneously introduces new attack surfaces. In this paper, we pioneer the exploration of attack surfaces in Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation (RACG), focusing on malicious dependency hijacking. We demonstrate how poisoned documentation containing hidden malicious dependencies (e.g., matplotlib_safe) can subvert RACG, exploiting dual trust chains: LLM reliance on RAG and developers' blind trust in LLM suggestions. To construct poisoned documents, we propose ImportSnare, a novel attack framework employing two synergistic strategies: 1)Position-aware beam search optimizes hidden ranking sequences to elevate poisoned documents in retrieval results, and 2)Multilingual inductive suggestions generate jailbreaking sequences to manipulate LLMs into recommending malicious dependencies. Through extensive experiments across Python, Rust, and JavaScript, ImportSnare achieves significant attack success rates (over 50% for popular libraries such as matplotlib and seaborn) in general, and is also able to succeed even when the poisoning ratio is as low as 0.01%, targeting both custom and real-world malicious packages. Our findings reveal critical supply chain risks in LLM-powered development, highlighting inadequate security alignment for code generation tasks. To support future research, we will release the multilingual benchmark suite and datasets. The project homepage is https://importsnare.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ImportSnare: Directed "Code Manual" Hijacking in Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation" investigates a new attack surface in Retrieval-Augmented Code Generation (RACG) systems. The authors demonstrate how malicious dependencies can be injected into code generation by poisoning code documentation used by RAG. This is achieved through the "ImportSnare" framework, which uses position-aware beam search to optimize the ranking of poisoned documents and multilingual inductive suggestions to manipulate LLMs into recommending these malicious dependencies. The paper shows the attack is effective across multiple programming languages (Python, Rust, JavaScript) and even with low poisoning ratios, posing a significant supply chain risk in LLM-powered development. The authors release a multilingual benchmark and datasets to support future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in identifying and exploiting a novel attack surface specific to RACG systems. While RAG poisoning and software supply chain attacks are established areas, the intersection of the two in the context of *code generation* is a relatively unexplored domain. The ImportSnare framework itself, with its two synergistic strategies (ranking sequences and inducing sequences), presents a novel approach to crafting effective poisoned documents.

*   **Significance:** The paper has substantial practical significance due to the increasing reliance on LLMs for code generation. Successfully demonstrating the hijacking of dependency recommendations highlights a serious vulnerability in the software development process and exposes a potential supply chain attack vector. The fact that the attack works with relatively low poisoning ratios underscores the severity of the threat. The release of the dataset is also useful for researchers to build defensive measures. The fact that these attacks are transferrable to closed source models means they are likely widespread and underreported.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clearly defined problem and threat model:** The paper articulates the problem clearly and establishes a reasonable threat model.
    *   **Well-designed attack framework:** The ImportSnare framework is technically sound and effectively leverages LLM vulnerabilities and weaknesses in RAG systems.
    *   **Comprehensive empirical evaluation:** The extensive experiments across multiple languages, models, and datasets provide strong evidence of the attack's effectiveness.
    *   **Practical implications:** The paper highlights the real-world risks associated with the attack, motivating the need for mitigation strategies.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited exploration of defenses:** While the paper proposes a detection-based mitigation strategy, it's preliminary and doesn't provide a robust solution.
    *   **Reliance on local proxy LLMs:** The surrogate queries and use of proxy LLMs potentially limit the generalizability of certain experiments.
    *   **Focus on specific attack types:** The paper primarily focuses on dependency hijacking. Other potential attack vectors in RACG (e.g., code injection) are not explored in depth.
    *   The lack of real-world attack cases hinders a complete evaluation of the attack’s potential effects.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research on the security of LLM-based code generation systems and RAG. It will likely spur the development of more robust defenses against RAG poisoning and supply chain attacks in this context. The finding about the ease with which closed source models can be fooled into using hijacked imports is especially troubling and useful for future investigation.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite its limitations, the paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field. The identification of a new attack surface, the design of an effective attack framework, and the comprehensive evaluation make it a valuable contribution. The results raise serious concerns about the security of LLM-assisted code generation and the potential for supply chain attacks. Further research is needed to develop effective defenses, but the paper provides a strong foundation for this work. For these reasons, I believe a score of 8 is justified.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Visual Representation Alignment for Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07979v1)
- **Authors**: Heeji Yoon, Jaewoo Jung, Junwan Kim, Hyungyu Choi, Heeseong Shin, Sangbeom Lim, Honggyu An, Chaehyun Kim, Jisang Han, Donghyun Kim, Chanho Eom, Sunghwan Hong, Seungryong Kim
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) trained with visual instruction tuning have achieved strong performance across diverse tasks, yet they remain limited in vision-centric tasks such as object counting or spatial reasoning. We attribute this gap to the prevailing text-only supervision paradigm, which provides only indirect guidance for the visual pathway and often leads MLLMs to discard fine-grained visual details during training. In this paper, we present VIsual Representation ALignment (VIRAL), a simple yet effective regularization strategy that aligns the internal visual representations of MLLMs with those of pre-trained vision foundation models (VFMs). By explicitly enforcing this alignment, VIRAL enables the model not only to retain critical visual details from the input vision encoder but also to complement additional visual knowledge from VFMs, thereby enhancing its ability to reason over complex visual inputs. Our experiments demonstrate consistent improvements across all tasks on widely adopted multimodal benchmarks. Furthermore, we conduct comprehensive ablation studies to validate the key design choices underlying our framework. We believe this simple finding opens up an important direction for the effective integration of visual information in training MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Visual Representation Alignment for Multimodal Large Language Models."

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of MLLMs losing fine-grained visual details during training due to the dominant text-only supervision paradigm. The authors propose VIsual Representation ALignment (VIRAL), a regularization strategy that aligns internal visual representations of MLLMs with those of pre-trained Vision Foundation Models (VFMs). This approach encourages MLLMs to retain and integrate detailed visual knowledge, improving their ability to reason over complex visual inputs. The authors demonstrate consistent improvements across various multimodal benchmarks, supported by ablation studies that validate their design choices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of aligning MLLM's internal visual representations with VFMs is incrementally novel. While previous work has explored stronger visual encoders or more expressive projectors, VIRAL takes a different approach by directly supervising the visual pathway. It's more sophisticated than simply using a stronger encoder as it tries to preserve the information learned through the visual pathway. The novelty lies in: 1) explicitly recognizing the visual pathway as under-supervised during text-only finetuning and 2) leveraging VFMs as teachers for internal representation regularization. However, alignment approaches are commonly used in other areas of deep learning, which diminishes the originality slightly. The idea of using a pre-trained model's representations to guide the learning of another isn't entirely new.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its practical impact on MLLM performance. By addressing the visual pathway's under-supervision, VIRAL offers a simple yet effective way to boost performance on vision-centric tasks. The consistent improvements across multiple benchmarks and vision encoders demonstrate the general applicability of the approach. Importantly, it opens a new research direction for improving visual reasoning in MLLMs without relying solely on more powerful encoders or projectors.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly identifies the issue of visual information loss during MLLM training.
    *   **Simple and effective solution:** VIRAL is easy to implement and integrates seamlessly into existing training pipelines.
    *   **Extensive experimentation:** The paper provides thorough experimental results across multiple benchmarks and ablation studies to support the claims.
    *   **Detailed analysis:** The paper includes detailed analysis, such as layer-wise alignment, attention analysis, and robustness analysis, to provide insights into the effectiveness of VIRAL.
    *   The ablation studies carefully validate design choices and highlight the benefits of the alignment strategy. The finding that DINOv2 works particularly well as a teacher VFM is insightful.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Incremental novelty:** As mentioned above, the idea of representation alignment isn't entirely novel.
    *   **Limited theoretical justification:** The paper could benefit from a more formal theoretical analysis of why aligning with VFMs helps preserve visual information. While the empirical results are convincing, a more grounded theoretical framework would strengthen the contribution.
    *   The paper is somewhat narrow in scope; it focuses primarily on the alignment objective and doesn't explore other ways to improve the visual pathway (e.g., modifying the architecture).
    *   Some benchmarks (especially synthetic datasets like MMVP) might not fully represent real-world visual reasoning.
    *   The improvements might be dependent on the quality of the VFM selected, requiring potentially time-consuming hyperparameter tuning.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research by:
    *   Motivating researchers to explore alternative supervision strategies for MLLMs.
    *   Encouraging the use of VFMs as teachers for internal representation learning.
    *   Inspiring the development of more sophisticated alignment techniques.
    *   Providing a baseline for future research on visual reasoning in MLLMs.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The paper provides solid empirical evidence to support its claims, and the analysis is generally well-executed. The ablation studies are a significant strength, but the limited exploration of alternative approaches and the somewhat incremental novelty of the core idea are areas that prevent a higher score. While the approach is effective, the overall impact on the broader field might be somewhat constrained by the lack of a more fundamental breakthrough.

Score: 7

Rationale: The paper presents a technically sound and empirically validated method for improving visual reasoning in MLLMs. While the core idea isn't entirely new, the specific application to MLLMs and the thorough experimental evaluation are significant contributions. The paper addresses a clear problem, provides a simple and effective solution, and has the potential to influence future research in the field. The limitations regarding theoretical justification and scope, however, prevent it from achieving a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Parallel-R1: Towards Parallel Thinking via Reinforcement Learning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.07980v1)
- **Authors**: Tong Zheng, Hongming Zhang, Wenhao Yu, Xiaoyang Wang, Xinyu Yang, Runpeng Dai, Rui Liu, Huiwen Bao, Chengsong Huang, Heng Huang, Dong Yu
- **Abstract**: Parallel thinking has emerged as a novel approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) by exploring multiple reasoning paths concurrently. However, activating such capabilities through training remains challenging, as existing methods predominantly rely on supervised fine-tuning (SFT) over synthetic data, which encourages teacher-forced imitation rather than exploration and generalization. Different from them, we propose \textbf{Parallel-R1}, the first reinforcement learning (RL) framework that enables parallel thinking behaviors for complex real-world reasoning tasks. Our framework employs a progressive curriculum that explicitly addresses the cold-start problem in training parallel thinking with RL. We first use SFT on prompt-generated trajectories from easier tasks to instill the parallel thinking ability, then transition to RL to explore and generalize this skill on harder problems. Experiments on various math benchmarks, including MATH, AMC23, and AIME, show that Parallel-R1 successfully instills parallel thinking, leading to 8.4% accuracy improvements over the sequential thinking model trained directly on challenging tasks with RL. Further analysis reveals a clear shift in the model's thinking behavior: at an early stage, it uses parallel thinking as an exploration strategy, while in a later stage, it uses the same capability for multi-perspective verification. Most significantly, we validate parallel thinking as a \textbf{mid-training exploration scaffold}, where this temporary exploratory phase unlocks a higher performance ceiling after RL, yielding a 42.9% improvement over the baseline on AIME25. Our model, data, and code will be open-source at https://github.com/zhengkid/Parallel-R1.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Parallel-R1, a reinforcement learning (RL) framework designed to instill parallel thinking abilities in large language models (LLMs) for complex mathematical reasoning. Unlike previous supervised fine-tuning (SFT) approaches that rely on complex data pipelines, Parallel-R1 leverages a progressive curriculum. This curriculum first uses SFT on easier tasks to establish the basic format of parallel thinking and then transitions to RL to generalize this skill to harder problems. The framework incorporates novel reward designs to balance accuracy and the encouragement of parallel thinking. Through detailed analysis, the paper reveals that models initially use parallel thinking for exploration and later for multi-perspective verification. The authors also present an exploration-as-a-scaffold approach, which showed significant improvement on complex benchmarks. The paper includes ablations, comparisons, and architectural variations of structured and causal models to support the claims.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its use of RL to train LLMs to perform parallel thinking from scratch on general mathematical reasoning tasks.  While previous work has explored parallel thinking via test-time strategies and SFT, this is the first RL framework that addresses the cold-start problem through a progressive curriculum and dedicated reward design. The revelation of the evolving strategic role of parallel thinking (exploration to verification) is also a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper’s significance rests on several factors:
    *   It addresses a critical gap in LLM reasoning, moving beyond imitation of pre-generated trajectories to learning parallel thinking through exploration.
    *   The progressive curriculum effectively tackles the challenge of training LLMs with RL for complex tasks, specifically addressing the cold start problem.
    *   The empirical results demonstrate substantial accuracy improvements on challenging math benchmarks, highlighting the potential of parallel thinking to enhance reasoning capabilities.
    *   The analysis of how parallel thinking strategies evolve during training provides insights into the underlying mechanisms, which can guide future research and development.
    *   The exploration-as-a-scaffold validates the use of parallel thinking as a mid-training approach, unlocking higher final performance ceilings after RL training.

* **Strengths:**
    *   The progressive curriculum is well-motivated and effectively implemented.
    *   The reward design is a key contribution, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights into its effectiveness.
    *   The analysis of the evolving strategic role of parallel thinking is insightful and novel.
    *   The empirical results are comprehensive, with comparisons to strong baselines and ablations on key components.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper compares well against baseline techniques like SFT and basic RL methods, the novelty is somewhat limited by the already large body of work on chain-of-thought reasoning which also utilizes these methods.
    *   Mathematical benchmarks are good, but the tasks remain constrained. The authors need to also validate the efficacy on more open-ended reasoning tasks or real world scenarios.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field in several ways:
    *   It establishes RL as a viable approach for training LLMs in complex reasoning skills, such as parallel thinking.
    *   The progressive curriculum and reward design can be adapted and extended to other reasoning tasks and LLM architectures.
    *   The insights into the strategic role of parallel thinking can inform the development of more effective reasoning strategies.
    *   The findings could lead to the design of more robust and adaptable LLMs capable of handling complex, real-world problems.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel RL framework for parallel thinking in LLMs, addressing a significant challenge in reasoning. The progressive curriculum, detailed analysis of learning dynamics, and performance improvements on mathematical benchmarks contribute meaningfully to the field. However, the novelty is somewhat limited by the existing CoT research and the use of mathematical benchmarks only. A score of 7 reflects the substantial contribution and potential influence, while acknowledging the identified weaknesses and potential for further exploration in more open-ended tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### SciGPT: A Large Language Model for Scientific Literature Understanding and Knowledge Discovery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08032v1)
- **Authors**: Fengyu She, Nan Wang, Hongfei Wu, Ziyi Wan, Jingmian Wang, Chang Wang
- **Abstract**: Scientific literature is growing exponentially, creating a critical bottleneck for researchers to efficiently synthesize knowledge. While general-purpose Large Language Models (LLMs) show potential in text processing, they often fail to capture scientific domain-specific nuances (e.g., technical jargon, methodological rigor) and struggle with complex scientific tasks, limiting their utility for interdisciplinary research. To address these gaps, this paper presents SciGPT, a domain-adapted foundation model for scientific literature understanding and ScienceBench, an open source benchmark tailored to evaluate scientific LLMs. Built on the Qwen3 architecture, SciGPT incorporates three key innovations: (1) low-cost domain distillation via a two-stage pipeline to balance performance and efficiency; (2) a Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (SMoE) attention mechanism that cuts memory consumption by 55\% for 32,000-token long-document reasoning; and (3) knowledge-aware adaptation integrating domain ontologies to bridge interdisciplinary knowledge gaps. Experimental results on ScienceBench show that SciGPT outperforms GPT-4o in core scientific tasks including sequence labeling, generation, and inference. It also exhibits strong robustness in unseen scientific tasks, validating its potential to facilitate AI-augmented scientific discovery.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the SciGPT paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SciGPT, a domain-adapted large language model (LLM) designed specifically for scientific literature understanding and knowledge discovery.  It addresses the limitations of general-purpose LLMs in handling the nuances and complexities of scientific texts.  SciGPT is built upon the Qwen3 architecture and incorporates three key innovations: (1) a low-cost domain distillation pipeline (Qwen-DS), (2) a Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (SMoE) attention mechanism for efficient long-document reasoning, and (3) knowledge-aware adaptation integrating domain ontologies. The authors also introduce ScienceBench, an open-source benchmark tailored for evaluating scientific LLMs. Experimental results on ScienceBench demonstrate that SciGPT outperforms GPT-4o in core scientific tasks like sequence labeling, generation, and inference, while also showcasing strong robustness in unseen scientific tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing and new techniques, with the primary novelty residing in the application and integration of these techniques within the context of scientific literature processing. Domain adaptation via distillation is known, and sparse MoE is also an established technique. However, the specific *way* they are combined *with domain knowledge and ontology integration* for this particular problem *and specifically with respect to long documents* represents a nontrivial advance. The ScienceBench benchmark is also a valuable contribution, as the paper correctly points out the lack of suitable benchmarks for scientific LLMs. The combination of these contributions raises the paper's novelty.

*   **Significance:** The exponential growth of scientific literature presents a significant challenge for researchers, and the need for AI-powered tools to aid in knowledge synthesis is well-recognized. If SciGPT truly delivers on its performance claims, it could substantially improve the efficiency of scientific research. The open-sourcing of SciGPT and ScienceBench is crucial for the field. By providing the community with a model and benchmark, the paper facilitates further research and development in scientific LLMs. The focus on long-document reasoning is particularly important because scientific literature often requires processing extended contexts. The knowledge-aware adaptation approach potentially bridges interdisciplinary knowledge gaps, a key bottleneck in modern research.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a real and important problem:** The information overload in scientific literature.
    *   **Clear technical description:** The paper explains the SciGPT architecture and training process in a sufficiently detailed manner.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** ScienceBench provides a rigorous evaluation across several relevant tasks.
    *   **Open-source release:** Facilitates reproducibility and further development.
    *   **Focus on long-context and domain knowledge**: Handles the inherent features of scientific literature better.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The individual components (distillation, SMoE) are not groundbreaking on their own, but their careful combination and application to scientific text is.
    *   **Limited comparison:** The comparison is primarily against GPT-4(o).  Comparing to other domain-specific LLMs, if any exist, would provide a stronger justification for SciGPT's superiority.
    *   **Overclaims of outperformance**: The paper claims better performance than GPT-4o on 'complex' tasks, which are not well defined.
    *   **Lack of Ablation Studies:** The impact of each individual contribution (e.g. ontologies) isn't clearly established. It is hard to distinguish the impact of Qwen-DS vs SMoE from the effect of domain knowledge addition.
    *   **ScienceBench limitations:** Although ScienceBench is a welcome addition, its scale (number of samples) is relatively small in some tasks, which is a concern. More validation on the diversity and representativeness of ScienceBench would strengthen the evaluation.
    *   **The claims about robustness** in the paper is not substantiated very well. The evidence provided is very narrow, and it is unclear whether the improvements are generalizable.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the performance claims are validated by the community, SciGPT could have a significant impact on scientific research. It could accelerate knowledge synthesis, facilitate interdisciplinary research, and potentially lead to new scientific discoveries.

**Justification for Score:**

Based on the evaluation, I'm assigning a score of 7. The paper addresses an important problem and presents a valuable contribution in the form of SciGPT and ScienceBench.  While the individual components of SciGPT are not entirely novel, their integration and application to the scientific domain show ingenuity. The open-sourcing of the model and benchmark is a significant strength, and the evaluation demonstrates promising results. However, the limitations discussed above -- incremental novelty, the limited comparisons with domain-specific models (assuming they exist), lack of ablation studies, and robustness of results -- prevent a higher score. The paper has the potential for a considerable impact, but further validation and improvements are needed.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### No for Some, Yes for Others: Persona Prompts and Other Sources of False Refusal in Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08075v1)
- **Authors**: Flor Miriam Plaza-del-Arco, Paul Röttger, Nino Scherrer, Emanuele Borgonovo, Elmar Plischke, Dirk Hovy
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly integrated into our daily lives and personalized. However, LLM personalization might also increase unintended side effects. Recent work suggests that persona prompting can lead models to falsely refuse user requests. However, no work has fully quantified the extent of this issue. To address this gap, we measure the impact of 15 sociodemographic personas (based on gender, race, religion, and disability) on false refusal. To control for other factors, we also test 16 different models, 3 tasks (Natural Language Inference, politeness, and offensiveness classification), and nine prompt paraphrases. We propose a Monte Carlo-based method to quantify this issue in a sample-efficient manner. Our results show that as models become more capable, personas impact the refusal rate less and less. Certain sociodemographic personas increase false refusal in some models, which suggests underlying biases in the alignment strategies or safety mechanisms. However, we find that the model choice and task significantly influence false refusals, especially in sensitive content tasks. Our findings suggest that persona effects have been overestimated, and might be due to other factors.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of persona prompting on false refusal rates in large language models (LLMs).  It systematically evaluates how different sociodemographic personas (gender, race, religion, disability), task choice (NLI, politeness, offensiveness classification), prompt paraphrases, and model choice affect the likelihood of LLMs refusing to answer seemingly safe requests.  The authors propose a Monte Carlo sampling method to efficiently explore the vast combinatorial space of these factors.  Their findings suggest that model choice and task choice have a greater influence on refusal rates than persona prompting, and that the impact of personas diminishes as models become more capable.  The study also reveals biases in certain models related to specific sociodemographic groups, and highlights the importance of careful experimental design and transparent reporting in LLM research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a significant contribution by moving beyond simple demonstrations of persona-based refusal and towards a more systematic quantification of the phenomenon.  Prior work highlighted the existence of this issue, but this paper attempts to isolate and measure the relative impact of different contributing factors. The Monte Carlo sampling approach is a novel methodological contribution, offering a practical way to manage the complexity of the experimental design. The examination across multiple model families and generations also provides a valuable temporal perspective. However, the use of simple string matching for refusal detection limits the scope and accuracy of analysis, which is a well known drawback to this approach and needs to be acknowledged more directly in the paper.

*   **Significance:**  The findings have several important implications:

    *   They suggest that the focus on persona prompting as the primary driver of false refusals may be misplaced, with model and task characteristics playing a more significant role.  This helps to refine the research agenda in the area.
    *   The identified biases related to specific personas underscore the ongoing challenges in ensuring fairness and equity in LLM behavior.
    *   The emphasis on experimental design and transparent reporting highlights the need for more rigorous methodologies in LLM research to avoid misleading conclusions.
    * The Wasserstein Distance analysis used to measure the sensitivity of models to different features is a notable strength, as it provides a quantitative metric for comparing the influence of various factors on refusal behavior. This allows the authors to move beyond anecdotal observations and make more concrete claims about the relative importance of model choice, task selection, and persona prompting.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The automatic refusal detection method based on string matching is a potential source of error. More sophisticated methods might reveal nuances in model behavior that are missed by this approach. This approach might overestimate or underestimate the number of refusals and reduce the strength of the conclusions drawn from the study.
    *   While the Monte Carlo sampling improves efficiency, it also introduces a degree of approximation, making the results less exact as compared to a more exhaustive assessment.
    *   The limitation to English-language tasks restricts the generalizability of the findings to other languages and cultural contexts. The influence of persona might vary across different languages.
    *   The study primarily examines smaller models.  It's unclear whether the findings would hold for larger, more capable models.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to influence future research on LLM safety and fairness by encouraging a more nuanced and systematic approach to studying false refusals.  It will also be of interest to practitioners who are developing and deploying LLMs in real-world applications, helping them to better understand and mitigate the potential for biased and unfair behavior. The findings will hopefully drive the development of improved safety and alignment strategies, taking into account a broader range of factors beyond just persona prompts.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's strong systematic approach, novel methodology, and significant findings regarding the relative importance of different factors contributing to false refusals in LLMs warrant a solid score. However, the methodological limitations (automatic refusal detection and sampling approximation), restrict the degree to which it should be considered a foundational study.  Taking these strengths and weaknesses into account, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ChatGPT for Code Refactoring: Analyzing Topics, Interaction, and Effective Prompts
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08090v1)
- **Authors**: Eman Abdullah AlOmar, Luo Xu, Sofia Martinez, Anthony Peruma, Mohamed Wiem Mkaouer, Christian D. Newman, Ali Ouni
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), such as ChatGPT, have become widely popular and widely used in various software engineering tasks such as refactoring, testing, code review, and program comprehension. Although recent studies have examined the effectiveness of LLMs in recommending and suggesting refactoring, there is a limited understanding of how developers express their refactoring needs when interacting with ChatGPT. In this paper, our goal is to explore interactions related to refactoring between developers and ChatGPT to better understand how developers identify areas for improvement in code, and how ChatGPT addresses developers' needs. Our approach involves text mining 715 refactoring-related interactions from 29,778 ChatGPT prompts and responses, as well as the analysis of developers' explicit refactoring intentions.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "ChatGPT for Code Refactoring: Analyzing Topics, Interaction, and Effective Prompts":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates how developers interact with ChatGPT when using it for code refactoring. The authors analyze a dataset of 715 refactoring-related interactions extracted from a larger set of ChatGPT prompts and responses. They identify common themes and topics discussed during these interactions, categorize the types of responses ChatGPT provides (including affirmations and apologies), and propose a prompt template designed to elicit more effective refactoring suggestions from the model. The study aims to understand developer expectations when using AI-assisted refactoring and to improve the quality of ChatGPT's responses through better prompt engineering. Key findings include a taxonomy of refactoring topics, observations on ChatGPT's use of affirmations and apologies in responses, and the evaluation of a refactoring prompt template to achieve concise, accurate, and satisfactory responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper tackles an interesting and relevant problem: understanding how developers interact with and leverage Large Language Models (LLMs) for code refactoring. While there's growing research on using LLMs for various software engineering tasks, the focus on *refactoring interactions* specifically, including the analysis of developer expectations and ChatGPT's apologetic/affirmative responses, adds a unique perspective. The taxonomy of refactoring topics is also a potentially valuable contribution. The prompt template itself isn't entirely novel, as other prompt engineering efforts exist, but the specific design tailored to refactoring and the empirical evaluation make it more useful. Prior work has primarily focused on benchmarking LLM refactoring capabilities. This paper fills a gap by examining *how developers perceive and interact* with these capabilities.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have practical implications for both researchers and practitioners. Understanding the topics developers discuss with ChatGPT during refactoring and the types of responses they receive can inform the design of better AI-assisted refactoring tools. The prompt template offers a concrete starting point for developers who want to use ChatGPT for refactoring more effectively. Identifying when ChatGPT struggles (leading to apologies) can guide future research into improving the model's capabilities in those specific areas. The study's focus on developer interaction moves beyond simply evaluating LLM code generation and contributes to the broader field of Human-AI collaboration in software engineering.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Approach:** The study uses a solid empirical approach, analyzing a substantial dataset of real-world interactions.
    *   **Clear Research Questions:** The research questions are well-defined and address important aspects of the problem.
    *   **Taxonomy:** The development of a taxonomy for ChatGPT refactoring topics provides a structured understanding of the domain.
    *   **Prompt Template:** The prompt template is a practical outcome that can be directly applied by developers.
    *   **Identification of Failure Points:** The analysis of ChatGPT's apologies offers valuable insights into the limitations of the model.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability:** The study focuses on ChatGPT, and while the findings may be relevant to other LLMs, this is not explicitly demonstrated. It needs evaluation with other LLMs.
    *   **Prompt Template Evaluation:** While the authors evaluate their prompt template, the evaluation could be strengthened by comparing it against other existing prompt engineering techniques. The prompt evaluation is compared to DevGPT. It needs to be validated using other LLMs and real-world developers.
    *   **Limited Severity Analysis**: The paper acknowledges that LLMs can introduce errors (fabrications, outdated API calls). A more thorough severity analysis of these errors would strengthen the paper. It also needs a case study with real-world developers evaluating the results of different prompts to evaluate the validity of results.
    *   **Threats to validity**: More work needs to be done in addressing threats to validity, especially construct validity, where potential problems are well-acknowledged.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence the design of future AI-assisted refactoring tools and prompt engineering techniques. It could also stimulate further research into Human-AI collaboration in software engineering and the limitations of LLMs in specific software development tasks.

*   **Overall:** The paper makes a valuable contribution to understanding the use of LLMs for code refactoring, particularly from a human-centric perspective. While there are some limitations, the study offers practical insights and paves the way for future research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper is novel, addresses an important topic, and has the potential to influence the field. It is mostly well executed with sufficient validity and a reasonable amount of data, but generalizability is limited and could be strengthened. While the paper shows promising findings, further development is needed to address its weaknesses. The use of real-world developers can validate the benefits of the proposed prompt. More LLMs need to be added to the prompt for validation. Thus, while contributing valuable insights, the areas for improvement prevent a higher rating.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Culturally transmitted color categories in LLMs reflect a learning bias toward efficient compression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08093v1)
- **Authors**: Nathaniel Imel, Noga Zaslavsky
- **Abstract**: Converging evidence suggests that systems of semantic categories across human languages achieve near-optimal compression via the Information Bottleneck (IB) complexity-accuracy principle. Large language models (LLMs) are not trained for this objective, which raises the question: are LLMs capable of evolving efficient human-like semantic systems? To address this question, we focus on the domain of color as a key testbed of cognitive theories of categorization and replicate with LLMs (Gemini 2.0-flash and Llama 3.3-70B-Instruct) two influential human behavioral studies. First, we conduct an English color-naming study, showing that Gemini aligns well with the naming patterns of native English speakers and achieves a significantly high IB-efficiency score, while Llama exhibits an efficient but lower complexity system compared to English. Second, to test whether LLMs simply mimic patterns in their training data or actually exhibit a human-like inductive bias toward IB-efficiency, we simulate cultural evolution of pseudo color-naming systems in LLMs via iterated in-context language learning. We find that akin to humans, LLMs iteratively restructure initially random systems towards greater IB-efficiency and increased alignment with patterns observed across the world's languages. These findings demonstrate that LLMs are capable of evolving perceptually grounded, human-like semantic systems, driven by the same fundamental principle that governs semantic efficiency across human languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can develop human-like semantic color categories, even without explicit training for efficiency. It draws on the Information Bottleneck (IB) principle, which suggests that human languages efficiently compress meanings into words. The authors replicate two human behavioral studies with LLMs (Gemini and Llama): an English color-naming experiment and an iterated language learning (IICLL) experiment. The results show that Gemini aligns well with English color naming and achieves high IB-efficiency. Both LLMs, similar to humans, restructure randomly initialized systems towards greater IB-efficiency and human-alignment through IICLL. The authors conclude that LLMs are capable of evolving human-like semantic systems driven by the principle of efficient communication.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Application of Theory:** The paper makes a strong case for connecting the IB principle with the capabilities of LLMs. This is a fresh and compelling application of the theoretical framework to a domain where its relevance hadn't been as thoroughly explored.
*   **Cogent Experimental Design:** Replicating classic human behavioral studies (color naming and iterated learning) provides a solid foundation for comparing LLM behavior to human behavior. The IICLL design is particularly insightful, as it allows the LLMs to "evolve" semantic systems.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The findings that LLMs can align with human color naming systems and improve IB-efficiency through iterated learning provide substantial evidence for the authors' claim. The comparisons between Gemini and Llama provide additional nuance and insight into the roles of model size and input modality.
*   **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is easy to follow and understand, even for readers who are not deeply familiar with all the relevant concepts (e.g., Information Bottleneck, iterated learning).
*   **Addresses a Significant Question:** The ability of LLMs to develop human-aligned semantic systems is crucial for ensuring effective communication and adaptation to changing environments. This is a question of great importance for AI safety and usability.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Model Scope:** The study only focuses on two LLMs (Gemini and Llama). While these models are prominent, it would be valuable to extend the analysis to a wider range of LLMs, including those with different architectures and training data. This would help to determine the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Simplification of Cultural Transmission:** While the IICLL paradigm is insightful, it is a simplified model of cultural transmission. In reality, cultural evolution is far more complex and involves factors such as social interaction, imitation, and innovation.
*   **Potential Confounding Factors:** The models were pre-trained on massive datasets. It is difficult to definitively rule out the possibility that the observed patterns were simply learned from the training data, rather than emerging as a result of an inductive bias toward IB-efficiency. Although the pseudo-words mitigate this, there could be subtle influences remaining.
*   **Lack of Detailed Ablation Studies:** The study would benefit from more detailed ablation studies to isolate the specific factors that contribute to the observed results. For example, it would be helpful to investigate the impact of different in-context learning strategies, loss functions, or model architectures on IB-efficiency and human-alignment.

**Novelty and Significance:**

This paper is a novel and significant contribution to the field. It's one of the first to examine the capability of LLMs to develop human-like semantic categories through the lens of the Information Bottleneck principle. The use of iterated language learning to probe the inductive biases of LLMs is particularly innovative.

The paper's significance lies in its demonstration that LLMs possess an inherent tendency to structure meaning in a way that is efficient and aligned with human cognition. This finding has important implications for the design and development of LLMs that can effectively communicate and adapt to human needs. It also suggests that LLMs may be capable of evolving semantic systems without explicit training.

Despite the potential limitations, the paper offers valuable insights into the learning biases and representational capacities of LLMs, and paves the way for future research in this area.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a novel application of an established theoretical framework, utilizes well-designed experiments, and generates convincing empirical evidence to answer an important and open question about LLMs. The combination of the Information Bottleneck principle and Iterated Language Learning within the context of LLMs makes this work significantly novel. While further exploration with diverse models and refined experimental designs is warranted, the paper's insights are substantial and should stimulate meaningful research in the field. Therefore, an 8 is assigned in recognition of the paper's rigor, novelty, and potential influence.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### MERLIN: Multi-Stage Curriculum Alignment for Multilingual Encoder and LLM Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08105v1)
- **Authors**: Kosei Uemura, David Guzmán, Quang Phuoc Nguyen, Jesujoba Oluwadara Alabi, En-shiun Annie Lee, David Ifeoluwa Adelani
- **Abstract**: Large language models excel in English but still struggle with complex reasoning in many low-resource languages (LRLs). Existing encoder-plus-decoder methods such as LangBridge and MindMerger raise accuracy on mid and high-resource languages, yet they leave a large gap on LRLs. We present MERLIN, a two-stage model-stacking framework that applies a curriculum learning strategy -- from general bilingual bitext to task-specific data -- and adapts only a small set of DoRA weights. On the AfriMGSM benchmark MERLIN improves exact-match accuracy by +12.9 pp over MindMerger and outperforms GPT-4o-mini. It also yields consistent gains on MGSM and MSVAMP (+0.9 and +2.8 pp), demonstrating effectiveness across both low and high-resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MERLIN, a two-stage framework designed to improve the multilingual reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs), especially in low-resource languages (LRLs). MERLIN uses a curriculum learning strategy. The first stage aligns a strong multilingual encoder with a frozen LLM through successive translation tasks, starting from general bilingual bitext to task-specific data, adapting only a small set of DORA weights. The second stage fine-tunes the LLM itself via parameter-efficient fine-tuning while keeping the learned alignment layer fixed. The approach is evaluated on various math reasoning datasets (MGSM, MSVAMP, AfriMGSM) and a natural language inference dataset (AfriXNLI).  MERLIN demonstrates significant performance improvements compared to existing methods, including LangBridge and MindMerger, particularly on LRLs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of several existing ideas into a cohesive and effective framework specifically tailored for LRLs. While model stacking, curriculum learning, alignment approaches, and parameter-efficient fine-tuning are not individually novel, MERLIN's distinct contribution lies in the specific way these concepts are combined and adapted. The curriculum learning approach, particularly, is well-justified and empirically supported by the ablation studies. The attention to LRL performance is another strength, addressing a significant gap in LLM capabilities.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses an important problem in the LLM field: the performance disparity between high-resource languages and LRLs. By creating a resource-efficient framework that leverages pre-trained multilingual encoders and targeted fine-tuning, the paper has the potential to enable improved reasoning abilities in LRLs with far less computational expense than other approaches. The performance improvements on AfriMGSM and AfriXNLI, especially compared to established baselines and closed source models such as GPT-4 mini, highlight the practical significance of MERLIN.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   The clear and well-structured presentation of the MERLIN framework.
    *   The rigorous empirical evaluation across diverse datasets and languages.
    *   The detailed ablation studies demonstrating the contribution of each component.
    *   The focus on improving LRL performance, a crucial area for equitable access to AI capabilities.
    *   Release of the code and models to facilitate further research and adoption.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The reliance on automatically translated data, a potential source of noise and error.
    *   The limited scope of the evaluation, focusing primarily on math reasoning and NLI. Other tasks and reasoning types could be explored.
    *   The use of separate MERLIN instances for each benchmark, preventing parameter sharing and cross-task transfer. A multi-task setup may lead to increased efficiency.
    *   It is not immediately clear how well this technique would scale to more complex reasoning tasks, given its design relies on a relatively strong multilingual encoder already providing good representations. If the encoder itself is not strong enough, the whole pipeline could falter.

*   **Justification for Score:** I'm assigning a score of **8**. This paper combines several existing techniques in a novel way, shows thorough analysis, and provides significant results, particularly in a key area of need (LRLs). While individual components are not revolutionary and there are limitations in the scope of experiments, the combination yields clear improvements that can impact the field. The strong empirical evidence and the focus on LRL performance are notable strengths.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### SCA-LLM: Spectral-Attentive Channel Prediction with Large Language Models in MIMO-OFDM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08139v1)
- **Authors**: Ke He, Le He, Lisheng Fan, Xianfu Lei, Thang X. Vu, George K. Karagiannidis, Symeon Chatzinotas
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the success of large language models (LLMs) has inspired growing interest in exploring their potential applications in wireless communications, especially for channel prediction tasks. However, directly applying LLMs to channel prediction faces a domain mismatch issue stemming from their text-based pre-training. To mitigate this, the ``adapter + LLM" paradigm has emerged, where an adapter is designed to bridge the domain gap between the channel state information (CSI) data and LLMs. While showing initial success, existing adapters may not fully exploit the potential of this paradigm. To address this limitation, this work provides a key insight that learning representations from the spectral components of CSI features can more effectively help bridge the domain gap. Accordingly, we propose a spectral-attentive framework, named SCA-LLM, for channel prediction in multiple-input multiple-output orthogonal frequency division multiplexing (MIMO-OFDM) systems. Specifically, its novel adapter can capture finer spectral details and better adapt the LLM for channel prediction than previous methods. Extensive simulations show that SCA-LLM achieves state-of-the-art prediction performance and strong generalization, yielding up to $-2.4~\text{dB}$ normalized mean squared error (NMSE) advantage over the previous LLM based method. Ablation studies further confirm the superiority of SCA-LLM in mitigating domain mismatch.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "SCA-LLM: Spectral-Attentive Channel Prediction with Large Language Models in MIMO-OFDM" introduces a novel framework for channel prediction in MIMO-OFDM systems. It addresses the domain mismatch issue encountered when directly applying large language models (LLMs) to channel data. The proposed SCA-LLM uses a spectral-attentive adapter to bridge this gap by learning representations from the spectral components of the channel state information (CSI). The adapter uses multi-spectral channel attention layers based on the 2D discrete cosine transform (DCT) to capture richer spectral information. The SCA-LLM consists of a spectral-attentive adapter, a pre-trained LLM, and an output head. Extensive simulations demonstrate that SCA-LLM achieves state-of-the-art prediction performance and strong generalization across different communication scenarios. Ablation studies confirm the superiority of SCA-LLM in mitigating domain mismatch.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's key novelty lies in the design of the spectral-attentive adapter, particularly the use of multi-spectral channel attention layers based on the 2D DCT. This is a significant improvement over prior adapter designs (like LLM4CP) that rely solely on global average pooling, which discards potentially valuable spectral information. The insight that learning representations from spectral components can effectively bridge the domain gap is a valuable contribution.
*   **Significance:** The work addresses a relevant and important problem: improving channel prediction in MIMO-OFDM systems. Accurate channel prediction is crucial for adaptive techniques that enhance performance in wireless communication. LLMs offer promise for this task, but the domain mismatch issue needs to be addressed effectively. SCA-LLM makes a significant step towards addressing this problem. The results demonstrate significant improvements over existing LLM-based methods and other traditional approaches. This can lead to more efficient resource allocation and improved communication reliability in MIMO-OFDM systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Technical Approach:** The use of spectral attention and 2D DCT within the adapter is a sound technical approach. The design choices are well-justified.
*   **Extensive Experimental Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive experimental evaluation, including comparisons with several baselines and ablation studies.  The results consistently demonstrate the superiority of the SCA-LLM framework.  The use of both in-distribution (UMa) and out-of-distribution (UMi) test sets showcases the generalization capabilities.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the problem, the proposed solution, and the experimental results.
*   **Addresses an Important Problem:** Channel prediction is a crucial aspect of improving wireless communication systems.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Complexity:** While the design is technically sound, the SCA module adds complexity to the system. Further exploration of more efficient implementations might be beneficial.
*   **Limited Scope:** The focus is primarily on the adapter design. While the choice of GPT-2 is reasonable, exploring other LLM architectures could potentially lead to further performance improvements.
*   **Dependency on QuaDRiGa Simulator**: The reliance on the QuaDRiGa channel simulator could be seen as a limitation, as it might not capture all real-world channel characteristics. Validating the approach with real-world channel data would strengthen the findings.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** While the use of spectral attention with 2D DCT within the adapter is novel, the overall "adapter + LLM" paradigm is already established. The contribution, while significant, builds upon existing work rather than representing a radical departure.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by providing a novel and effective method for adapting LLMs for channel prediction. The improvement over existing LLM-based techniques, especially in terms of generalization, is significant. The technical approach is sound, the experimental evaluation is comprehensive, and the paper is well-written.

However, the approach builds upon existing paradigms (adapter+LLM) and the benefits are primarily derived from more effective spectral feature extraction rather than revolutionary LLM adaptation techniques. The dependence on simulated data and the inherent complexity of the proposed model also temper its potential impact.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.IT
- **Score**: 7/10

### From Limited Data to Rare-event Prediction: LLM-powered Feature Engineering and Multi-model Learning in Venture Capital
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08140v1)
- **Authors**: Mihir Kumar, Aaron Ontoyin Yin, Zakari Salifu, Kelvin Amoaba, Afriyie Kwesi Samuel, Fuat Alican, Yigit Ihlamur
- **Abstract**: This paper presents a framework for predicting rare, high-impact outcomes by integrating large language models (LLMs) with a multi-model machine learning (ML) architecture. The approach combines the predictive strength of black-box models with the interpretability required for reliable decision-making. We use LLM-powered feature engineering to extract and synthesize complex signals from unstructured data, which are then processed within a layered ensemble of models including XGBoost, Random Forest, and Linear Regression. The ensemble first produces a continuous estimate of success likelihood, which is then thresholded to produce a binary rare-event prediction. We apply this framework to the domain of Venture Capital (VC), where investors must evaluate startups with limited and noisy early-stage data. The empirical results show strong performance: the model achieves precision between 9.8X and 11.1X the random classifier baseline in three independent test subsets. Feature sensitivity analysis further reveals interpretable success drivers: the startup's category list accounts for 15.6% of predictive influence, followed by the number of founders, while education level and domain expertise contribute smaller yet consistent effects.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework for predicting rare, high-impact outcomes, specifically focusing on startup success in Venture Capital (VC).  The approach combines Large Language Models (LLMs) for feature engineering from unstructured data (founder profiles, company descriptions) with a multi-model machine learning architecture (XGBoost, Random Forest, Linear Regression). The system first produces a continuous funding prediction, then uses a logistic regression layer to predict a binary success outcome, optimizing for high precision. The authors evaluate their system on a dataset of startup founders, demonstrating significantly improved precision compared to a random classifier baseline. The paper also includes feature sensitivity analysis, revealing key factors influencing success predictions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of combining LLMs for feature engineering with a more traditional ensemble learning approach is not entirely novel, as the paper itself cites related works (GPTree, RRF).  However, the *specific* application to the VC domain, the focus on high-precision rare-event prediction, and the thorough ablation studies contribute some degree of novelty. The details of how the LLMs are used for feature extraction and the specific ensemble architecture are also important differentiators.  It goes further than previous work by employing LLMs for feature selection and engineering across a richer feature space to achieve a higher precision of 10x compared to previous work like GPTree which has a precision of 9.4x.

* **Significance:** Predicting startup success is a practically important problem with significant financial implications.  The paper's demonstration of improved precision over a baseline suggests the potential for better investment decisions. The interpretability aspects are also important, as understanding the drivers of success is crucial for VC firms. Feature sensitivity analysis provides insights into the relative importance of different factors.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical Validation:**  The paper includes thorough empirical validation with a sizable dataset and appropriate evaluation metrics (precision, recall, MAPE).  The use of multiple test subsets enhances the robustness of the results.
    * **Ablation Studies:** The extensive ablation studies are a major strength, systematically evaluating the contribution of different components of the system, including the LLM-derived features, embedding sources, and model architecture choices. This allows to isolate the impact of individual design decisions and offers insights into the system's operation.
    * **Interpretability:** The paper addresses the important aspect of interpretability, providing feature sensitivity analysis that highlights key drivers of success, thus enabling the decision-makers to understand the model output.
    * **Practical Application:** The VC focus offers a specific, practically relevant application for the techniques used.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Generalizability:** A major concern is the generalizability of the results to other VC ecosystems or different types of rare-event prediction tasks.  The specific features and relationships learned might be highly dependent on the particular dataset used.
    * **Data Quality and Bias:** The paper acknowledges the limitations of relying on publicly available data, which may be biased.  The LLM-derived features are also subject to the potential for misclassification and hallucination. The reliance on data reflecting only founders with a significant online presence is a substantial bias that's acknowledged but not addressed or mitigated.
    * **Limited Baseline:** The comparison to a random classifier, while useful, is a relatively weak baseline. A comparison to other existing VC prediction models, if available, would strengthen the evaluation.
    * **Reproducibility:** While the paper describes the methodology in detail, it lacks information on code and datasets. The practical implementation of the proposed models often involves multiple hyper-parameters tuning in different steps, which requires significant work to reproduce.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the adoption of LLM-based feature engineering in VC and other related domains. The focus on high precision and interpretability aligns with the requirements of real-world decision-making. The ablation studies offer useful guidance for building similar systems.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a strong approach and convincing results, however, there are several major limitations that prevents the overall rating from going up. The combination of LLMs and traditional ML has value, and the empirical study is performed well. The weaknesses are also significant, especially the potential for bias and lack of generalizability, lack of code and data.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Bias after Prompting: Persistent Discrimination in Large Language Models
