- **Score**: 7/10

### Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting in Large Language Models with Forgetting-aware Pruning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08255v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Huang, Anda Cheng, Yinggui Wang
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have shown impressive capabilities in various downstream tasks but typically face Catastrophic Forgetting (CF) during fine-tuning. In this paper, we propose the Forgetting-Aware Pruning Metric (FAPM), a novel pruning-based approach to balance CF and downstream task performance. Our investigation reveals that the degree to which task vectors (i.e., the subtraction of pre-trained weights from the weights fine-tuned on downstream tasks) overlap with pre-trained model parameters is a critical factor for CF. Based on this finding, FAPM employs the ratio of the task vector to pre-trained model parameters as a metric to quantify CF, integrating this measure into the pruning criteria. Importantly, FAPM does not necessitate modifications to the training process or model architecture, nor does it require any auxiliary data. We conducted extensive experiments across eight datasets, covering natural language inference, General Q&A, Medical Q&A, Math Q&A, reading comprehension, and cloze tests. The results demonstrate that FAPM limits CF to just 0.25\% while maintaining 99.67\% accuracy on downstream tasks. We provide the code to reproduce our results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses catastrophic forgetting (CF) in large language models (LLMs) during fine-tuning.  It proposes a novel pruning-based approach called Forgetting-Aware Pruning Metric (FAPM). FAPM leverages the ratio between the task vector (the difference between fine-tuned and pre-trained weights) and the pre-trained model parameters. The core idea is that high ratios indicate parameters prone to inducing CF.  FAPM integrates this ratio into a pruning criterion, selectively removing weights to balance downstream task performance and knowledge retention. The method doesn't require modifications to the training process, model architecture, or any auxiliary data. Experiments conducted on various datasets (natural language inference, question answering, cloze tests, and reading comprehension) show that FAPM limits CF while maintaining accuracy on downstream tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific pruning metric used – the ratio of the task vector to the pre-trained weights.  While the concept of using pruning to mitigate CF is not entirely new, the proposed FAPM metric offers a fresh perspective by explicitly considering the relationship between fine-tuned and pre-trained parameters. It's a relatively simple yet potentially effective way to identify parameters critical for knowledge retention.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is in providing a practical and lightweight solution for mitigating CF.  The method is attractive because it doesn't require access to pre-training data or modifications to the training process. Its ease of implementation and computational efficiency make it potentially valuable for practitioners deploying LLMs in real-world scenarios. The experimental results seem promising, demonstrating a good balance between downstream task performance and CF reduction.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Simple and Effective:** The proposed FAPM metric is relatively simple to understand and implement, yet appears to be effective in mitigating CF.
    *   **Practicality:**  The method doesn't require access to pre-training data or modifications to training, increasing its usability.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a comprehensive set of experiments across different datasets and LLMs, strengthening the validity of the results.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The analysis of sparsity ratios contributes to understanding FAPM's behavior.
    *   **LoRA Extension:** Addressing the applicability to LoRA is a positive aspect and shows awareness of PEFT techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited theoretical justification:** While the paper provides some intuition behind FAPM, a more rigorous theoretical analysis of why the proposed metric is effective would be beneficial.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the results are promising, the improvements over strong baselines (particularly some of the weight-based methods) might be incremental rather than groundbreaking. More direct comparison to SOTA pruning methods on the same task would increase confidence.
    *   **Specificity to certain tasks**: The evaluation mainly focuses on the specific tasks as the performance metric. Whether FAPM would generalize effectively across broader and varying tasks requires further investigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence researchers and practitioners to consider the relationship between task vectors and pre-trained weights when addressing CF. Its simplicity and ease of implementation could lead to its adoption as a baseline for future research on CF mitigation techniques.
    * The work provides a specific metric for balancing specialization and generation in LLMs, offering new practical guidance for training LLMs.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and practical method for mitigating catastrophic forgetting in LLMs. The FAPM metric offers a valuable and simple addition to pruning approaches, and the extensive experiments support the effectiveness of the technique.  However, the improvements, while consistent, are not dramatically better than existing techniques and theoretical justification needs more backing.


- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### A Systematic Survey on Large Language Models for Evolutionary Optimization: From Modeling to Solving
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08269v1)
- **Authors**: Yisong Zhang, Ran Cheng, Guoxing Yi, Kay Chen Tan
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs), with their strong understanding and reasoning capabilities, are increasingly being explored for tackling optimization problems, especially in synergy with evolutionary computation. Despite rapid progress, however, the field still lacks a unified synthesis and a systematic taxonomy. This survey addresses this gap by providing a comprehensive review of recent developments and organizing them within a structured framework. We classify existing research into two main stages: LLMs for optimization modeling and LLMs for optimization solving. The latter is further divided into three paradigms according to the role of LLMs in the optimization workflow: LLMs as stand-alone optimizers, low-level LLMs embedded within optimization algorithms, and high-level LLMs for algorithm selection and generation. For each category, we analyze representative methods, distill technical challenges, and examine their interplay with traditional approaches. We also review interdisciplinary applications spanning the natural sciences, engineering, and machine learning. By contrasting LLM-driven and conventional methods, we highlight key limitations and research gaps, and point toward future directions for developing self-evolving agentic ecosystems for optimization. An up-to-date collection of related literature is maintained at https://github.com/ishmael233/LLM4OPT.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a systematic survey of research on using Large Language Models (LLMs) for evolutionary optimization. It categorizes existing work into two main stages: (1) LLMs for optimization modeling, and (2) LLMs for optimization solving. The latter is further subdivided into three paradigms: LLMs as stand-alone optimizers, low-level LLMs embedded within optimization algorithms, and high-level LLMs for algorithm selection and generation. For each category, the authors analyze representative methods, technical challenges, and their connection to traditional approaches.  They also review interdisciplinary applications and highlight limitations, research gaps, and future directions, envisioning a future with self-evolving agentic optimization ecosystems. The survey aims to provide a unified framework for understanding and organizing research in this emerging interdisciplinary field.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Scope:** The survey aims to cover the entire spectrum of LLM integration with evolutionary optimization, from modeling to solving, providing a holistic overview of the field. The proposed taxonomy, while perhaps not revolutionary, is helpful for organizing the disparate research efforts.
*   **Clear Structure:** The paper is well-organized, clearly delineating the different stages and paradigms. This makes it easier for readers to navigate the complex landscape of LLM-augmented optimization.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The authors go beyond simply listing papers; they delve into the technical challenges and limitations of each approach, offering valuable insights.
*   **Future Directions:** The paper identifies key research gaps and envisions future developments, including bridging modeling and solving, transitioning to dynamic methods, and building agentic optimization ecosystems. These are potentially impactful areas for future research.
*   **Up-to-date Resource:** The accompanying GitHub repository is a valuable resource, providing a dynamic collection of related literature.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty in Taxonomy:** While the proposed taxonomy is useful, it primarily serves as a classification scheme rather than a groundbreaking conceptual innovation. A deeper, more theoretically grounded framework could have significantly enhanced the paper's impact. The connections between the different categories and potential synergies are not explored deeply enough.
*   **Superficial Evaluation of Some Approaches:**  The depth of analysis varies across different approaches. Some methods receive more detailed scrutiny than others. It could benefit from more critical assessment of the empirical results presented in the surveyed papers.  Are the performance gains attributed to LLMs truly significant, or could they be explained by other factors?
*   **Overly Optimistic Vision:** While the vision of self-evolving agentic ecosystems is appealing, it may be overly optimistic given the current limitations of LLMs, particularly their mathematical reasoning abilities and the computational cost associated with their use. The challenges associated with realizing this vision are perhaps understated.
*   **Missing Critical Benchmarking:**  The paper lacks a rigorous meta-analysis of the performance of various LLM-enhanced optimization techniques compared to traditional methods.  A table summarizing performance metrics across different problem domains would have been very useful.  Without this, it's difficult to gauge the true practical impact of LLMs in this field.

**Significance:**

The survey addresses a clear need for a comprehensive synthesis of the rapidly growing research area of LLMs for optimization.  It provides a valuable resource for researchers entering the field and helps to structure the existing literature. However, the relatively incremental nature of the proposed taxonomy and the lack of critical benchmarking somewhat limit the paper's overall significance.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper's comprehensiveness and clear structure are definite strengths. However, the lack of deep theoretical insights, variable analysis depth, and the somewhat optimistic vision prevent it from achieving a higher score. The survey provides a useful overview but needs a stronger analytical and critical component to be considered a truly exceptional contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.NE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Who Gets Seen in the Age of AI? Adoption Patterns of Large Language Models in Scholarly Writing and Citation Outcomes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08306v1)
- **Authors**: Farhan Kamrul Khan, Hazem Ibrahim, Nouar Aldahoul, Talal Rahwan, Yasir Zaki
- **Abstract**: The rapid adoption of generative AI tools is reshaping how scholars produce and communicate knowledge, raising questions about who benefits and who is left behind. We analyze over 230,000 Scopus-indexed computer science articles between 2021 and 2025 to examine how AI-assisted writing alters scholarly visibility across regions. Using zero-shot detection of AI-likeness, we track stylistic changes in writing and link them to citation counts, journal placement, and global citation flows before and after ChatGPT. Our findings reveal uneven outcomes: authors in the Global East adopt AI tools more aggressively, yet Western authors gain more per unit of adoption due to pre-existing penalties for "humanlike" writing. Prestigious journals continue to privilege more human-sounding texts, creating tensions between visibility and gatekeeping. Network analyses show modest increases in Eastern visibility and tighter intra-regional clustering, but little structural integration overall. These results highlight how AI adoption reconfigures the labor of academic writing and reshapes opportunities for recognition.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, "Who Gets Seen in the Age of AI? Adoption Patterns of Large Language Models in Scholarly Writing and Citation Outcomes":

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the impact of AI-assisted writing tools, specifically Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT, on scholarly visibility across different regions.  The authors analyze a large dataset of computer science articles to track stylistic changes indicative of AI use, linking these changes to citation counts, journal placement, and citation networks.  The study reveals that while authors in the Global East are adopting AI tools more aggressively, Western authors still benefit more per adoption due to existing biases.  Prestigious journals still favor "human-like" writing, creating tensions.  Network analysis shows limited structural integration despite some increased Eastern visibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Relevant and Timely Research Question:** The paper tackles a crucial and timely question concerning the evolving landscape of academic writing in the age of AI. The study addresses concerns about equity and access within the scholarly communication.
    *   **Large-Scale Data Analysis:** The use of a substantial dataset (over 230,000 Scopus-indexed articles) provides a solid foundation for empirical analysis.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The authors employ a range of statistical methods, including zero-shot AI-likeness detection, ZINB regression models, and network analysis, providing a multifaceted approach to the problem. They take care to address potential issues of endogeneity.
    *   **Nuanced Findings:** The paper avoids simplistic conclusions, revealing complex and often contradictory trends. The study highlights that, while AI can potentially equalize opportunities, existing power structures can still perpetuate inequalities.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** The paper comprehensively analyzes the issue from the different angles of authorship region, GPT version, journal ranking, and citation.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on AI-Likeness Detection:**  The accuracy and validity of using stylistic "AI-likeness" as a proxy for actual AI use are somewhat questionable.  While the authors employ a zero-shot detection method, it's not foolproof. Articles with highly stylistic human writing can be misidentified as Al generated, and the detection tool could be biased.
    *   **Focus on Computer Science:** The study's focus solely on computer science limits the generalizability of the findings to other disciplines.  AI adoption patterns and editorial preferences may vary significantly across different fields.
    *   **Causality Concerns:** While the authors acknowledge and address endogeneity issues, establishing definitive causal relationships remains challenging. Other unobserved factors could be influencing the observed trends.  Instrumental variable regressions are suggested but not employed.
    *   **Network Analysis Limitations:**  The country-level network analysis may mask finer-grained dynamics at the individual author or institutional level.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a substantial contribution by going beyond simply measuring the prevalence of LLMs in academic writing and assessing the socio-economic impact of it.
    *   The work highlights how editorial preferences may be misaligned with equitable access for all authorship regions.
    *   The finding that existing systemic inequalities could perpetuate the advantage of Global West authorship, even with the stylistic influence of Generative AI, is critical.
    *   The authors employ rigorous analyses to identify potential causal connections.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper is a well-executed and insightful analysis of a complex and evolving issue.  While limitations exist, the study's strengths, especially its large-scale data, rigorous methodology, and nuanced findings, make it a significant contribution to the field.  The paper offers valuable empirical evidence to inform discussions about AI's role in shaping academic communication and its potential impact on equity and access.  The findings have practical implications for journals, researchers, and policymakers alike. However, the reliance on potentially biased AI-likeness detection methods and the single-discipline focus slightly detracts from the strength.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Accelerating Reinforcement Learning Algorithms Convergence using Pre-trained Large Language Models as Tutors With Advice Reusing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08329v1)
- **Authors**: Lukas Toral, Teddy Lazebnik
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms often require long training to become useful, especially in complex environments with sparse rewards. While techniques like reward shaping and curriculum learning exist to accelerate training, these are often extremely specific and require the developer's professionalism and dedicated expertise in the problem's domain. Tackling this challenge, in this study, we explore the effectiveness of pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) as tutors in a student-teacher architecture with RL algorithms, hypothesizing that LLM-generated guidance allows for faster convergence. In particular, we explore the effectiveness of reusing the LLM's advice on the RL's convergence dynamics. Through an extensive empirical examination, which included 54 configurations, varying the RL algorithm (DQN, PPO, A2C), LLM tutor (Llama, Vicuna, DeepSeek), and environment (Blackjack, Snake, Connect Four), our results demonstrate that LLM tutoring significantly accelerates RL convergence while maintaining comparable optimal performance. Furthermore, the advice reuse mechanism shows a further improvement in training duration but also results in less stable convergence dynamics. Our findings suggest that LLM tutoring generally improves convergence, and its effectiveness is sensitive to the specific task, RL algorithm, and LLM model combination.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using pre-trained Large Language Models (LLMs) as tutors to accelerate the convergence of Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms. The authors propose a student-teacher architecture where an RL agent (the student) receives advice from an LLM (the teacher) to guide its learning process. They also investigate reusing previously generated LLM advice to further improve training efficiency. They conduct experiments across three RL environments (Blackjack, Connect Four, and Snake) using DQN, PPO, and A2C algorithms, paired with three open-source LLMs (DeepSeek-R1, Llama3.1, and Vicuna). The results indicate that LLM tutoring can significantly speed up RL convergence while maintaining competitive performance. However, the effectiveness varies based on the specific task, RL algorithm, and LLM combination, and the advice reuse mechanism introduces some instability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined problem and motivation:** Addressing the slow convergence of RL algorithms is a significant challenge. Using LLMs as tutors is a promising approach.
*   **Comprehensive experimentation:** The authors conduct a thorough empirical evaluation, covering multiple environments, RL algorithms, and LLMs. This provides a reasonably robust assessment of the proposed approach. The inclusion of the advice reuse mechanism is a sensible extension.
*   **Clear presentation:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow. The methodology is clearly explained, and the results are presented concisely with tables and figures. The limitations are acknowledged, which is a sign of academic integrity.
*   **Relevant LLM integration:** By integrating LLM-suggested actions directly into RL policy and adapting the training compatible to it, the authors explore a simple but direct integration methodology. This direct integration approach allows to extract insights that may be lost in a more complex integration.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty:** The concept of using LLMs as tutors for RL agents is not entirely novel (the authors themselves acknowledge previous work).  The novelty lies primarily in the specific architecture and the advice reuse mechanism, but the incremental improvement from reuse is not groundbreaking.
*   **Lack of Theoretical Foundation:** The paper is primarily empirical. It lacks a theoretical analysis of why LLM tutoring works and how the different factors interact. This makes it difficult to generalize the results or predict the performance on new environments or RL algorithms.
*   **Limited Exploration of Prompt Engineering:** While prompt engineering is mentioned, the paper provides limited details on the specific prompts used and how they were optimized. This is a crucial aspect of LLM-based approaches, and a more in-depth discussion would be beneficial.
*   **Computational Constraints and Generalizability:** The experiments were conducted with limited GPU memory, potentially affecting the performance of larger LLMs. Also, the choice of environments (simple games) raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to more complex, real-world scenarios. It is possible the benefit of large language model tutoring decreases with increasing complexity of environment and RL algorithm. The claim that a direct proportional relationship between LLM size and performance exists could be misleading due to the small number of LLMs being studied.
*   **Statistical Significance:** The weak correlation between LLM size and its tutoring effectiveness is not statistically significant, which undermines the analysis.

**Significance:**

The paper contributes to the growing body of work on integrating LLMs with RL. It provides empirical evidence that LLMs can accelerate RL convergence. The advice reuse mechanism, while not a dramatic improvement, is a valuable addition to the architecture. The findings can inform the design of future RL systems that leverage LLMs. By providing a simple framework, the authors encourage for future works with more complex approaches for LLM-RL integration.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper provides valuable empirical insights into LLM-assisted RL but suffers from a lack of significant novelty and a weak theoretical analysis. The experiments are thorough, but the generalizability of the results is limited by the simple environments and computational constraints. The statistically insignificant correlation is also a drawback. While the advice reuse mechanism is a sensible addition, it does not represent a major breakthrough. The existing literature's limitations lead to the current work's potential significance; however, these same limitations constrain the score assigned to this work. However, the paper builds upon existing ideas and demonstrates how LLMs can be effectively used for RL, therefore it warrants a score above 5, but not significantly higher.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Physics-Guided Rectified Flow for Low-light RAW Image Enhancement
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08330v1)
- **Authors**: Juntai Zeng
- **Abstract**: Enhancing RAW images captured under low light conditions is a challenging task. Recent deep learning based RAW enhancement methods have shifted from using real paired data to relying on synthetic datasets. These synthetic datasets are typically generated by physically modeling sensor noise, but existing approaches often consider only additive noise, ignore multiplicative components, and rely on global calibration that overlooks pixel level manufacturing variations. As a result, such methods struggle to accurately reproduce real sensor noise. To address these limitations, this paper derives a noise model from the physical noise generation mechanisms that occur under low illumination and proposes a novel composite model that integrates both additive and multiplicative noise. To solve the model, we introduce a physics based per pixel noise simulation and calibration scheme that estimates and synthesizes noise for each individual pixel, thereby overcoming the restrictions of traditional global calibration and capturing spatial noise variations induced by microscopic CMOS manufacturing differences. Motivated by the strong performance of rectified flow methods in image generation and processing, we further combine the physics-based noise synthesis with a rectified flow generative framework and present PGRF a physics-guided rectified flow framework for low light image enhancement. PGRF leverages the ability of rectified flows to model complex data distributions and uses physical guidance to steer the generation toward the desired clean image. To validate the effectiveness of the proposed model, we established the LLID dataset, an indoor low light benchmark captured with the Sony A7S II camera. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves significant improvements in low light RAW image enhancement.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PGRF: Physics-Guided Rectified Flow for Low-light RAW Image Enhancement":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the challenging problem of low-light RAW image enhancement by focusing on improving the accuracy of noise modeling in synthetic training data. The authors propose a composite noise model that integrates both additive and multiplicative noise components, reflecting a more accurate representation of the physical noise generation mechanisms in image sensors. They introduce a novel per-pixel calibration scheme to capture spatial non-uniformity in noise characteristics due to microscopic variations in CMOS manufacturing, improving the consistency between synthetic and real noise distributions. Furthermore, the authors integrate this refined noise model with a Rectified Flow generative framework, creating PGRF (Physics-Guided Rectified Flow). By introducing physics-based conditional control, PGRF effectively constrains the inherent stochasticity of generative models, allowing for efficient and high-quality low-light image enhancement. To validate their approach, they introduce a new indoor low-light dataset, LLID, captured with a Sony A7S2 camera. Experimental results demonstrate the proposed framework's superior performance and generalization compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper exhibits significant novelty in its approach to low-light RAW image enhancement.
    *   The composite noise model that includes multiplicative components and the per-pixel noise calibration scheme is a valuable contribution, addressing limitations in existing physics-based noise models.
    *   The integration of a refined noise model with the Rectified Flow generative framework (PGRF) is also a novel approach, leveraging the strengths of generative models while maintaining control through physical priors.
    *   The LLID dataset serves as a valuable contribution to the research community.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a meaningful contribution to the field of low-light image enhancement.
    *   The improved noise modeling leads to more realistic synthetic training data, which is crucial for the effectiveness of deep learning-based enhancement methods.
    *   PGRF offers a promising alternative to existing methods, achieving both high-quality results and improved efficiency.
    *   The experimental results on the LLID dataset demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach and provide a benchmark for future research.
    *   The insights into noise behavior at the pixel level due to CMOS variations are significant for sensor characterization and potentially for improving sensor design.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper provides a thorough analysis of the physical noise generation mechanisms in image sensors, forming a solid foundation for the proposed noise model.
    *   The per-pixel noise calibration scheme is well-motivated and addresses a critical limitation of global calibration approaches.
    *   The integration of the noise model with Rectified Flow is a creative and effective approach, leveraging the strengths of both.
    *   The LLID dataset provides a valuable resource for evaluating low-light image enhancement algorithms.
    *   The experimental results are comprehensive and clearly demonstrate the superiority of the proposed approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational complexity of the per-pixel noise calibration scheme.
    *   While the LLID dataset is a valuable contribution, it is relatively small. A larger and more diverse dataset would further strengthen the validation of the proposed approach.
    *   The paper makes the implicit assumption that the long-exposure images in SID are noise-free, this might not be correct and should be discussed in the limitations.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of low-light image enhancement by:
    *   Providing a more accurate and realistic approach to noise modeling in synthetic training data.
    *   Offering a new generative framework that combines the strengths of physics-based modeling and Rectified Flow.
    *   Serving as a benchmark for future research in the field.
    *   Potentially informing sensor design to reduce noise due to pixel variations.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant advancement in the field of low-light RAW image enhancement by tackling the critical issue of inaccurate noise modeling in synthetic training data. The proposed composite noise model, per-pixel calibration scheme, and integration with Rectified Flow are all novel and well-motivated contributions. The LLID dataset provides a valuable resource for the research community. While there are some minor limitations, such as a relatively small LLID dataset, the strengths of the paper far outweigh its weaknesses. Overall, this paper represents a valuable contribution to the field and is likely to have a significant impact on future research.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Accelerating Mixture-of-Expert Inference with Adaptive Expert Split Mechanism
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08342v1)
- **Authors**: Jiaming Yan, Jianchun Liu, Hongli Xu, Liusheng Huang
- **Abstract**: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) has emerged as a promising architecture for modern large language models (LLMs). However, massive parameters impose heavy GPU memory (i.e., VRAM) demands, hindering the widespread adoption of MoE LLMs. Offloading the expert parameters to CPU RAM offers an effective way to alleviate the VRAM requirements for MoE inference. Existing approaches typically cache a small subset of experts in VRAM and dynamically prefetch experts from RAM during inference, leading to significant degradation in inference speed due to the poor cache hit rate and substantial expert loading latency. In this work, we propose MoEpic, an efficient MoE inference system with a novel expert split mechanism. Specifically, each expert is vertically divided into two segments: top and bottom. MoEpic caches the top segment of hot experts, so that more experts will be stored under the limited VRAM budget, thereby improving the cache hit rate. During each layer's inference, MoEpic predicts and prefetches the activated experts for the next layer. Since the top segments of cached experts are exempt from fetching, the loading time is reduced, which allows efficient transfer-computation overlap. Nevertheless, the performance of MoEpic critically depends on the cache configuration (i.e., each layer's VRAM budget and expert split ratio). To this end, we propose a divide-and-conquer algorithm based on fixed-point iteration for adaptive cache configuration. Extensive experiments on popular MoE LLMs demonstrate that MoEpic can save about half of the GPU cost, while lowering the inference latency by about 37.51%-65.73% compared to the baselines.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Accelerating Mixture-of-Expert Inference with Adaptive Expert Split Mechanism" introduces MoEpic, a novel MoE inference system designed to reduce the VRAM requirements and accelerate inference speed on resource-constrained devices. MoEpic employs an expert split mechanism, dividing each expert into top and bottom segments. The top segment of hot experts is cached in VRAM to increase the cache hit rate, while the bottom segments are prefetched from RAM during inference to reduce loading latency. A divide-and-conquer algorithm based on fixed-point iteration is proposed for adaptive cache configuration (VRAM budget and split ratio) per layer. Experiments on popular MoE LLMs demonstrate that MoEpic can reduce GPU costs and inference latency compared to existing baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The expert split mechanism is a reasonably novel approach. Prior work has focused on either caching full experts or prefetching. This method combines caching and prefetching in a hierarchical manner, potentially leveraging the strengths of both approaches. The adaptive cache configuration algorithm also adds to the novelty, attempting to optimize resource allocation based on hardware and model characteristics. The LCP cache priority is, however, less innovative, simply combining LFU and LRU through a weighted average, so while useful, has limited novelty.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical problem in deploying MoE models: high VRAM requirements. Lowering the VRAM footprint can significantly broaden the accessibility of these models. If MoEpic's performance claims hold up under broader scrutiny and in diverse real-world scenarios, it could enable more widespread adoption of MoE LLMs, especially in on-premises deployments. The reported latency improvements are also significant, directly impacting the usability and QoS of MoE-based applications.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:**  The paper tackles a clearly defined and significant problem in the LLM deployment space.
    *   **Technical Approach:** The expert split mechanism and adaptive cache configuration algorithm are sound in principle. The fixed-point iteration approach offers a practical way to approximate the optimal configuration.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:**  The experiments are reasonably comprehensive, comparing MoEpic against multiple baselines on popular MoE models (Qwen and Mixtral).  The ablation study helps to understand the contribution of individual components.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the approach and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability Claims:** While the experiments are performed on Qwen1.5 and Mistral-8x7B, these represent just two models in a rapidly evolving landscape.  It's unclear how well MoEpic would generalize to other MoE architectures, different expert sizes, or larger models. The authors haven't sufficiently demonstrated that MoEpic adapts well to different MoE architectures.
    *   **Parameter Sensitivity:** Though they perform a limited sensitivity analysis, the method has several parameters that require fine-tuning (split ratio, decay rate, allocation granularity). Parameter tuning can be computationally expensive and require substantial human effort to do effectively, making real-world implementation complex. A sensitivity analysis should be performed against these parameters and a procedure provided to facilitate parameter selection.
    *   **Hardware Specificity:** The performance evaluation is performed on a single hardware configuration (RTX A6000). Results might vary significantly with different GPUs, PCIe bandwidth, or CPU RAM speeds, limiting the immediate applicability. The benefit of the fixed-point iteration also needs to be demonstrably superior to a basic grid-search given that the fixed-point iteration would likely be compute intensive as well.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper focuses on inference performance, but ignores practical implementation hurdles, such as the complexity of modifying existing LLM frameworks to support the expert split mechanism or memory management overhead.

*   **Potential Influence:**  If MoEpic's benefits are proven robust, it could influence future designs for MoE inference systems, leading to more efficient and accessible LLM deployments. Further exploration of the concept of hierarchical expert caching could also be beneficial.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

MoEpic presents a novel approach to tackling a significant challenge in MoE LLM deployment. While the results are promising and the approach is technically sound, there are several limitations that prevent it from achieving a higher score. The limited generalizability claims and the potential impact of hardware specifics significantly restrict its immediate impact. These concerns, coupled with the tuning and potential complexities of integration, significantly reduces the score. If it could be demonstrated that these techniques could be scaled to different MoE architectures and different models, a higher score would be justified.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### <think> So let's replace this phrase with insult... </think> Lessons learned from generation of toxic texts with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08358v1)
- **Authors**: Sergey Pletenev, Daniil Moskovskiy, Alexander Panchenko
- **Abstract**: Modern Large Language Models (LLMs) are excellent at generating synthetic data. However, their performance in sensitive domains such as text detoxification has not received proper attention from the scientific community. This paper explores the possibility of using LLM-generated synthetic toxic data as an alternative to human-generated data for training models for detoxification. Using Llama 3 and Qwen activation-patched models, we generated synthetic toxic counterparts for neutral texts from ParaDetox and SST-2 datasets. Our experiments show that models fine-tuned on synthetic data consistently perform worse than those trained on human data, with a drop in performance of up to 30% in joint metrics. The root cause is identified as a critical lexical diversity gap: LLMs generate toxic content using a small, repetitive vocabulary of insults that fails to capture the nuances and variety of human toxicity. These findings highlight the limitations of current LLMs in this domain and emphasize the continued importance of diverse, human-annotated data for building robust detoxification systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) can generate suitable synthetic toxic data for training text detoxification models, serving as an alternative to human-annotated datasets. The authors use LLMs (Llama 3, Qwen) to generate toxic counterparts to neutral texts from the ParaDetox and SST-2 datasets. Their experiments show that models trained on synthetic data perform worse than those trained on human data, with a performance drop of up to 30% in joint metrics.  The primary reason for this underperformance is a "lexical diversity gap": LLMs generate toxic content using a narrow, repetitive vocabulary, failing to capture the breadth and nuance of human-generated toxic language. The paper concludes that relying solely on LLM-generated data for text detoxification is currently insufficient and highlights the continued importance of human annotation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Important and timely research question:**  Exploring the limitations of LLMs for tasks that require nuanced understanding of human expression, especially in sensitive domains like toxicity, is valuable.  The question of whether LLMs can *replace* humans in data annotation pipelines is increasingly relevant.
    *   **Well-defined methodology:** The paper has a clear and replicable methodology. It uses established datasets, fine-tunes standard detoxification models, and employs appropriate evaluation metrics (including human evaluation using GPT-4).
    *   **Clear identification of the problem:** The "lexical diversity gap" is a well-articulated and compelling explanation for the observed underperformance. The analysis of the frequency of toxic terms provides strong evidence for this claim.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper explores different LLMs and datasets, making the conclusions more robust.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors have made their code publically available.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited scope of diversity analysis:** While the paper identifies the 'lexical diversity gap,' it primarily focuses on the frequency of toxic *words*. The analysis could have been deeper by considering other aspects of diversity, such as the *types* of toxicity (e.g., hate speech, insults, threats), the *targets* of toxicity, and the *contextual* use of toxic language. A more multifaceted analysis of diversity would strengthen the paper's central argument.
    *   **Reliance on a limited set of LLMs:** While Llama 3 and Qwen are strong models, the conclusions might not generalize to all LLMs, especially those with explicit safety training or those designed for different purposes.
    *   **Limited exploration of mitigation strategies:** The paper clearly identifies the problem but only briefly suggests future research directions. Exploring techniques to enhance the diversity of LLM-generated toxic text (e.g., using different prompting strategies, augmenting the vocabulary, or using more sophisticated generation techniques) could make the paper more impactful.
    *   **Lack of comparison with other synthetic data generation methods:** It would have been good to compare the performance obtained with the LLM-generated synthetic data, with the performance obtained when other synthetic data generation methods are used to generate synthetic toxic data.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the limitations of current LLMs in generating high-quality, diverse toxic data for text detoxification. While the idea that LLMs might struggle with complex human language nuances is not entirely new, the paper provides empirical evidence in a specific and important application domain.
    *   The identification of the "lexical diversity gap" is a significant finding that sheds light on a key challenge in using LLMs for synthetic data generation in sensitive contexts. This finding has implications for other applications beyond text detoxification, where the diversity and realism of the generated data are critical.

**Overall Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, and taking a critical view of the contributions to the field, I would rate this paper a **7**.

**Justification:**

The paper addresses a timely and relevant research question, provides a well-defined methodology, and offers a compelling explanation for its findings. The empirical results are convincing, and the identification of the lexical diversity gap is a novel and significant contribution.

However, the paper's limited scope of diversity analysis and lack of exploration of mitigation strategies prevent it from achieving a higher score. Also, the authors could have compared their results with other studies, to see how good the models obtained by them performed, with respect to the model trained on data generated using other approaches. While the paper highlights an important limitation of LLMs, it does not offer a clear path toward overcoming this limitation, which reduces its overall impact on the field. Nevertheless, the paper provides valuable insights that will inform future research and development in text detoxification and synthetic data generation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Bitrate-Controlled Diffusion for Disentangling Motion and Content in Video
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08376v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Li, Qi Chen, Xiulian Peng, Kai Yu, Xie Chen, Yan Lu
- **Abstract**: We propose a novel and general framework to disentangle video data into its dynamic motion and static content components. Our proposed method is a self-supervised pipeline with less assumptions and inductive biases than previous works: it utilizes a transformer-based architecture to jointly generate flexible implicit features for frame-wise motion and clip-wise content, and incorporates a low-bitrate vector quantization as an information bottleneck to promote disentanglement and form a meaningful discrete motion space. The bitrate-controlled latent motion and content are used as conditional inputs to a denoising diffusion model to facilitate self-supervised representation learning. We validate our disentangled representation learning framework on real-world talking head videos with motion transfer and auto-regressive motion generation tasks. Furthermore, we also show that our method can generalize to other types of video data, such as pixel sprites of 2D cartoon characters. Our work presents a new perspective on self-supervised learning of disentangled video representations, contributing to the broader field of video analysis and generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents Bitrate-Controlled Diffusion (BCD), a novel self-supervised framework for disentangling motion and content in video data. BCD uses a transformer-based architecture with a low-bitrate vector quantization bottleneck to promote disentanglement and form a meaningful discrete motion space. A latent denoising diffusion model is used as a generative decoder, conditioned on the disentangled motion and content features, to facilitate self-supervised representation learning. The framework is validated on talking head videos and pixel sprites, demonstrating its effectiveness in motion transfer and auto-regressive motion generation. The authors minimize assumptions and task-specific inductive biases, aiming for a general-purpose approach to video disentanglement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the combination of several existing techniques into a unified framework for self-supervised video disentanglement. Specifically:

    *   **Transformer Architecture:** While using transformers for video processing is not entirely new, the way BCD jointly generates flexible implicit motion features and clip-wise content features within a transformer setting is noteworthy.

    *   **Low-Bitrate Vector Quantization:** Using a low-bitrate bottleneck (inspired by neural codec methods) to enforce disentanglement is a good approach, but the theoretical underpinnings regarding the bitrate as a disentanglement prior need to be strengthened. The insights could be more rigorous and directly linked to formal disentanglement criteria.

    *   **Latent Diffusion Model:** Using a latent diffusion model as a decoder is relatively common these days.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to advance the field of video analysis and generation by providing a more general and practical framework for disentangled video representations.

    *   **General-Purpose Approach:** Minimizing assumptions and task-specific biases makes the framework potentially applicable to a broader range of video datasets and tasks. This is a key advantage.

    *   **Real-World Application:** Demonstrating results on real-world talking head videos is important, as many existing disentanglement methods focus on simpler datasets or make unrealistic assumptions.

    *   **Evaluation:** Evaluation is reasonably thorough, including motion transfer, auto-regressive motion generation, and user studies. It provides convincing qualitative and quantitative evidence supporting the effectiveness of the proposed framework. But evaluation does not compare to SOTA model from compression field with similar target bitrate, making results unclear.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The design choices are well-motivated and generally lead to good empirical results.
    *   The results of the evaluation on real-world and synthetic data are good.
    *   Ablation studies provide some insights into the importance of the different components of the framework.
    *   Addresses ethical considerations appropriately.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The theoretical justification for using a low-bitrate bottleneck as a disentanglement prior could be improved. A more rigorous connection to formal disentanglement criteria would strengthen the paper.
    *   The paper should include more comparisons of model performance with other model from video compress field.
    *   The paper's title could be more descriptive.
    *   The performance comparison is not entirely convincing in places.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research in video disentanglement by demonstrating the effectiveness of combining transformers, low-bitrate bottlenecks, and latent diffusion models. The general-purpose nature of the framework could encourage its adoption in a wider range of applications.

*   **Detailed Rationale for the Score:**

I am assigning this paper a score of 6 because, while well-executed, it has limitations regarding theoretical justification and incremental technical advancement. The core innovation lies in the combination of existing techniques rather than a groundbreaking theoretical insight or novel architectural component. The claims of generality are reasonable based on the provided experiments, but the strength of novelty is not substantial enough to warrant a high score. The paper provides meaningful practical value and contributes to the field of video disentanglement, making the contribution noteworthy. However, the lack of a substantial theoretical or methodological leap holds it back from a higher score.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### LatentVoiceGrad: Nonparallel Voice Conversion with Latent Diffusion/Flow-Matching Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08379v1)
- **Authors**: Hirokazu Kameoka, Takuhiro Kaneko, Kou Tanaka, Yuto Kondo
- **Abstract**: Previously, we introduced VoiceGrad, a nonparallel voice conversion (VC) technique enabling mel-spectrogram conversion from source to target speakers using a score-based diffusion model. The concept involves training a score network to predict the gradient of the log density of mel-spectrograms from various speakers. VC is executed by iteratively adjusting an input mel-spectrogram until resembling the target speaker's. However, challenges persist: audio quality needs improvement, and conversion is slower compared to modern VC methods designed to operate at very high speeds. To address these, we introduce latent diffusion models into VoiceGrad, proposing an improved version with reverse diffusion in the autoencoder bottleneck. Additionally, we propose using a flow matching model as an alternative to the diffusion model to further speed up the conversion process without compromising the conversion quality. Experimental results show enhanced speech quality and accelerated conversion compared to the original.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper based on the provided text.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Latent VoiceGrad," an improved non-parallel voice conversion (VC) technique. It builds upon the existing VoiceGrad method, which uses a score-based diffusion model for mel-spectrogram conversion. Latent VoiceGrad addresses limitations of the original VoiceGrad by incorporating latent diffusion models within an autoencoder bottleneck.  The approach aims to speed up conversion and improve audio quality by operating in a lower-dimensional latent space. Additionally, the paper explores using a flow matching model as an alternative to the diffusion model for further acceleration. The experimental results demonstrate improved speech quality and faster conversion compared to the original VoiceGrad. The paper also explores the effect of using different input feature representations (mel-spectrograms, STFT spectrograms, and discrete acoustic token sequences), and investigates the effects of various training parameters. Subjective and objective evaluations confirm the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The novelty of this paper lies in the integration of latent diffusion models and flow matching models into the existing VoiceGrad framework for VC.  While the individual components (diffusion models, autoencoders, flow matching) are not entirely new in the context of generative modeling, their specific application *within* the VoiceGrad architecture for VC, particularly with the aim of speeding up conversion and improving quality, does present a novel combination.

The paper's exploration of various input feature representations (mel-spectrograms, STFT spectrograms, DAT sequences) and their impact on performance is also a valuable, although incremental, contribution.

**Significance:**

The significance of the paper stems from the potential to improve the efficiency and quality of non-parallel voice conversion. Addressing the slow conversion speed of diffusion-based VC methods is a critical step towards making them more practical for real-world applications. The demonstrated improvements in audio quality through the use of latent diffusion models are also significant.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of the original VoiceGrad (slow conversion, audio quality).
*   **Well-Defined Solutions:** The proposed solutions (latent diffusion, flow matching) are well-motivated and clearly explained.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experimental section is quite thorough, including both objective (pMOS, CER, SECS, RTF) and subjective (MOS tests) evaluations. The exploration of different input features and training parameters adds further value.
*   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper provides detailed analysis and comparison of the different approaches.
*   **Code Availability:** Mention of code availability improves reproducibility.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While the paper presents a novel combination of techniques, the improvement over the original VoiceGrad might be considered incremental. It is essentially building upon an existing method rather than presenting a radically new paradigm.
*   **Complexity:**  The integration of multiple components (autoencoders, diffusion models/flow matching, vocoders) adds complexity to the system. This can make it harder to train and optimize, as well as potentially limiting its practical applicability due to computational resource requirements.
*   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily conducted on a single dataset (VCTK), and while the cross-dataset experiment shows reasonable performance, further evaluation on more diverse datasets would strengthen the conclusions.
*   **Lack of Direct Comparison to More Recent SOTA:** The paper compares to its own previous method and to methods from a few years ago. Given the rapid advancements in VC, a more direct comparison to the very latest state-of-the-art non-parallel VC methods (beyond Diff-VC and FACodec) would be beneficial. Specifically, comparing to more recent methods that also explore fast DPM or flow-based generative modelling strategies is important to accurately gauge the paper's performance.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of voice conversion by demonstrating effective ways to improve the efficiency and quality of diffusion-based methods. The insights gained from the experiments with different input feature representations and training parameters could also be valuable to other researchers working in this area. This paper could motivate future research into combining autoencoders with generative models for voice conversion and other speech processing tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

Based on the above analysis, I assign a score of **7**. The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of voice conversion by addressing limitations of existing diffusion-based methods and proposing novel combinations of techniques. However, the incremental nature of the improvements, the complexity of the proposed system, and the limited scope of the experiments prevent it from achieving a higher score. A more thorough comparison to current cutting-edge methods and expansion of the experimental scope to include more diverse datasets and a more in-depth exploration into the trade-off between complexity and performance would have significantly elevated the paper's impact.

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 0/10

### Co-Investigator AI: The Rise of Agentic AI for Smarter, Trustworthy AML Compliance Narratives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08380v1)
- **Authors**: Prathamesh Vasudeo Naik, Naresh Kumar Dintakurthi, Zhanghao Hu, Yue Wang, Robby Qiu
- **Abstract**: Generating regulatorily compliant Suspicious Activity Report (SAR) remains a high-cost, low-scalability bottleneck in Anti-Money Laundering (AML) workflows. While large language models (LLMs) offer promising fluency, they suffer from factual hallucination, limited crime typology alignment, and poor explainability -- posing unacceptable risks in compliance-critical domains. This paper introduces Co-Investigator AI, an agentic framework optimized to produce Suspicious Activity Reports (SARs) significantly faster and with greater accuracy than traditional methods. Drawing inspiration from recent advances in autonomous agent architectures, such as the AI Co-Scientist, our approach integrates specialized agents for planning, crime type detection, external intelligence gathering, and compliance validation. The system features dynamic memory management, an AI-Privacy Guard layer for sensitive data handling, and a real-time validation agent employing the Agent-as-a-Judge paradigm to ensure continuous narrative quality assurance. Human investigators remain firmly in the loop, empowered to review and refine drafts in a collaborative workflow that blends AI efficiency with domain expertise. We demonstrate the versatility of Co-Investigator AI across a range of complex financial crime scenarios, highlighting its ability to streamline SAR drafting, align narratives with regulatory expectations, and enable compliance teams to focus on higher-order analytical work. This approach marks the beginning of a new era in compliance reporting -- bringing the transformative benefits of AI agents to the core of regulatory processes and paving the way for scalable, reliable, and transparent SAR generation.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've analyzed the provided text. Here's a summary and critical evaluation:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Co-Investigator AI," an agentic framework designed to improve the process of generating Suspicious Activity Reports (SARs) for Anti-Money Laundering (AML) compliance. It addresses the limitations of traditional manual methods and direct application of Large Language Models (LLMs), which often suffer from hallucinations, lack of explainability, and difficulty in aligning with specific crime typologies. The framework employs specialized AI agents for planning, crime type detection, external intelligence gathering, and compliance validation, all while maintaining human oversight.  It incorporates dynamic memory management, an AI-Privacy Guard for sensitive data handling, and a real-time validation agent. The paper presents empirical evidence and expert assessments to demonstrate the framework's efficiency and accuracy gains in SAR drafting, along with its ability to meet regulatory expectations. It also discusses a thorough experimentation to showcase the efficacy of Agentic AI compared to the failure modes of current state-of-the-art models (LLMs) in these real-world scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel agentic approach to SAR generation. While the individual components (LLMs, agent architectures, privacy measures) aren't entirely new, their integration into a purpose-built framework for AML compliance, with its emphasis on explainability, validation, and human-in-the-loop control, is a significant step forward. The AI-Privacy Guard layer and the Agent-as-a-Judge paradigm for real-time validation are particularly interesting and add to the novelty. Compared to a black-box LLM approach, the emphasis on transparent reasoning (Chain-of-Thought) and confidence scoring is commendable.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is highly significant. AML compliance is a costly and challenging area for financial institutions, and improvements in SAR generation can lead to significant cost savings, improved regulatory compliance, and better detection of financial crimes. The move away from monolithic LLM applications towards a modular, agentic design addresses key concerns about trust, reliability, and explainability in compliance-critical domains. The reported efficiency gains and accuracy improvements, as validated by domain experts, demonstrate the potential impact of the framework.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-structured agentic architecture with detailed descriptions of each component.

    *   Robust and detailed study on the shortcomings of state-of-the-art models.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper could benefit from a more detailed comparison against existing rule-based AML systems, highlighting the advantages of the AI-driven approach beyond just efficiency.

    *   The paper does not provide detailed information on the scalability of the system, which is a crucial consideration for real-world deployment across a wide range of institutions.
    *   Discussion on the computational overhead of running the multi-agent system could be included.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of AI-powered compliance tools. It demonstrates a practical and responsible approach to applying LLMs in a high-stakes domain, focusing on transparency, control, and human oversight. The agentic architecture and validation methods can serve as a blueprint for other compliance applications.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7.5**.
