### Low-Resource Fine-Tuning for Multi-Task Structured Information Extraction with a Billion-Parameter Instruction-Tuned Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08381v1)
- **Authors**: Yu Cheng Chih, Yong Hao Hou
- **Abstract**: Deploying large language models (LLMs) for structured data extraction in domains such as financial compliance reporting, legal document analytics, and multilingual knowledge base construction is often impractical for smaller teams due to the high cost of running large architectures and the difficulty of preparing large, high-quality datasets. Most recent instruction-tuning studies focus on seven-billion-parameter or larger models, leaving limited evidence on whether much smaller models can work reliably under low-resource, multi-task conditions. This work presents ETLCH, a billion-parameter LLaMA-based model fine-tuned with low-rank adaptation on only a few hundred to one thousand samples per task for JSON extraction, knowledge graph extraction, and named entity recognition. Despite its small scale, ETLCH outperforms strong baselines across most evaluation metrics, with substantial gains observed even at the lowest data scale. These findings demonstrate that well-tuned small models can deliver stable and accurate structured outputs at a fraction of the computational cost, enabling cost-effective and reliable information extraction pipelines in resource-constrained environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores low-resource fine-tuning strategies for multi-task structured information extraction using a 1B-parameter language model (LLM). The work presents ETLCH, a LLaMA-based model fine-tuned with low-rank adaptation (LoRA) on a limited number of samples (100-1000) per task. The tasks include JSON extraction, knowledge graph extraction (KGE), and named entity recognition (NER).  The authors demonstrate that despite its small size and limited training data, ETLCH outperforms stronger baselines across most evaluation metrics, particularly in JSON and KGE. The work highlights the potential of well-tuned small models for achieving stable and accurate structured outputs in resource-constrained environments, offering a cost-effective alternative to large LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a crucial gap in LLM research by focusing on low-resource settings, making LLMs accessible to SMEs and research teams with limited computational resources.
    *   **Multi-Task Learning:** The design of a multi-task training framework with JSON extraction, KGE, and NER not only enhances model performance but also aligns with real-world industrial reasoning needs. The paper provides compelling arguments regarding the benefits of a chained approach from NER to KGE to JSON.
    *   **Detailed Experimental Setup:** The paper provides a clear and reproducible experimental setup, detailing the model architecture, training parameters, and data annotation procedures.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The experimental results show that ETLCH outperforms the open-source baseline, demonstrating considerable improvements in JSON and KGE tasks, even at low data scales.
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The paper showcases the potential to have high data efficency with low-resource LLMs.
    *   **JSON Correctness** The paper focuses on JSON correctness, which is typically ignored.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Novelty in LoRA Application:** The use of LoRA for fine-tuning is not novel in itself. The novelty lies in its application to a small-scale LLM for multi-task learning.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Metrics:** While the paper uses ROUGE-L and Cosine Similarity, other evaluation metrics that take into consideration correctness of the output might be useful.
    *   **Dependency on ChatGPT for Data Generation:** The use of ChatGPT for data generation introduces a potential bias and limits the replicability of the results. The quality of the data directly impacts the training and evaluation, and using an external API introduces a layer of uncertainty.
    *   **Statistical Significance in NER:** The statistical significance results in the NER task are marginal, which suggests the model's performance is comparable to other models, especially Qwen.
    *   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** The paper primarily compares ETLCH against open-source baselines. It would be beneficial to compare it against other state-of-the-art low-resource fine-tuning techniques specifically designed for these tasks.
    *   **Chinese only training:** While the paper states that the model was only trained in Chinese, the implications of transfer learning to other languages might be discussed.

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The novelty primarily lies in the empirical demonstration that a carefully tuned small-scale LLM can achieve competitive performance in structured information extraction tasks under extreme resource constraints. This challenges the prevailing trend of relying on large models and offers a more sustainable and accessible approach. This has implications for small businesses with large document backlogs.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by encouraging researchers to explore more resource-efficient approaches to LLM fine-tuning. It could inspire new techniques for training small models and lead to wider adoption of LLMs in industries with limited resources.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has some significant limitations. It doesn't contain much novel methods or experiments; it is a mostly empirical validation that builds upon existing techniques. The work is not methodologically novel, and it has certain statistical limitations.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Efficient Decoding Methods for Language Models on Encrypted Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08383v1)
- **Authors**: Matan Avitan, Moran Baruch, Nir Drucker, Itamar Zimerman, Yoav Goldberg
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) power modern AI applications, but processing sensitive data on untrusted servers raises privacy concerns. Homomorphic encryption (HE) enables computation on encrypted data for secure inference. However, neural text generation requires decoding methods like argmax and sampling, which are non-polynomial and thus computationally expensive under encryption, creating a significant performance bottleneck. We introduce cutmax, an HE-friendly argmax algorithm that reduces ciphertext operations compared to prior methods, enabling practical greedy decoding under encryption. We also propose the first HE-compatible nucleus (top-p) sampling method, leveraging cutmax for efficient stochastic decoding with provable privacy guarantees. Both techniques are polynomial, supporting efficient inference in privacy-preserving settings. Moreover, their differentiability facilitates gradient-based sequence-level optimization as a polynomial alternative to straight-through estimators. We further provide strong theoretical guarantees for cutmax, proving it converges globally to a unique two-level fixed point, independent of the input values beyond the identity of the maximizer, which explains its rapid convergence in just a few iterations. Evaluations on realistic LLM outputs show latency reductions of 24x-35x over baselines, advancing secure text generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper tackles the challenge of efficient text generation using large language models (LLMs) in privacy-preserving settings, specifically using homomorphic encryption (HE). The core problem is that standard decoding methods like argmax and nucleus sampling are computationally expensive under HE because they rely on non-polynomial operations.  The authors introduce `CutMax`, an HE-friendly argmax algorithm that reduces ciphertext operations compared to previous methods. They also present the first HE-compatible nucleus (top-p) sampling method, leveraging `CutMax`.  The paper provides theoretical guarantees for `CutMax`, proving its global convergence, and demonstrates its differentiability, enabling gradient-based sequence-level optimization. Experimental results show significant latency reductions compared to baseline HE-based argmax implementations, making secure text generation more practical.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in several key areas. Firstly, the `CutMax` algorithm represents a significant departure from traditional comparison-based argmax implementations for HE. Removing comparisons altogether is a conceptually clean and pragmatically impactful contribution. The theoretical proof of convergence is also a strong addition. Secondly, the HE-compatible nucleus sampling method fills a crucial gap, as it allows for stochastic decoding in HE, which is important for generating more diverse and coherent text. The differentiability of the proposed methods also adds another layer of novelty, potentially enabling new training paradigms for privacy-preserving LLMs.  While polynomial approximations for HE are not entirely new, the specific application to argmax and nucleus sampling, coupled with the efficiency gains and theoretical guarantees, represent a non-trivial advance.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to advance the practical deployment of privacy-preserving LLMs. By significantly reducing the computational overhead of decoding, the paper lowers a major barrier to secure text generation. Differentiable HE-friendly primitives can facilitate more efficient training of private LLMs, potentially avoiding the need for computationally expensive straight-through estimators (STEs). The experimental results, demonstrating substantial latency reductions (24x-35x), underscore the practical relevance of the contributions. However, the experimental evaluation section lacks information regarding what type of model it was tested with, and it only tested for accuracy when choosing the best next token, but not necessarily the complete picture of HE-friendly text generation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of HE-based decoding and the limitations of existing methods.
    *   **Novel algorithms:**  `CutMax` and the HE-friendly nucleus sampling method offer innovative solutions with clear advantages over prior approaches.
    *   **Theoretical guarantees:** The convergence proof for `CutMax` strengthens the theoretical foundation of the approach.
    *   **Differentiability:** Differentiable decoding primitives provide potential to refine language generation capabilities with gradient-based methods without relying on STEs.
    *   **Experimental validation:** Empirical results demonstrate significant performance improvements, showcasing the practical value of the proposed techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Training Data:** It does not evaluate the long-run generation performance, and the study only measured for a single token accuracy, and does not account for multi-token sequences.
    *   **Dependence on CKKS:** While CKKS is a popular HE scheme, the techniques might require adaptation for other HE schemes.
    *   **Limited comparative benchmarking:** The Nexus comparison might not be a fair comparison due to its own inefficiency from SIGN approximations.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of privacy-preserving LLMs and could inspire further research into HE-friendly algorithms for various deep learning tasks. The demonstrated efficiency gains make HE-based text generation more feasible, potentially opening new application areas where privacy is paramount.

*   **Conclusion:** The paper makes significant contributions to the field of privacy-preserving LLMs by providing efficient and theoretically sound algorithms for decoding in HE settings. The reported performance gains and differentiability offer a path towards more practical and effective secure text generation.
    *  **Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates significant efficiency gains and provides a novel approach to argmax computation and sampling under HE, coupled with valuable theoretical properties. While the paper lacks comparative evaluations of the HE-based models in real-world scenarios, the proposed techniques present a significant step in enabling wider use of HE-based LLMs.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-Guided Ansätze Design for Quantum Circuit Born Machines in Financial Generative Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08385v1)
- **Authors**: Yaswitha Gujju, Romain Harang, Tetsuo Shibuya
- **Abstract**: Quantum generative modeling using quantum circuit Born machines (QCBMs) shows promising potential for practical quantum advantage. However, discovering ans\"atze that are both expressive and hardware-efficient remains a key challenge, particularly on noisy intermediate-scale quantum (NISQ) devices. In this work, we introduce a prompt-based framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to generate hardware-aware QCBM architectures. Prompts are conditioned on qubit connectivity, gate error rates, and hardware topology, while iterative feedback, including Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, circuit depth, and validity, is used to refine the circuits. We evaluate our method on a financial modeling task involving daily changes in Japanese government bond (JGB) interest rates. Our results show that the LLM-generated ans\"atze are significantly shallower and achieve superior generative performance compared to the standard baseline when executed on real IBM quantum hardware using 12 qubits. These findings demonstrate the practical utility of LLM-driven quantum architecture search and highlight a promising path toward robust, deployable generative models for near-term quantum devices.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to quantum circuit Born machine (QCBM) design using large language models (LLMs).  The authors propose a prompt-based framework where an LLM generates hardware-aware QCBM architectures. The LLM's prompts are conditioned on specific hardware characteristics (qubit connectivity, error rates, topology), and the resulting circuits are iteratively refined using feedback based on Kullback–Leibler (KL) divergence, circuit depth, and circuit validity.  The method is evaluated on a financial modeling task: generating daily changes in Japanese government bond (JGB) interest rates.  The results show that the LLM-generated circuits are shallower and perform better (lower KL divergence) than a standard `TwoLocal` ansatz when run on real IBM quantum hardware.  The paper argues this demonstrates the potential of LLMs for automated and hardware-aware quantum circuit design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to design quantum circuits is interesting and represents a developing area of research.  While other papers have explored LLM-driven quantum circuit design, this paper's focus on hardware-aware QCBM design, specifically with iterative refinement based on KL divergence and deployed on real quantum hardware, provides a more complete pipeline. Previous works referenced in the paper focus more on conceptual design and lack implementation on real hardware. This makes this paper relatively novel within the context of existing research on LLMs and quantum circuit design. However, some concerns might be raised about the true level of automation versus the level of manual prompt-engineering involved, which the paper does not fully clarify.

*   **Significance:**  The ability to automatically generate circuits tailored to specific quantum hardware is highly significant.  Current quantum computers are extremely sensitive to noise, and adapting circuit design to mitigate this noise is crucial. Successfully demonstrating that LLMs can produce circuits that outperform standard approaches on real hardware, even in a limited setting, represents a meaningful step forward.  The financial modeling task provides a concrete application, although the complexity of the chosen task is not particularly high.  If this approach could be scaled to more complex problems, it could greatly accelerate the development of quantum algorithms for near-term devices. A notable strength is the comparison of the LLM-generated circuits to the standard `TwoLocal` ansatz, showing both decreased depth *and* increased performance on real hardware after training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Hardware-Aware Design:** The explicit incorporation of hardware constraints into the LLM prompts is critical for producing usable circuits.
    *   **Iterative Refinement:** The feedback loop using KL divergence, circuit depth, and validity is essential for guiding the LLM towards optimal circuit designs.
    *   **Real Hardware Validation:**  Demonstrating improved performance on real quantum hardware is a major strength.
    *   **Concrete Application:** The focus on the JGB interest rate modeling task provides a concrete test case.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Complexity of the Application:**  The financial modeling task is relatively simple.
    *   **Lack of Detailed Comparison to other Circuit Design Methods:** The paper focuses heavily on TwoLocal comparison, it would have been good to test against other established quantum architectures or other LLM-driven architectures.
    *   **Scaling Concerns:** The LLM-based approach may face scalability issues as the number of qubits and circuit complexity increase. Prompt engineering and the overall iterative process could become more challenging.
    *   **Reproducibility and Generalizability:** The role of the specific prompts used in this work is very important to the result. The prompts are not explicitly given in the paper. This makes it difficult to reproduce the findings and raises some questions about generalizability without prompt engineering expertise.
    *   **Role of Error Mitigation:** The paper mentions error mitigation but it's not clear how sophisticated the error mitigation approach is. It may be necessary to improve the approach for the quantum advantage to be significant.
    *   **Automation Clarification:**  The paper needs to explicitly state the levels of prompt engineering performed for the LLM and what the process is, and the extent of the human's role in it.

*   **Potential Impact:** This paper could potentially shift the direction of research toward automated quantum algorithm design by integrating LLMs with hardware-aware constraints. However, this shift will be highly dependent on whether the approach can scale.

**Score: 7/10**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and interesting application of LLMs to quantum circuit design. The real hardware results are compelling, demonstrating the potential to generate hardware-aware circuits that perform better than standard architectures on noisy devices. However, the limited complexity of the problem, lack of other architectures comparisons, scaling concerns, transparency and reproducibility concerns through lack of information about the prompts/prompt engineering are significant weaknesses. If the approach could be shown to scale to more complex problems, the score would be higher. A score of 7 reflects the potential impact of the idea, tempered by the current limitations.

- **Classification**: quant-ph
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ubiquitous Intelligence Via Wireless Network-Driven LLMs Evolution
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08400v1)
- **Authors**: Xingkun Yin, Feiran You, Hongyang Du, Kaibin Huang
- **Abstract**: We introduce ubiquitous intelligence as a paradigm where Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve within wireless network-driven ecosystems. Unlike static model deployments, this approach enables scalable and continuous intelligence ascension through coordination between networks and LLMs. Wireless networks support system-orchestrated lifelong learning, while LLMs drive the next-generation network development that is more adaptive and responsive. This co-evolution highlights a shift toward self-improving systems, sustaining capability growth across diverse and resource-constrained environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "ubiquitous intelligence," a paradigm where Large Language Models (LLMs) and wireless networks co-evolve. It contrasts this approach with traditional cloud-centric LLM deployments, highlighting the limitations of the latter concerning latency, privacy, and personalization. The paper argues that wireless networks, by enabling distributed inference and continual learning closer to data sources, offer a path towards more responsive, private, and personalized LLM applications. It explores the synergistic evolution of LLMs and wireless networks, outlining how LLMs can benefit network optimization (e.g., context-aware scheduling, semantic compression) and how wireless networks can support the scaling and continuous learning of LLMs through experience scaling and adaptive task offloading. The paper concludes by identifying key challenges and opportunities, including scalable experience exchange, global model consistency, communication efficiency, security, and efficient knowledge representation. The paper outlines that shifting to ubiquitous intelligence requires a collaborative environment using dense access, edge computing, D2D communication, permeable semantic networking and AI for Network.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The concept of co-evolution between LLMs and wireless networks, particularly focusing on "experience scaling," presents a novel perspective. Existing research often treats LLMs and networks as separate entities. This paper makes explicit arguments and proposes a co-evolution approach and presents the challenges and opportunities of that approach. The idea of moving beyond human-generated data to environmental data using wireless connectivity to drive LLM advancements is also relatively novel.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses critical challenges in LLM deployment, particularly in resource-constrained and privacy-sensitive environments. The move toward edge intelligence is a crucial direction, and the paper's exploration of how wireless networks can facilitate this is significant. The identified challenges like "Scalable experience exchange" and "Global model consistency" are important areas of research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper effectively highlights the limitations of cloud-centric LLMs and the potential of wireless networks.
    *   **Comprehensive overview:** The paper provides a good overview of relevant advancements in both LLMs and wireless networks.
    *   **Well-structured argument:** The paper logically builds its argument for ubiquitous intelligence and outlines the key enabling technologies.
    *   **Identified challenges:**  The paper correctly outlines the practical issues of implementation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Lack of concrete examples:** The paper remains primarily conceptual. While it identifies opportunities, it lacks specific examples or simulations demonstrating the feasibility and benefits of its proposed approach. It is difficult to assess the practical value without some concrete experiments.
    *   **Limited quantitative analysis:**  The paper doesn't provide quantitative data (simulations, performance metrics) to support its claims. It would be stronger if it included some initial quantitative analysis to demonstrate the potential advantages of this approach.
    *   **High-level description:** The descriptions of key technical elements (e.g., "permeable semantic networking," "experience scaling") remain somewhat high-level and could benefit from further elaboration.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by highlighting the importance of co-designing LLMs and wireless networks. It could encourage researchers to explore new distributed learning algorithms, efficient communication protocols, and resource allocation strategies specifically tailored for LLMs in wireless environments.

*   **Overall:** This is a paper with an interesting and novel approach that has the potential to influence the research of LLMs and Wireless Networks. However, there's a notable absence of concrete examples and simulation data to test the ideas.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper has a solid conceptual framework and identifies an important research direction. However, the lack of concrete examples and quantitative analysis prevents it from being a more significant contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 6/10

### An Iterative LLM Framework for SIBT utilizing RAG-based Adaptive Weight Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08407v1)
- **Authors**: Zhuo Xiao, Qinglong Yao, Jingjing Wang, Fugen Zhou, Bo Liu, Haitao Sun, Zhe Ji, Yuliang Jiang, Junjie Wang, Qiuwen Wu
- **Abstract**: Seed implant brachytherapy (SIBT) is an effective cancer treatment modality; however, clinical planning often relies on manual adjustment of objective function weights, leading to inefficiencies and suboptimal results. This study proposes an adaptive weight optimization framework for SIBT planning, driven by large language models (LLMs). A locally deployed DeepSeek-R1 LLM is integrated with an automatic planning algorithm in an iterative loop. Starting with fixed weights, the LLM evaluates plan quality and recommends new weights in the next iteration. This process continues until convergence criteria are met, after which the LLM conducts a comprehensive evaluation to identify the optimal plan. A clinical knowledge base, constructed and queried via retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), enhances the model's domain-specific reasoning. The proposed method was validated on 23 patient cases, showing that the LLM-assisted approach produces plans that are comparable to or exceeding clinically approved and fixed-weight plans, in terms of dose homogeneity for the clinical target volume (CTV) and sparing of organs at risk (OARs). The study demonstrates the potential use of LLMs in SIBT planning automation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel framework for automating seed implant brachytherapy (SIBT) planning using a Large Language Model (LLM). The core idea is to iteratively refine the objective function weights within an optimization loop, guided by an LLM that evaluates plan quality and suggests weight adjustments.  A key component is a locally deployed, domain-specific Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) knowledge base that provides the LLM with the necessary clinical guidelines and planning rules, allowing it to make more informed decisions.  The authors demonstrate that this LLM-assisted approach achieves planning quality comparable to or exceeding that of clinical plans and fixed-weight optimization strategies, with improved OAR sparing and enhanced efficiency.  They validated their approach on a cohort of 23 head and neck cancer patient cases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The use of LLMs for automating SIBT planning, particularly with a local RAG knowledge base and an iterative refinement loop, represents a valuable and novel contribution. Previous work has explored LLMs in radiation therapy, but this is the first known study on LLMs in brachytherapy planning. The use of a local LLM + RAG is also a significant plus, addressing the data privacy concerns of using cloud-based API solutions.

*   **Significance:** The potential impact on the field is significant. SIBT planning is a complex, time-consuming process that relies heavily on planner expertise. Automating weight optimization can improve efficiency, reduce inter-operator variability, and potentially lead to better patient outcomes. The local deployment of the LLM and RAG system makes the approach more accessible and practical for clinical use.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of manual weight adjustment in SIBT planning and the need for automation.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework, including the LLM-driven evaluation, iterative refinement, and RAG-based knowledge integration, is well-designed and explained.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The comparative study against clinical and fixed-weight plans provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.  The inclusion of CTV and OAR dosimetry analysis, along with needle usage, paints a complete picture. The ablation study regarding RAG is also a strength.
    *   **Addressing Practical Concerns:** The emphasis on local deployment and computational efficiency makes the approach more clinically feasible.
    *   **Excellent presentation and writing.** The authors clearly explain the motivations, methods, experiments, and results of this work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** The validation is performed only on head and neck cancer SIBT cases. Further validation across different anatomical sites and treatment modalities is needed to demonstrate broader generalizability.
    *   **LLM Trustworthiness and Safety:** The reliance on an LLM for crucial planning decisions raises concerns about its reliability and safety. While the RAG module helps, further measures, such as human-in-the-loop validation, are necessary to mitigate potential risks.  It's also important to evaluate the consistency and reproducibility of the LLM's recommendations. A deeper analysis of the LLM's reasoning process (e.g., through explainable AI techniques) would be beneficial.
    *   **Benchmark Comparison:** While a clinical dataset is included for comparison, the comparison to a well established, non-AI optimization approach is not done.
    *   **Lack of Discussion on Failure Modes:** The paper doesn't thoroughly discuss potential failure modes of the LLM-guided optimization.  What happens when the LLM makes incorrect weight adjustment suggestions?  How robust is the system to noisy or incomplete data?
    *   **Limited Clinical Significance Analysis:** While demonstrating comparable or exceeding clinical plans, there is a lack of statistical analysis on if the differences are significant.
    *   **Details about hyper parameter tuning is not sufficiently discussed.** It should be explored what other values can be tuned to improve the study or make it more efficient.
    *   **Although the results are presented well, there are little visualizations to supplement the results. Visualizations of plans with the dosimetry, DVH, etc., would improve the impact of the work.**

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper is likely to inspire further research on the use of LLMs for radiotherapy planning automation. It can serve as a foundation for developing more sophisticated and clinically integrated LLM-based planning systems. Specifically, the idea of a local deployment is likely to influence researchers in countries or sites that are resource-restrained.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper offers a novel and clinically relevant contribution by applying LLMs to automate SIBT planning.  The framework's design, particularly the local RAG integration, addresses key practical and ethical concerns. The experimental results demonstrate promising performance. However, the limited generalizability, the need for more comprehensive safety validation, and the absence of a discussion on potential failure modes, justify a score of 7.5 rather than a higher mark. Further work is required to address these limitations and realize the full potential of the proposed approach in clinical practice. While clinically significant analysis of the results is not included, if it were, a higher score can be warranted. If the visualizations were added, it will improve the impact of the work and result in a higher score.

- **Classification**: physics.med-ph
- **Score**: 7/10

### AutoVeriFix: Automatically Correcting Errors and Enhancing Functional Correctness in LLM-Generated Verilog Code
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08416v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Tan, Xiangchen Meng, Zijun Jiang, Yangdi Lyu
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in generating software code for high-level programming languages such as Python and C++. However, their application to hardware description languages, such as Verilog, is challenging due to the scarcity of high-quality training data. Current approaches to Verilog code generation using LLMs often focus on syntactic correctness, resulting in code with functional errors. To address these challenges, we present AutoVeriFix, a novel Python-assisted two-stage framework designed to enhance the functional correctness of LLM-generated Verilog code. In the first stage, LLMs are employed to generate high-level Python reference models that define the intended circuit behavior. In the second stage, these Python models facilitate the creation of automated tests that guide the generation of Verilog RTL implementations. Simulation discrepancies between the reference model and the Verilog code are iteratively used to identify and correct errors, thereby improving the functional accuracy and reliability of the LLM-generated Verilog code. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach significantly outperforms existing state-of-the-art methods in improving the functional correctness of generated Verilog code.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary, critical evaluation, and a novelty score for the paper "AutoVeriFix: Automatically Correcting Errors and Enhancing Functional Correctness in LLM-Generated Verilog Code."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AutoVeriFix, a two-stage framework designed to improve the functional correctness of Verilog code generated by Large Language Models (LLMs). The first stage involves using an LLM to create a Python-based reference model from a hardware specification. This model serves as a "golden" standard for the intended circuit behavior. A testbench with high code coverage is then generated through iterative refinement and coverage analysis in Python. The second stage utilizes another LLM to generate Verilog RTL code based on the same hardware specification. This Verilog code is then validated against the Python reference model using the generated testbench. Discrepancies between the simulation results of the Verilog code and the reference model are fed back to the LLM for iterative correction and refinement. The paper presents experimental results demonstrating that AutoVeriFix significantly outperforms existing methods in enhancing the functional correctness of LLM-generated Verilog code, achieving high accuracy on several benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Approach:** The paper presents a novel two-stage framework integrating LLM-assisted Python reference model generation, automated testing, and feedback-driven Verilog code generation. This method specifically addresses the limitations of existing approaches that primarily focus on syntactic correctness, neglecting functional errors.
    *   **Leveraging LLM Strengths:** It smartly exploits LLMs' proven capabilities in generating high-quality Python code to define correct behavior and drive the Verilog generation and correction process. It tackles the lack of high-quality RTL code training data.
    *   **Iterative Feedback Mechanism:** The iterative feedback loop, driven by testbench discrepancies, allows the LLM to learn and improve code generation, leading to significant gains in functional correctness. This iterative improvement based on coverage analysis is strong.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough experimental evaluation using multiple benchmarks and comparing against various state-of-the-art commercial, open-source and domain-specific models. The use of *pass@k* and FPR metrics is appropriate. The reference model’s accuracy and the testbench coverage are also evaluated.
    *   **Significant Performance Improvement:** The results show a substantial performance increase compared to existing methods on various benchmarks, demonstrating the effectiveness of the AutoVeriFix framework.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The framework's success heavily relies on the quality and consistency of the LLMs used. Inconsistent or poor performance from the LLM could undermine the entire process. The chosen LLM should be justified further.
    *   **Coverage Limitations:** While the framework emphasizes testbench coverage, it acknowledges that achieving 100% coverage is challenging. There's a possibility of uncovered corner cases that can lead to functional errors despite passing testbench validation. While mentioned, the inability to guarantee complete functional correctness is significant, as hardware errors can be costly.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The iterative process involving LLM code generation, simulation, and feedback can be computationally expensive and time-consuming, especially for complex designs. There could be limitations to design size and complexity. More information should be provided on computational complexity.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The paper doesn't delve into optimizing the framework for specific hardware architectures or design constraints. This might limit its applicability in certain scenarios. It only mentions that their code will be open-sourced after publication, but does not provide access currently.
    *   **Generalization:** The chosen benchmarks may not completely reflect the diverse range of real-world hardware designs. More rigorous generalization studies should be performed.
    *   **Limited discussion on prompt engineering**. The effect of prompt engineering on the generated code correctness should be more explicitly addressed.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper tackles a crucial problem in the application of LLMs to hardware design – ensuring functional correctness.
    *   AutoVeriFix offers a practical and effective approach for bridging the gap between high-level hardware specifications and reliable Verilog RTL code.
    *   The framework can potentially accelerate the hardware design process by automating code generation and error correction, reducing the need for manual intervention.
    *   The work provides insights into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in hardware design, paving the way for future research and development in this area.

**Novelty Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper's novelty lies in its innovative combination of LLM-assisted Python reference model generation, automated testing, and iterative feedback to improve Verilog code functional correctness. While individual components like LLM-based code generation and automated testing are not entirely new, the specific integration and feedback mechanism demonstrate a substantial advancement over existing methods.

The significance is high because it directly addresses a major bottleneck in LLM-based hardware design – the difficulty of guaranteeing functional correctness. The demonstrated performance gains and the potential for accelerating the design process make this work highly relevant. However, the reliance on LLM quality and the limitations of testbench coverage prevent it from achieving a higher score. More discussion is required to prove how they reduce hallucination, particularly in state machines.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 8/10

### LD-ViCE: Latent Diffusion Model for Video Counterfactual Explanations
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08422v1)
- **Authors**: Payal Varshney, Adriano Lucieri, Christoph Balada, Sheraz Ahmed, Andreas Dengel
- **Abstract**: Video-based AI systems are increasingly adopted in safety-critical domains such as autonomous driving and healthcare. However, interpreting their decisions remains challenging due to the inherent spatiotemporal complexity of video data and the opacity of deep learning models. Existing explanation techniques often suffer from limited temporal coherence, insufficient robustness, and a lack of actionable causal insights. Current counterfactual explanation methods typically do not incorporate guidance from the target model, reducing semantic fidelity and practical utility. We introduce Latent Diffusion for Video Counterfactual Explanations (LD-ViCE), a novel framework designed to explain the behavior of video-based AI models. Compared to previous approaches, LD-ViCE reduces the computational costs of generating explanations by operating in latent space using a state-of-the-art diffusion model, while producing realistic and interpretable counterfactuals through an additional refinement step. Our experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of LD-ViCE across three diverse video datasets, including EchoNet-Dynamic (cardiac ultrasound), FERV39k (facial expression), and Something-Something V2 (action recognition). LD-ViCE outperforms a recent state-of-the-art method, achieving an increase in R2 score of up to 68% while reducing inference time by half. Qualitative analysis confirms that LD-ViCE generates semantically meaningful and temporally coherent explanations, offering valuable insights into the target model behavior. LD-ViCE represents a valuable step toward the trustworthy deployment of AI in safety-critical domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LD-VICE: Latent Diffusion Model for Video Counterfactual Explanations":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LD-VICE, a novel framework for generating counterfactual video explanations.  It aims to address limitations in existing methods, such as high computational costs, poor temporal coherence, and lack of explicit model guidance. LD-VICE leverages a latent diffusion model (LDM) to operate in a compressed latent space, reducing computational complexity and improving temporal consistency.  It incorporates feedback from the target video model during the generation process, ensuring semantic alignment and causal relevance.  The framework includes techniques like SmoothGrad for robust guidance and a denoising refinement step to mitigate artifacts.  The authors evaluate LD-VICE on three diverse video datasets (EchoNet-Dynamic, FERV39k, and Something-Something V2), demonstrating its effectiveness in generating realistic, interpretable, and temporally coherent counterfactual explanations. The method also exhibits significant computational efficiency compared to pixel-space methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its explicit combination of a latent diffusion model with classifier-guided generation for video counterfactual explanations. While prior work has used diffusion models for video manipulation and counterfactual generation, and classifier guidance has been applied in image-based settings, their combination within a *latent space* for *video* explanations is a distinct contribution. The introduction of SmoothGrad and the denoising artifact suppression module adds further, albeit incremental, innovation. The novelty hinges on integrating these existing ideas and adapting them into a working system that addresses the limitations of pre-existing video explanation methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of explainable AI (XAI), specifically for video-based models. Video is a complex data modality, and robust, efficient, and interpretable explanation techniques are crucial for trustworthy AI deployment in safety-critical domains. LD-VICE offers a promising solution that improves upon existing approaches in several key aspects:
    *   **Efficiency:** Operating in latent space significantly reduces computational costs, making the method more scalable to longer and higher-resolution videos.
    *   **Temporal Coherence:** By leveraging the temporal awareness of the LDM, LD-VICE generates more consistent and realistic counterfactuals than frame-by-frame methods.
    *   **Model Guidance:** Integrating feedback from the target model ensures that the generated explanations are causally relevant and aligned with the model's decision-making process.
    *   **Actionability:** The focus on counterfactuals offers insights into what minimal changes are necessary to alter the model's predictions, thereby facilitating more informed decision-making.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined problem statement and motivation.
    *   Clear and detailed explanation of the LD-VICE framework.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation across diverse datasets.
    *   Quantitative and qualitative results demonstrating improved performance compared to existing methods.
    *   Ablation studies that validate the effectiveness of individual components.
    *   Good writing and overall presentation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The significance relies heavily on integrating ideas that already exist in separate areas (LDMs, classifier guidance, SmoothGrad). While their combination is well-executed, it reduces the conceptual leap.
    *   The subjective nature of qualitative evaluation is a potential weakness, particularly the interpretability assessment. Expert validation (e.g. medical professionals for EchoNet-Dynamics) would strengthen the claims.
    *   The performance on Something-Something V2, while mentioned, is less impressive, suggesting that the framework might not generalize equally well to all types of video data, particularly those with subtle and complex action patterns. The discussion of this dataset and possible limitations could be more detailed.
    *   The paper could benefit from a more rigorous discussion of the limitations of the evaluation metrics used, especially frame-wise metrics that might not fully capture the temporal aspects of video explanations. Addressing concerns regarding metrics like LPIPS not being temporally aware would add to the rigor.
    *   The paper doesn't explore or mention how to find the best counterfactual *prompt c* for the LDM model, a crucial ingredient to generate accurate counterfactuals, leaving as an unaddressed question if their method is prone to prompt-engineering.
    *   There is no discussion or experimentation on different text encoders used.
    *   Lack of discussion about the potential bias in the datasets used for training and evaluation and the impact on the fairness and reliability of the generated counterfactuals.

*   **Potential Influence:** LD-VICE has the potential to influence future research in video XAI by demonstrating the benefits of latent diffusion models and classifier-guided generation. It can inspire the development of more efficient, interpretable, and actionable explanation techniques for video-based AI systems. The framework can also be adapted to other domains and modalities beyond video.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

The paper presents a significant contribution by effectively combining existing techniques in a novel way to address a challenging problem in video XAI. The computational efficiency, temporal coherence, and model guidance offered by LD-VICE are substantial improvements over previous approaches. While the novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking, the thorough evaluation and demonstrated performance gains warrant a positive assessment.  The limitations discussed, such as a lack of discussion about datasets' bias, slightly less generalizability across datasets and the need for expert validation, prevent a higher score. A score of 7 reflects the paper's valuable contribution to the field while acknowledging its areas for improvement and dependence on existing methods. Addressing the limitations in future work could increase its long-term impact.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### PegasusFlow: Parallel Rolling-Denoising Score Sampling for Robot Diffusion Planner Flow Matching
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08435v1)
- **Authors**: Lei Ye, Haibo Gao, Peng Xu, Zhelin Zhang, Junqi Shan, Ao Zhang, Wei Zhang, Ruyi Zhou, Zongquan Deng, Liang Ding
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models offer powerful generative capabilities for robot trajectory planning, yet their practical deployment on robots is hindered by a critical bottleneck: a reliance on imitation learning from expert demonstrations. This paradigm is often impractical for specialized robots where data is scarce and creates an inefficient, theoretically suboptimal training pipeline. To overcome this, we introduce PegasusFlow, a hierarchical rolling-denoising framework that enables direct and parallel sampling of trajectory score gradients from environmental interaction, completely bypassing the need for expert data. Our core innovation is a novel sampling algorithm, Weighted Basis Function Optimization (WBFO), which leverages spline basis representations to achieve superior sample efficiency and faster convergence compared to traditional methods like MPPI. The framework is embedded within a scalable, asynchronous parallel simulation architecture that supports massively parallel rollouts for efficient data collection. Extensive experiments on trajectory optimization and robotic navigation tasks demonstrate that our approach, particularly Action-Value WBFO (AVWBFO) combined with a reinforcement learning warm-start, significantly outperforms baselines. In a challenging barrier-crossing task, our method achieved a 100% success rate and was 18% faster than the next-best method, validating its effectiveness for complex terrain locomotion planning. https://masteryip.github.io/pegasusflow.github.io/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the PegasusFlow paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PegasusFlow, a novel hierarchical rolling-denoising framework designed to overcome the limitations of diffusion models for robot trajectory planning, specifically the reliance on expert demonstrations.  PegasusFlow enables direct and parallel sampling of trajectory score gradients from environmental interaction, effectively bypassing the need for expert data.  The core of PegasusFlow is the Weighted Basis Function Optimization (WBFO) algorithm, a sampling technique that utilizes spline basis representations for better sample efficiency and faster convergence compared to Model Predictive Path Integral (MPPI) methods. The framework is built on a scalable, asynchronous parallel simulation architecture using IsaacGym, allowing for massively parallel rollouts for efficient data collection. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of PegasusFlow on trajectory optimization and robot navigation tasks, showing improved performance over baselines, particularly in a challenging barrier-crossing task. They also introduce a technique based on reinforcement learning to warm-start the search.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces several novel components. The most significant is the WBFO algorithm which looks like a smart way to better reuse samples, using some low-dimensional basis to project down the search space, and hence accelerate convergence. Using ideas from MPC such as warm starting and batch simulations is not a breakthrough, but the combination together does allow a new framework for diffusion methods.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in applying diffusion models to robotics: the need for large, high-quality datasets of expert demonstrations, especially for complex robotic systems.  By enabling direct score gradient estimation from environmental interaction, PegasusFlow has the potential to unlock the power of diffusion models for scenarios where expert data is scarce or difficult to obtain. The experimental results, particularly the barrier-crossing task, demonstrate a practical improvement in performance compared to existing methods.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies the bottleneck of expert data dependence in diffusion model training for robotics.
    *   **Novel Algorithm:** The WBFO algorithm appears to be a significant contribution to improving the sample efficiency of score gradient estimation.
    *   **Parallel Architecture:** The use of IsaacGym and an asynchronous parallel simulation architecture addresses the computational demands of direct score estimation.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of PegasusFlow across diverse tasks and robotic platforms. The detailed comparison against MPPI and the ablation studies provide strong evidence for the benefits of the proposed approach.
    *   **RL warm-starting provides a good prior to sample and can accelerate convergence**

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The system is complex. It is hard to imagine how this whole system can be deployed in real time, as many assumptions such as perfect sensors, perfect dynamics, and zero transfer errors between sim and real are necessary.

    *   **Clarity in some sections:** The details of the loss function in the objective function could have been explored more and better justified.

    *   **Limited Scope:** While the paper demonstrates the benefits of PegasusFlow in simulation, there is no discussion of the real-world applicability of the approach or the challenges of deploying it on physical robots.

*   **Potential Impact:** PegasusFlow has the potential to significantly impact the field of robot trajectory planning by reducing the reliance on expert data and enabling the application of diffusion models to a wider range of robotic systems. By addressing the expert data bottleneck, the paper paves the way for more autonomous and adaptable robots that can learn directly from their interactions with the environment.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

PegasusFlow is a novel and technically sound contribution to the field of robot trajectory planning. The introduction of the WBFO algorithm and the parallel simulation architecture addresses a significant challenge in applying diffusion models to robotics. The experimental results are strong, demonstrating a clear improvement over existing methods in simulation. Although the system is complex and the real-world applicability of the approach remains to be seen, the potential impact of PegasusFlow on the field is significant. Therefore, a score of 7 is given, which reflects the solid contributions of the paper but acknowledges its limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### Spherical Brownian Bridge Diffusion Models for Conditional Cortical Thickness Forecasting
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08442v1)
- **Authors**: Ivan Stoyanov, Fabian Bongratz, Christian Wachinger
- **Abstract**: Accurate forecasting of individualized, high-resolution cortical thickness (CTh) trajectories is essential for detecting subtle cortical changes, providing invaluable insights into neurodegenerative processes and facilitating earlier and more precise intervention strategies. However, CTh forecasting is a challenging task due to the intricate non-Euclidean geometry of the cerebral cortex and the need to integrate multi-modal data for subject-specific predictions. To address these challenges, we introduce the Spherical Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model (SBDM). Specifically, we propose a bidirectional conditional Brownian bridge diffusion process to forecast CTh trajectories at the vertex level of registered cortical surfaces. Our technical contribution includes a new denoising model, the conditional spherical U-Net (CoS-UNet), which combines spherical convolutions and dense cross-attention to integrate cortical surfaces and tabular conditions seamlessly. Compared to previous approaches, SBDM achieves significantly reduced prediction errors, as demonstrated by our experiments based on longitudinal datasets from the ADNI and OASIS. Additionally, we demonstrate SBDM's ability to generate individual factual and counterfactual CTh trajectories, offering a novel framework for exploring hypothetical scenarios of cortical development.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Spherical Brownian Bridge Diffusion Model (SBDM) for forecasting individualized cortical thickness (CTh) trajectories.  The core idea is to use a bi-directional conditional Brownian bridge diffusion process at the vertex level of registered cortical surfaces. A new denoising model, the conditional spherical U-Net (CoS-UNet), is proposed, which combines spherical convolutions and dense cross-attention to integrate cortical surfaces and tabular conditions. Experiments on ADNI and OASIS datasets demonstrate reduced prediction errors compared to existing methods. The paper also showcases the model's ability to generate factual and counterfactual CTh trajectories, potentially enabling exploration of hypothetical scenarios in cortical development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper has several notable novel aspects:

*   **Brownian Bridge Diffusion for CTh Prediction:**  Applying Brownian bridge diffusion models to CTh forecasting, especially at a fine-grained vertex level, is a fresh approach. Previous diffusion-based models in this area (like CTh-DDPM) operate at a coarser region level or use standard denoising diffusion probabilistic models (DDPMs), which don't explicitly model the relationship between a start and end point (baseline and future CTh). The bridge allows specifying that the forecasted CTh should be probabilistically related to a prior CTh measurement, making it a plausible assumption.
*   **CoS-UNet Architecture:**  The design of the CoS-UNet is significant. Combining spherical convolutions (appropriate for cortical surface geometry) with cross-attention (to incorporate tabular data like demographics and diagnoses) is a good architectural choice. While spherical U-Nets exist, their integration with cross-attention for conditional forecasting appears novel.
*   **Counterfactual Trajectory Generation:**  The ability to generate counterfactual CTh trajectories by conditioning on different diagnoses is a valuable contribution. It offers the potential to explore "what-if" scenarios and better understand the impact of different conditions on cortical development. This could lead to more insight into Alzheimer's progression.

*Significance:*

The paper's significance lies in the potential impact on:

*   **Improved Accuracy in CTh Forecasting:** The experiments demonstrate a reduction in prediction errors compared to state-of-the-art methods. Accurate CTh forecasting is crucial for early diagnosis, treatment optimization, and clinical trial design in neurodegenerative diseases.
*   **Understanding Disease Progression:** The ability to generate factual and counterfactual trajectories opens new avenues for studying the dynamics of cortical changes and the factors that influence disease progression. This can provide a deeper understanding of the underlying mechanisms of neurodegenerative diseases.
*   **Personalized Treatment Strategies:** More accurate and individualized CTh forecasting could facilitate the development of personalized treatment strategies tailored to an individual's specific disease trajectory.

*Strengths:*

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of the model on two different datasets (ADNI and OASIS) and includes comparisons with multiple baselines. The ablation study on the denoising model provides valuable insights into the importance of the proposed architecture.
*   **Sound Technical Approach:**  The method is well-motivated and grounded in established principles of diffusion models and spherical convolutional neural networks. The use of Brownian bridges is an appropriate choice for this task.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the method and experimental setup.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Analysis of Counterfactuals:**  While the paper demonstrates the ability to generate counterfactual trajectories, the analysis of these trajectories is somewhat limited.  A deeper investigation into the plausibility and interpretability of these trajectories would strengthen the paper. The paper acknowledges that assessing the realism of counterfactuals is inherently difficult.
*   **Reliance on FreeSurfer:**  The use of FreeSurfer for CTh extraction is standard, but it's important to acknowledge that the accuracy of the model is dependent on the quality of the FreeSurfer segmentations.
*   **Complexity of Implementation:** Diffusion models, in general, can be complex to implement and train.  This might limit the accessibility of the method to some researchers.

*Potential Influence:*

This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of neuroimaging and computational neuroscience. The proposed SBDM model provides a promising approach for forecasting CTh trajectories and generating counterfactual scenarios. The method has the potential to impact early diagnosis, treatment optimization, and clinical trial design in neurodegenerative diseases.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper.

*   The novelty is strong, with the creative application of Brownian bridge diffusion models and the development of the CoS-UNet architecture.
*   The significance is high, with the potential for improved accuracy in CTh forecasting and a deeper understanding of disease progression.
*   The evaluation is comprehensive, with thorough comparisons and ablation studies.
*   The limitations are relatively minor, with the main concern being the limited analysis of the counterfactual scenarios.
*   While diffusion models are increasingly popular, this is a particular instantiation on cortical thickness forecasting using well established and new techniques.

The paper represents a significant advance in the field and has the potential to inspire further research on diffusion-based models for neuroimaging analysis.
Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Adapting Vision-Language Models for Neutrino Event Classification in High-Energy Physics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08461v1)
- **Authors**: Dikshant Sagar, Kaiwen Yu, Alejandro Yankelevich, Jianming Bian, Pierre Baldi
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated their remarkable capacity to process and reason over structured and unstructured data modalities beyond natural language. In this work, we explore the applications of Vision Language Models (VLMs), specifically a fine-tuned variant of LLaMa 3.2, to the task of identifying neutrino interactions in pixelated detector data from high-energy physics (HEP) experiments. We benchmark this model against a state-of-the-art convolutional neural network (CNN) architecture, similar to those used in the NOvA and DUNE experiments, which have achieved high efficiency and purity in classifying electron and muon neutrino events. Our evaluation considers both the classification performance and interpretability of the model predictions. We find that VLMs can outperform CNNs, while also providing greater flexibility in integrating auxiliary textual or semantic information and offering more interpretable, reasoning-based predictions. This work highlights the potential of VLMs as a general-purpose backbone for physics event classification, due to their high performance, interpretability, and generalizability, which opens new avenues for integrating multimodal reasoning in experimental neutrino physics.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Vision-Language Models (VLMs), specifically fine-tuned LLaMA 3.2, to classify neutrino interactions in pixelated detector data from high-energy physics experiments. The authors compare the performance of VLMs against state-of-the-art Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), traditionally used in such experiments. They evaluate both the classification accuracy and the interpretability of the model predictions.  The results indicate that VLMs can outperform CNNs and provide greater flexibility in integrating auxiliary information, offering more interpretable, reasoning-based predictions. The paper concludes that VLMs have the potential to serve as general-purpose backbones for physics event classification, improving performance, interpretability, and generalizability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The application of VLMs to neutrino event classification is a relatively novel approach.  While machine learning has been extensively used in high-energy physics, leveraging the joint vision-language capabilities of models like LLaMA 3.2 to offer both strong classification *and* interpretable explanations is a step beyond traditional CNN-based methods. The use of QLoRA for efficient fine-tuning is also a useful implementation detail.

* **Significance:**  The significance lies in the potential to address the "black box" problem often associated with deep learning in physics.  The ability of VLMs to provide natural-language explanations for their classifications could increase trust and facilitate error diagnosis, leading to more robust and scientifically sound results. Furthermore, the ability to incorporate text descriptions and domain knowledge directly into the model alongside visual input holds promise for incorporating multimodal data streams and improving performance. The demonstration of performance with degraded image data demonstrates real-world applicability.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem statement and motivation:**  The paper clearly articulates the challenges of event classification in high-energy physics and the limitations of existing methods.
    * **Well-defined methodology:**  The paper provides a detailed description of the dataset, the VLM architecture, the fine-tuning process, and the evaluation metrics.  The inclusion of a CNN baseline for comparison is crucial.
    * **Strong results:** The results convincingly demonstrate that VLMs can outperform CNNs in this task, both in terms of accuracy and interpretability.
    * **Generalization study:** The generalization study demonstrates the ability to function even when image resolution is degraded.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Computational Cost:** The paper acknowledges the substantially higher computational cost of VLMs compared to CNNs. However, a more detailed analysis of the trade-offs between accuracy, interpretability, and computational resources could strengthen the paper.
    * **Limited explanation depth:** While VLMs provide textual explanations, the depth and accuracy of these explanations could be questioned. The paper mentions the explanations are *not* a perfect reflection of causal reasoning. There is room for improvement in generating more in-depth and reliable rationales. This is particularly important given the high stakes of scientific analysis.
    * **Narrow problem scope:** The paper focuses on a specific neutrino experiment simulation. While the results are promising, further work is needed to demonstrate the generalizability of VLMs to other types of detectors and physics problems.
    * **No evaluation of alternative explanation methods:** While the paper highlights the ability to produce text explanations, it would have been beneficial to compare against other explainable AI (XAI) techniques used on the CNN baseline (e.g., Grad-CAM) to provide a more holistic view of the benefit provided by the VLM approach.

* **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence the adoption of VLMs in high-energy physics and other scientific domains. It demonstrates the feasibility of using these models to address complex classification tasks and improve the interpretability of machine learning predictions. However, the significant computational cost may limit immediate adoption in resource-constrained environments. The demonstration of generalizability helps boost the potential.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution by exploring a novel and promising application of VLMs in high-energy physics. The results are compelling, and the discussion of interpretability is particularly valuable. However, the computational cost remains a significant challenge, and the study's scope is somewhat limited. Given these strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7 is warranted. The work provides a valuable proof-of-concept and motivates further research into the use of VLMs for scientific data analysis.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Acquiescence Bias in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08480v1)
- **Authors**: Daniel Braun
- **Abstract**: Acquiescence bias, i.e. the tendency of humans to agree with statements in surveys, independent of their actual beliefs, is well researched and documented. Since Large Language Models (LLMs) have been shown to be very influenceable by relatively small changes in input and are trained on human-generated data, it is reasonable to assume that they could show a similar tendency. We present a study investigating the presence of acquiescence bias in LLMs across different models, tasks, and languages (English, German, and Polish). Our results indicate that, contrary to humans, LLMs display a bias towards answering no, regardless of whether it indicates agreement or disagreement.
- **Summary**: This paper investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit acquiescence bias, the tendency to agree with statements regardless of content, a well-documented phenomenon in human surveys. The authors evaluated five LLMs of varying sizes across nine tasks in English, German, and Polish, adapting questions from A/B choices to yes/no formats. Contrary to human behavior, the results indicated that LLMs display a bias towards answering "no," irrespective of whether it signifies agreement or disagreement, particularly in English. The authors suggest this "no"-saying bias has implications for prompt design and LLM suitability for simulating human survey responses.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on acquiescence bias (or rather, the "no"-saying bias) in LLMs across multiple languages and tasks. While previous work (Tjuatja et al., 2024) touched on similar biases, this paper provides a more in-depth and extensive analysis, particularly by examining different phrasing variations. It also addresses a potential limitation in the previous study related to question rephrasing with negative constructs. However, the broader idea of cognitive biases in LLMs is not new, as many other works have explored various biases.

*   **Significance:** The findings are significant for several reasons. First, they challenge the assumption that LLMs might directly replicate human biases like acquiescence bias. Second, the discovery of a "no"-saying bias provides a counterintuitive insight into LLM response patterns, suggesting potential issues with prompt design and their ability to simulate human decision-making in survey contexts. This also highlights that LLMs do not necessarily have a systematic bias to just agree as previously thought and they might have biases in the other direction, where they tend to not agree at all. However, the study is limited to binary questions within the legal domain; generalizability to other question types and domains could be argued. Furthermore, the inconsistent results across the three languages suggests this "no-saying bias" is limited to English and more research is needed.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The experimental design is reasonably thorough, testing multiple models and languages with varied phrasing.
    *   The paper acknowledges limitations, such as the specific nature of the legal domain and the single prompt used per condition.
    *   The statistical analysis (McNemar's tests) adds robustness to the findings.
    *   Addresses a limitation of prior work by avoiding negative yes/no questions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on legal texts raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other domains.
    *   The prompts, while designed to be minimal, might still introduce unintended biases. The study could have benefited from an evaluation on the individual prompts used to see if the results change with different prompts.
    *   The lack of a clear pattern in German and Polish data weakens the overall claim of a universal "no"-saying bias and emphasizes the need for more cross-linguistic investigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper contributes to a growing body of knowledge on LLM biases and their potential implications. It could influence future research in prompt engineering, LLM evaluation, and the ethical use of LLMs for simulating human responses. The findings suggest a need for caution when using LLMs for survey-related tasks and underscore the importance of understanding and mitigating their inherent biases.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper has some novelty in its specific focus and extensive analysis of the "no"-saying bias across languages. It also contributes with the implication that the bias might indicate limits in LLM reasoning. However, the findings are not universally applicable (being stronger in English) and the reliance on legal text limits generalizability. The work is also related to prior studies on cognitive biases in LLMs but is more focused on this specific bias. The potential influence of this paper on the LLM understanding and mitigation effort is notable and could be useful for those prompt engineering and evaluating LLM in the future.
