- **Score**: 6/10

### Too Helpful, Too Harmless, Too Honest or Just Right?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08486v1)
- **Authors**: Gautam Siddharth Kashyap, Mark Dras, Usman Naseem
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit strong performance across a wide range of NLP tasks, yet aligning their outputs with the principles of Helpfulness, Harmlessness, and Honesty (HHH) remains a persistent challenge. Existing methods often optimize for individual alignment dimensions in isolation, leading to trade-offs and inconsistent behavior. While Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures offer modularity, they suffer from poorly calibrated routing, limiting their effectiveness in alignment tasks. We propose TrinityX, a modular alignment framework that incorporates a Mixture of Calibrated Experts (MoCaE) within the Transformer architecture. TrinityX leverages separately trained experts for each HHH dimension, integrating their outputs through a calibrated, task-adaptive routing mechanism that combines expert signals into a unified, alignment-aware representation. Extensive experiments on three standard alignment benchmarks-Alpaca (Helpfulness), BeaverTails (Harmlessness), and TruthfulQA (Honesty)-demonstrate that TrinityX outperforms strong baselines, achieving relative improvements of 32.5% in win rate, 33.9% in safety score, and 28.4% in truthfulness. In addition, TrinityX reduces memory usage and inference latency by over 40% compared to prior MoE-based approaches. Ablation studies highlight the importance of calibrated routing, and cross-model evaluations confirm TrinityX's generalization across diverse LLM backbones.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TrinityX, a modular alignment framework for Large Language Models (LLMs) designed to improve Helpfulness, Harmlessness, and Honesty (HHH).  TrinityX integrates a Mixture of Calibrated Experts (MoCaE) within the Transformer architecture.  Each expert is independently trained for one HHH dimension, and a calibrated, task-adaptive routing mechanism combines their outputs. The framework uses lightweight task vectors and entropy/KL divergence regularization.  Experiments on Alpaca, BeaverTails, and TruthfulQA demonstrate that TrinityX outperforms baselines in win rate, safety, and truthfulness, while also reducing memory usage and inference latency compared to prior MoE-based methods.  The authors provide ablation studies and cross-model evaluations to highlight the importance of calibrated routing and generalizability.

**Rigorous Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a significant challenge in LLM alignment: achieving consistent and reliable performance across multiple, often conflicting, dimensions (HHH). While prior works have tackled these dimensions in isolation, or through full fine-tuning which leads to catastrophic forgetting, this paper attempts to create a modular, scalable framework that integrates these concerns directly into the model architecture.

**Novelty:**

The novelty lies in the integration of several techniques:
*   **Mixture of Calibrated Experts (MoCaE) for HHH:** Using a MoE architecture with dedicated experts for each alignment dimension is a reasonable approach to modularity, but the critical aspect is the *calibrated* routing mechanism.
*   **Lightweight Task Vectors:** The use of low-rank task vectors that don't overwrite shared model parameters is a parameter-efficient way to encode task-specific knowledge and prevent catastrophic forgetting. This is an improvement over full model fine-tuning.
*   **Calibrated Routing with Regularization:** The use of entropy and KL divergence regularization for expert selection aims to stabilize training and encourage diverse expert activation. This addresses a key problem in MoE architectures, where routing can be unstable and lead to poor performance.

However, while these components are individually known, the specific combination and application to the HHH alignment problem are reasonably novel. It builds upon existing MoE research but incorporates alignment considerations more directly. The choice of evaluation datasets and metrics is also standard, so the comparison to baselines is meaningful.

**Significance:**

The potential significance of this work lies in its ability to:
*   **Improve HHH Alignment:** The experimental results suggest that TrinityX can indeed improve performance across all three alignment dimensions, outperforming previous methods on standard benchmarks.  The gains are significant, especially in honesty and safety.
*   **Reduce Resource Consumption:** The claim of reduced memory usage and inference latency is a practical benefit, making the framework more deployable in resource-constrained environments.  This could have a real-world impact.
*   **Enable Modular Alignment:** The modular design of TrinityX could allow for easier adaptation to new alignment objectives or datasets, as new experts can be trained and integrated without retraining the entire model.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Existing Components:** The framework relies on established techniques like MoEs and task vectors. The primary contribution is the way these are combined and adapted for HHH alignment, which slightly reduces the impact.
*   **Complexity:** MoE architectures are inherently more complex than standard Transformers.  This adds engineering overhead and can make training and debugging more challenging.
*   **Qualitative Analysis:** The qualitative analysis provided in the Appendix reveals some weaknesses in nuanced, knowledge-intensive, or safety-critical scenarios. The model is not flawless, and further improvements are needed in factual accuracy and safety assurance.
*   **Limited novelty:** The components are all known, which means its impact to the field as a whole is more limited.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-engineered solution to a vital problem in the field of LLM alignment. It effectively combines existing techniques to produce a modular and scalable framework. The experimental results demonstrate clear improvements over existing methods, and the paper provides a thorough analysis of the framework's components and limitations. While the individual components are not fundamentally new, the combination and application to the HHH alignment problem, along with the focus on calibrated routing, offer a meaningful contribution. Given these factors, a score reflecting the importance of the problem, the effectiveness of the proposed approach, and the limitations, is:

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Send to which account? Evaluation of an LLM-based Scambaiting System
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08493v1)
- **Authors**: Hossein Siadati, Haadi Jafarian, Sima Jafarikhah
- **Abstract**: Scammers are increasingly harnessing generative AI(GenAI) technologies to produce convincing phishing content at scale, amplifying financial fraud and undermining public trust. While conventional defenses, such as detection algorithms, user training, and reactive takedown efforts remain important, they often fall short in dismantling the infrastructure scammers depend on, including mule bank accounts and cryptocurrency wallets. To bridge this gap, a proactive and emerging strategy involves using conversational honeypots to engage scammers and extract actionable threat intelligence. This paper presents the first large-scale, real-world evaluation of a scambaiting system powered by large language models (LLMs). Over a five-month deployment, the system initiated over 2,600 engagements with actual scammers, resulting in a dataset of more than 18,700 messages. It achieved an Information Disclosure Rate (IDR) of approximately 32%, successfully extracting sensitive financial information such as mule accounts. Additionally, the system maintained a Human Acceptance Rate (HAR) of around 70%, indicating strong alignment between LLM-generated responses and human operator preferences. Alongside these successes, our analysis reveals key operational challenges. In particular, the system struggled with engagement takeoff: only 48.7% of scammers responded to the initial seed message sent by defenders. These findings highlight the need for further refinement and provide actionable insights for advancing the design of automated scambaiting systems.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a large-scale, real-world evaluation of an LLM-based scambaiting system. The system, deployed for five months, engaged with actual scammers using LLM-generated responses, sometimes with human-in-the-loop oversight. The study analyzes data from over 2,600 engagements and 18,700 messages, evaluating the system's effectiveness in eliciting sensitive information (e.g., mule bank accounts), maintaining human-acceptable response quality, and sustaining engagement. Key metrics include Information Disclosure Rate (IDR), Human Acceptance Rate (HAR), Takeoff Ratio, and Engagement Endurance. The analysis highlights the benefits of human-in-the-loop intervention, the importance of early-stage engagement management, and the influence of message style and timing on scammer responsiveness. The paper also identifies operational challenges and offers design insights for future automated scambaiting systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the emerging field of AI-driven cybersecurity, specifically in the realm of proactive scam defense.  The key strength of the paper is its *scale* and *real-world deployment*.  While prior research has explored LLM-based scambaiting, those studies have primarily been limited to simulations, short-term deployments, or smaller datasets. This paper represents the *first* large-scale evaluation of an operational system interacting with real scammers over a significant period (five months). The resulting dataset and the insights derived from it offer unique empirical evidence that wasn't available before.

**Strengths:**

*   **Large-Scale, Real-World Data:** The use of a large dataset from a real deployment makes the results more generalizable and relevant to practical applications.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation Framework:** The paper introduces a well-defined set of metrics tailored to automated scambaiting, going beyond simple success rates to include measures of speed, message quality, and engagement dynamics. Some metrics like Message Freshness and Response Invocation offer interesting, new ways to evaluate LLM effectiveness in this adversarial context.
*   **Practical Design Insights:** The paper provides actionable insights that can directly inform the design and deployment of future scambaiting systems, such as the optimal message length for initial outreach, the importance of human oversight, and the need for strategic timing of engagements.
*   **Rigorous Analysis:** The paper provides extensive analysis and clear presentation of the empirical results, making the findings easy to understand and apply.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Baseline Comparison:** While the paper compares LLM-only and Human-in-the-Loop modes, it lacks a direct comparison to a traditional, non-LLM scambaiting approach. Including such a comparison would further highlight the advantages (or disadvantages) of using LLMs.
*   **Lack of Details on Prompt Engineering:** The paper mentions using a single-prompt architecture but provides limited detail on the specific prompt templates used. More information on prompt engineering and its impact on performance would be valuable.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** The paper acknowledges ethical concerns but could have dedicated more space to discussing and mitigating them, particularly regarding deception and potential for unintended harm.
*   **Information Disclosure Rate (IDR):** Even with the novel strategy, the IDR rate of the system remains low, meaning that in almost 70% of the attempts, the scammers don't disclose financial information. It raises the questions on the cost benefit effectiveness of the system

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it demonstrates the feasibility and potential of LLM-based scambaiting as a proactive cybersecurity defense. It provides a valuable empirical foundation for future research and development in this area. The insights on human-in-the-loop intervention and engagement management are particularly relevant for building practical and effective systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Despite the aforementioned weaknesses, the paper's strengths, particularly its scale and real-world deployment, outweigh its shortcomings. The comprehensive evaluation framework and actionable insights make this a significant contribution that will likely influence future research and development in LLM-based security applications. This paper clearly presents valuable findings and adds a new layer to understanding LLM-powered adversarial dialogue systems in security contexts. I believe the limitations are balanced by the contribution and it represents a substantial step forward and the first large scale study.

### HumanAgencyBench: Scalable Evaluation of Human Agency Support in AI Assistants
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08494v1)
- **Authors**: Benjamin Sturgeon, Daniel Samuelson, Jacob Haimes, Jacy Reese Anthis
- **Abstract**: As humans delegate more tasks and decisions to artificial intelligence (AI), we risk losing control of our individual and collective futures. Relatively simple algorithmic systems already steer human decision-making, such as social media feed algorithms that lead people to unintentionally and absent-mindedly scroll through engagement-optimized content. In this paper, we develop the idea of human agency by integrating philosophical and scientific theories of agency with AI-assisted evaluation methods: using large language models (LLMs) to simulate and validate user queries and to evaluate AI responses. We develop HumanAgencyBench (HAB), a scalable and adaptive benchmark with six dimensions of human agency based on typical AI use cases. HAB measures the tendency of an AI assistant or agent to Ask Clarifying Questions, Avoid Value Manipulation, Correct Misinformation, Defer Important Decisions, Encourage Learning, and Maintain Social Boundaries. We find low-to-moderate agency support in contemporary LLM-based assistants and substantial variation across system developers and dimensions. For example, while Anthropic LLMs most support human agency overall, they are the least supportive LLMs in terms of Avoid Value Manipulation. Agency support does not appear to consistently result from increasing LLM capabilities or instruction-following behavior (e.g., RLHF), and we encourage a shift towards more robust safety and alignment targets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HUMANAGENCYBENCH (HAB), a novel benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of AI assistants to support human agency. The authors define human agency as the capacity for individuals to willfully shape their future by acting in their environment. HAB operationalizes this concept into six measurable dimensions: Ask Clarifying Questions, Avoid Value Manipulation, Correct Misinformation, Defer Important Decisions, Encourage Learning, and Maintain Social Boundaries. The benchmark uses LLMs to simulate user queries and to evaluate the responses of AI assistants based on specific rubrics. The authors evaluate 20 different LLMs, finding low-to-moderate agency support overall, with substantial variation across developers and dimensions. They also compare LLM evaluations with human evaluations. The study highlights tensions between current AI development practices focused on instruction-following and the support of human agency.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a unique perspective to AI evaluation by focusing on the concept of human agency.  Most existing benchmarks emphasize task performance, safety, or alignment with specific values. HAB's focus on empowering users and preserving their autonomy in interactions with AI assistants is a novel and timely contribution. The operationalization of agency into measurable dimensions, while not exhaustive, is a practical and innovative approach. The use of LLMs to generate test cases and evaluate responses is also a scalable methodology.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a growing concern about the potential for AI systems to inadvertently undermine human autonomy and control. By providing a framework for evaluating agency support, HAB can contribute to the development of more human-centered AI systems. The findings reveal significant variations in agency support across different LLMs, suggesting that current training methods may not adequately prioritize this aspect. The paper encourages a shift towards more robust safety and alignment targets that specifically address agency.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Definition of Agency:** The paper provides a well-defined and theoretically grounded definition of human agency, which is essential for a rigorous evaluation framework.
    *   **Measurable Dimensions:** The operationalization of agency into six dimensions makes it possible to quantitatively assess and compare different AI systems.
    *   **Scalable Methodology:** The use of LLMs for test case generation and evaluation allows for scalable and adaptive benchmark development.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation of 20 different LLMs provides a valuable overview of the current state of agency support in AI assistants.
    *   **Comparison with Human Evaluations:** The study includes a comparison of LLM evaluations with human evaluations, which helps to validate the accuracy and reliability of the automated evaluation framework.

* **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Subjectivity of Agency:** The concept of agency is inherently subjective, and the operationalization of agency into measurable dimensions may not fully capture all aspects of this complex concept. While the authors acknowledge the conceptual subjectivity, further research on the specific design choices in HAB (like the social boundary examples chosen) would further ground the results.
    *   **Potential Trade-offs:** The paper recognizes that supporting human agency may involve trade-offs with other desiderata of AI systems, such as efficiency, accuracy, or safety. However, the paper does not fully explore these trade-offs or provide guidance on how to balance competing objectives.
    *   **Generalizability:** The evaluation is limited to text-based interactions with AI assistants. The extent to which the findings generalize to other modalities (e.g., voice, visual) or other types of AI systems (e.g., autonomous vehicles, medical diagnosis systems) is unclear.

* **Potential Influence:** HAB could significantly impact AI safety and alignment research by providing a tangible and measurable target for promoting human agency. The framework could be adopted by AI developers to evaluate and improve the agency-supporting capabilities of their systems. It could also inform policy discussions about the ethical and societal implications of AI. The limitations, however, should also be noted so it isn't over-relied upon.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** While the paper has notable strengths in its novelty, clear methodology, and scalability, limitations in subjectivity, exploration of trade-offs, and generalizability prevent a higher score. The operationalization of agency into tangible dimensions and its rigorous evaluation warrants a fairly high score, despite recognizing areas of additional work and improvement. The work is significant for pushing the field towards a more nuanced evaluation of LLMs by emphasizing agency and promoting human-centered AI development. The human element of 'agency' can sometimes be lost in complex and otherwise very rigorous analyses.

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### TCPO: Thought-Centric Preference Optimization for Effective Embodied Decision-making
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08500v1)
- **Authors**: Kechen Jiao, Zhirui Fang, Jiahao Liu, Bei Li, Qifan Wang, Xinyu Liu, Junhao Ruan, Zhongjian Qiao, Yifan Zhu, Yaxin Xu, Jingang Wang, Xiu Li
- **Abstract**: Using effective generalization capabilities of vision language models (VLMs) in context-specific dynamic tasks for embodied artificial intelligence remains a significant challenge. Although supervised fine-tuned models can better align with the real physical world, they still exhibit sluggish responses and hallucination issues in dynamically changing environments, necessitating further alignment. Existing post-SFT methods, reliant on reinforcement learning and chain-of-thought (CoT) approaches, are constrained by sparse rewards and action-only optimization, resulting in low sample efficiency, poor consistency, and model degradation. To address these issues, this paper proposes Thought-Centric Preference Optimization (TCPO) for effective embodied decision-making. Specifically, TCPO introduces a stepwise preference-based optimization approach, transforming sparse reward signals into richer step sample pairs. It emphasizes the alignment of the model's intermediate reasoning process, mitigating the problem of model degradation. Moreover, by incorporating Action Policy Consistency Constraint (APC), it further imposes consistency constraints on the model output. Experiments in the ALFWorld environment demonstrate an average success rate of 26.67%, achieving a 6% improvement over RL4VLM and validating the effectiveness of our approach in mitigating model degradation after fine-tuning. These results highlight the potential of integrating preference-based learning techniques with CoT processes to enhance the decision-making capabilities of vision-language models in embodied agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces Thought-Centric Preference Optimization (TCPO), a novel approach for improving the decision-making abilities of vision-language models (VLMs) in embodied AI. TCPO addresses the limitations of existing post-SFT methods, which often suffer from low sample efficiency, poor consistency, and model degradation due to sparse rewards and action-only optimization. TCPO focuses on aligning the model's intermediate reasoning process (Chain-of-Thought - CoT) rather than solely optimizing actions. It employs a stepwise preference-based optimization technique and incorporates an Action Policy Consistency Constraint (APC) to maintain model consistency. Experiments on ALFWorld demonstrate that TCPO achieves improved success rates compared to RL4VLM, showcasing its effectiveness in mitigating model degradation and enhancing decision-making capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its thought-centric approach to preference optimization. It recognizes that refining the reasoning process is crucial for effective decision-making in embodied AI, rather than just focusing on the action output. The introduction of the Action Policy Consistency Constraint (APC) is also a novel element, aiming to preserve the pre-trained model's inherent consistency and prevent catastrophic forgetting during online adaptation. Integrating a stepwise Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) for improved sample efficiency is another significant contribution. This helps tackle the challenge of sparse rewards and long-horizon tasks.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a critical challenge in embodied AI: generalizing VLM capabilities to dynamic environments. By improving sample efficiency and model consistency during fine-tuning, TCPO contributes to making these models more practical and robust for real-world applications.  The focus on interpretable reasoning through CoT, coupled with consistency constraints, enhances the transparency and reliability of the decision-making process. Results showing a 6% improvement over RL4VLM (a strong baseline) are noteworthy.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation: The paper articulates the limitations of existing approaches well.
    *   Well-defined approach: The TCPO framework, with its stepwise preference optimization and APC, is clearly explained.
    *   Strong experimental results: The ALFWorld experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of TCPO.
    *   Ablation studies: The paper includes ablation studies to validate the contribution of key components (APC, APW).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited Environment Variety: The primary evaluation is conducted on ALFWorld.  While this is a standard benchmark, expanding evaluations to more diverse and complex embodied environments (e.g., VirtualHome) would strengthen the generalization claims.
    *   Hyperparameter Sensitivity: The discussion regarding the sensitivity of the 'к' parameter (controlling APC intensity) suggests potential challenges in adapting TCPO to new environments. While an optimal 'к' value was found through comprehensive testing, a method to automate the 'к' finding process would enhance the usability.
    *   Markovian Assumption: Acknowledgement of the model's reliance on the Markovian assumption represents a weakness, especially as real-world tasks are often non-Markovian. The paper does suggest future research to incorporate temporal models, but this is a current limitation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in embodied AI by shifting the focus towards reasoning-centric optimization. The proposed TCPO framework provides a solid foundation for building more robust and consistent VLMs for embodied agents. The techniques of step-wise preference optimization and action policy consistency constraints could also be adopted and extended in other related domains.

**Justification for Score:**

TCPO represents a valuable advancement in the field of embodied AI. While the experimental evaluation could benefit from broader environmental testing, the core idea of thought-centric optimization with consistency constraints addresses a fundamental challenge in adapting VLMs for dynamic environments. The demonstrated performance improvements, along with the well-reasoned design and clear presentation, contribute to the significance of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Agents of Discovery
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08535v1)
- **Authors**: Sascha Diefenbacher, Anna Hallin, Gregor Kasieczka, Michael Krämer, Anne Lauscher, Tim Lukas
- **Abstract**: The substantial data volumes encountered in modern particle physics and other domains of fundamental physics research allow (and require) the use of increasingly complex data analysis tools and workflows. While the use of machine learning (ML) tools for data analysis has recently proliferated, these tools are typically special-purpose algorithms that rely, for example, on encoded physics knowledge to reach optimal performance. In this work, we investigate a new and orthogonal direction: Using recent progress in large language models (LLMs) to create a team of agents -- instances of LLMs with specific subtasks -- that jointly solve data analysis-based research problems in a way similar to how a human researcher might: by creating code to operate standard tools and libraries (including ML systems) and by building on results of previous iterations. If successful, such agent-based systems could be deployed to automate routine analysis components to counteract the increasing complexity of modern tool chains. To investigate the capabilities of current-generation commercial LLMs, we consider the task of anomaly detection via the publicly available and highly-studied LHC Olympics dataset. Several current models by OpenAI (GPT-4o, o4-mini, GPT-4.1, and GPT-5) are investigated and their stability tested. Overall, we observe the capacity of the agent-based system to solve this data analysis problem. The best agent-created solutions mirror the performance of human state-of-the-art results.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in an agent-based system to automate data analysis tasks in high-energy physics. The authors create a framework with several agents (researcher, coder, code reviewer, logic reviewer) built on LLMs.  They test the system on the LHC Olympics anomaly detection challenge dataset, focusing on anomaly detection. They compare different prompting strategies and LLM models (GPT-4o, GPT-4.1, o4-mini, GPT-5) in terms of performance, stability, cost, and reproducibility. The best solutions generated by the agents mirrored the performance of human state-of-the-art results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** While LLMs have been applied to various tasks, including code generation and assisting individual steps in data analysis, the paper's novelty lies in its attempt to create a complete, agent-based *workflow* for a complex physics analysis problem. The idea of orchestrating several LLM instances, each with specific roles and interacting through tools, mimics the iterative and collaborative nature of human research more closely than simply using an LLM for a single task. This agent-based approach, while inspired by work in other fields (chemistry, materials science, software engineering), is a first for high-energy physics data analysis. The use of the LHC Olympics dataset as a standardized benchmark adds to the value.

* **Significance:** The increasing complexity of data analysis workflows in high-energy physics poses a real challenge.  Automating such workflows using AI has the potential to significantly increase efficiency, improve reproducibility, and potentially lead to faster scientific discovery. This paper demonstrates the *feasibility* of such an approach, suggesting a new direction in how physicists might interact with data analysis in the future. The paper clearly presents how the proposed workflow, composed of task automation and provenance tracking, helps speed up research. It demonstrates that commercially available LLMs are capable of creating solutions on par with state-of-the-art human solutions.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined Framework:** The agent-based framework is clearly described, with the roles of each agent and their interactions well-defined. The use of tools to interface with the external environment is a crucial design element for reproducibility and control.
    * **Systematic Evaluation:** The authors perform a systematic comparison of different LLMs and prompting strategies, providing quantitative metrics for performance, cost, and stability. This allows for a more objective assessment of the approach. The thoroughness in evaluating metrics associated with code quality (linting errors, etc.) is commendable.
    * **Reproducibility Focus:** The emphasis on reproducibility is a major strength. The design preserves a clear record of what has been run, allowing researchers to inspect and validate results.
    * **Interesting Results:** The results show that LLMs like GPT-5 can achieve performance comparable to human experts on this anomaly detection task. This suggests that this method could be very valuable to physicists in the future.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to a single task (anomaly detection) and a specific dataset. While this is a good starting point, it remains to be seen how well the approach generalizes to other, more complex analyses.
    * **Dependence on Commercial LLMs:**  The framework relies on commercially available LLMs, raising concerns about long-term availability, cost, and control over the underlying models. The lack of seeding in the LLMs makes reproducing runs very hard, if not impossible.
    * **"Black Box" Nature of LLMs:**  While the framework attempts to improve transparency, the internal workings of the LLMs remain a "black box," making it difficult to fully understand *why* certain decisions are made and how to optimize the system.
    * **No real-world deployment:** The paper doesn't actually deploy this system as an improvement over standard approaches. It focuses on the theoretical implications instead, which leaves room for question on the true application, efficiency, and usefulness.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper can influence the field by:
    * Inspiring further research into agent-based systems for data analysis in high-energy physics and other scientific domains.
    *  Providing a framework and benchmark for evaluating the performance of LLMs in these applications.
    *  Potentially leading to the development of more automated and efficient data analysis workflows.
    * Demonstrating that LLMs are already performing human-like analyses.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a promising new approach to automating complex data analysis tasks in high-energy physics. The results show the feasibility of using LLMs in an agent-based system to achieve performance comparable to human experts. However, the limitations regarding scope, dependence on commercial LLMs, and the "black box" nature of the models prevent a higher score. This paper is a good first step that will need significantly more exploration to establish its usefulness.

### MESH -- Understanding Videos Like Human: Measuring Hallucinations in Large Video Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08538v1)
- **Authors**: Garry Yang, Zizhe Chen, Man Hon Wong, Haoyu Lei, Yongqiang Chen, Zhenguo Li, Kaiwen Zhou, James Cheng
- **Abstract**: Large Video Models (LVMs) build on the semantic capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) and vision modules by integrating temporal information to better understand dynamic video content. Despite their progress, LVMs are prone to hallucinations-producing inaccurate or irrelevant descriptions. Current benchmarks for video hallucination depend heavily on manual categorization of video content, neglecting the perception-based processes through which humans naturally interpret videos. We introduce MESH, a benchmark designed to evaluate hallucinations in LVMs systematically. MESH uses a Question-Answering framework with binary and multi-choice formats incorporating target and trap instances. It follows a bottom-up approach, evaluating basic objects, coarse-to-fine subject features, and subject-action pairs, aligning with human video understanding. We demonstrate that MESH offers an effective and comprehensive approach for identifying hallucinations in videos. Our evaluations show that while LVMs excel at recognizing basic objects and features, their susceptibility to hallucinations increases markedly when handling fine details or aligning multiple actions involving various subjects in longer videos.
- **Summary**: The paper introduces MESH, a benchmark for evaluating hallucinations in large video models (LVMs) using a question-answering framework. MESH assesses LVMs' ability to understand videos by evaluating their perception of basic objects, subject features, and subject-action pairs, aligning with human video understanding. The benchmark includes target and trap instances in binary and multi-choice question formats. The authors demonstrate that while LVMs can recognize basic objects and features, their susceptibility to hallucinations increases when handling fine details or aligning multiple actions involving various subjects in longer videos. They evaluate several LVMs and analyze the impact of multi-frame understanding on hallucination levels and video question answering (VQA) performance.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty of the paper lies primarily in the benchmark design. The idea of a bottom-up approach, mirroring human video understanding by focusing on mise-en-scène and categorizing visual elements into setting, character, and stage, is reasonably novel. The use of target/trap question structures, inspired by POPE, is not new, but the adaptation to video and the creation of more complex trap instances across different categories (setting, character, action) adds some value. However, many video understanding benchmarks already exist (the paper cites numerous ones). The unique aspect of MESH is its targeted approach to measuring specific types of hallucinations related to core video understanding concepts and its integration of both binary and multi-choice questions with tailored negative sampling strategies (e.g. spatial awareness, feature similarity filtering). The comparative approach by focusing on similar actions between characters also adds to the novelty.

**Significance:** The paper's significance is moderate. Hallucination in LVMs is an important problem, and a benchmark specifically designed to evaluate different facets of this issue is valuable. Demonstrating that LVMs struggle with finer details and multi-subject interactions, despite excelling at basic object recognition, provides actionable insights for future research. The correlation between MESH performance and general VQA benchmarks is also promising, suggesting that improvements in MESH could translate to broader advancements in video understanding. However, the paper's reliance on the TVQA+ dataset and the generation of questions based on its annotations might limit its generality. Furthermore, the lack of thorough ablation studies exploring alternative question generation strategies and trap instance selection mechanisms weakens the paper's claims about the superiority of the proposed benchmark. The benchmark's overall significance would be significantly strengthened by comparative experiments against other contemporary video understanding benchmarks with more detailed discussion of when MESH shows unique strength or insight, and through showing transferrability across different video domains beyond those seen in TV shows.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-defined benchmark with a clear methodology.
*   Addresses an important problem: hallucination in LVMs.
*   Provides insightful analysis of LVM performance across different aspects of video understanding.
*   Aligns with human-centric video understanding by focusing on mise-en-scène.

**Weaknesses:**

*   Relies heavily on the TVQA+ dataset, potentially limiting generality.
*   Question generation process is complex and requires manual adjustment.
*   Limited ablation studies exploring design choices.
*   Limited exploration and comparisons against contemporary video understanding benchmarks.
*   The experiments show that models trained using additional video data during fine-tuning (LLaVA-Video) often do better than other models.
*   The contribution of novel loss functions to address specific problem remains unclear.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more robust and reliable LVMs by providing a targeted benchmark for evaluating and mitigating hallucinations. It could also inspire further research into human-inspired video understanding and the design of more comprehensive evaluation metrics.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, the paper makes a modest contribution to the field. While the benchmark is well-defined and addresses an important problem, its limited scope, and lack of extensive validation and comparisons with existing benchmarks limits its overall impact. The insights gained from the experiments are valuable, but not groundbreaking. Furthermore, the overall influence would have been increased by showing the approach to hallucination detection could also transfer to other video understanding tasks by fine-tuning the underlying models to address specific types of hallucination.

### CM-Align: Consistency-based Multilingual Alignment for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08541v1)
- **Authors**: Xue Zhang, Yunlong Liang, Fandong Meng, Songming Zhang, Yufeng Chen, Jinan Xu, Jie Zhou
- **Abstract**: Current large language models (LLMs) generally show a significant performance gap in alignment between English and other languages. To bridge this gap, existing research typically leverages the model's responses in English as a reference to select the best/worst responses in other languages, which are then used for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training. However, we argue that there are two limitations in the current methods that result in noisy multilingual preference data and further limited alignment performance: 1) Not all English responses are of high quality, and using a response with low quality may mislead the alignment for other languages. 2) Current methods usually use biased or heuristic approaches to construct multilingual preference pairs. To address these limitations, we design a consistency-based data selection method to construct high-quality multilingual preference data for improving multilingual alignment (CM-Align). Specifically, our method includes two parts: consistency-guided English reference selection and cross-lingual consistency-based multilingual preference data construction. Experimental results on three LLMs and three common tasks demonstrate the effectiveness and superiority of our method, which further indicates the necessity of constructing high-quality preference data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the CM-Align paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CM-Align, a novel consistency-based method for improving the multilingual alignment of large language models (LLMs).  The core idea is to construct high-quality multilingual preference data for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training. CM-Align addresses two key limitations of existing approaches: 1) the unreliable quality of English reference responses and 2) the use of biased/heuristic methods for creating multilingual preference pairs.  CM-Align consists of two main components: consistency-guided English reference selection (choosing the most consistent English response as the "anchor") and cross-lingual consistency-based preference data construction (selecting preferred/dispreferred responses in other languages based on consistency with the English anchor). The paper evaluates CM-Align on Math, Code, and General Instruction Following tasks using three different LLMs, demonstrating its effectiveness and superiority over baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary contribution lies in the consistency-based approach for multilingual alignment. The idea of using a reliable English reference based on self-consistency, rather than treating all English responses as equally valid, is a significant improvement. Similarly, the idea of constructing multilingual preference pairs based on *cross-lingual consistency* is more principled than relying on translation probabilities or simply using translated English responses directly. While previous work has explored multilingual alignment, CM-Align provides a more robust and data-driven strategy for constructing preference data. The design of task-specific consistency metrics also enhances the method's practical applicability.

*   **Significance:** The demonstrated improvements over baselines across various tasks and LLMs suggest CM-Align has the potential to significantly impact the field of multilingual LLMs. Overcoming the performance imbalance across languages is crucial for widespread adoption of LLMs in diverse linguistic contexts. The paper also shows promising results in out-of-domain generalization, indicating that collaborative learning across languages is possible. The method's reliance on DPO makes it computationally efficient and scalable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Principled Approach:** The consistency-based framework is well-motivated and theoretically sound.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering a range of tasks, model sizes, and languages.
    *   **Task-Specific Metrics:** The use of tailored consistency metrics for different tasks is a crucial design choice.
    *   **Generalizability:** Demonstrated improvements are consistent across different LLMs and tasks.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on English:** While CM-Align addresses issues with English-centric approaches, it still heavily relies on English as the anchor language. Future work could explore methods to reduce this dependency or use other high-resource languages.
    *   **Limited Language Coverage:** The experiments cover a limited set of languages. Evaluating on a more diverse set of languages, including low-resource languages, would further strengthen the paper.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper mentions the importance of the hyperparameter α for balancing CodeBLEU and CodeBERTScore, but it doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the sensitivity of CM-Align to other hyperparameters.
    *   **Limited Comparisons:** More comprehensive comparisons to other related methods, especially the newer multi-lingual alignment techniques, would enhance the comparative assessment.
    *   **Iterative Refinement:** The method does not implement Iterative DPO. This can be a future work.

*   **Potential Influence:** CM-Align provides a practical and effective solution for improving multilingual alignment, which could be readily adopted by researchers and practitioners. The method's focus on data quality and consistency could influence the development of future multilingual alignment techniques.

*   **Justification for Score:** I assign a score of 7. The paper presents a novel and well-validated approach to a significant problem (multilingual alignment). While the method has limitations, its strengths and potential influence on the field are substantial. Future work should focus on addressing the limitations, such as reducing the reliance on English and evaluating on a more diverse set of languages. In addition, the work will be further strengthened by including iterative DPO and increasing the data volume.
