### BitROM: Weight Reload-Free CiROM Architecture Towards Billion-Parameter 1.58-bit LLM Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08542v1)
- **Authors**: Wenlun Zhang, Xinyu Li, Shimpei Ando, Kentaro Yoshioka
- **Abstract**: Compute-in-Read-Only-Memory (CiROM) accelerators offer outstanding energy efficiency for CNNs by eliminating runtime weight updates. However, their scalability to Large Language Models (LLMs) is fundamentally constrained by their vast parameter sizes. Notably, LLaMA-7B - the smallest model in LLaMA series - demands more than 1,000 cm2 of silicon area even in advanced CMOS nodes. This paper presents BitROM, the first CiROM-based accelerator that overcomes this limitation through co-design with BitNet's 1.58-bit quantization model, enabling practical and efficient LLM inference at the edge. BitROM introduces three key innovations: 1) a novel Bidirectional ROM Array that stores two ternary weights per transistor; 2) a Tri-Mode Local Accumulator optimized for ternary-weight computations; and 3) an integrated Decode-Refresh (DR) eDRAM that supports on-die KV-cache management, significantly reducing external memory access during decoding. In addition, BitROM integrates LoRA-based adapters to enable efficient transfer learning across various downstream tasks. Evaluated in 65nm CMOS, BitROM achieves 20.8 TOPS/W and a bit density of 4,967 kB/mm2 - offering a 10x improvement in area efficiency over prior digital CiROM designs. Moreover, the DR eDRAM contributes to a 43.6% reduction in external DRAM access, further enhancing deployment efficiency for LLMs in edge applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BitROM, a novel Compute-in-Read-Only-Memory (CiROM) accelerator designed specifically for BitNet-based Large Language Models (LLMs).  It addresses the scalability limitations of traditional CiROM architectures when applied to large models like LLaMA-7B. BitROM achieves this through three key innovations: a Bidirectional ROM Array (BiROMA) storing two ternary weights per transistor, a Tri-Mode Local Accumulator (TriMLA) optimized for ternary-weight computations, and an integrated Decode-Refresh (DR) eDRAM for on-die KV-cache management. The DR eDRAM significantly reduces external memory access during decoding. The design also integrates LoRA adapters for efficient transfer learning.  Evaluated in 65nm CMOS, BitROM demonstrates high energy efficiency (20.8 TOPS/W) and bit density (4,967 kB/mm²), along with reduced external DRAM access.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents several genuinely novel aspects.  The BiROMA architecture, enabling storage of *two* ternary weights per transistor, is a key contribution towards improving memory density.  The TriMLA, specifically designed for ternary computations, is also a useful optimization.  The integration of DR eDRAM to address KV-cache management is a practical solution to a known bottleneck in LLM inference. The combination of all three into a coherent architecture is a valuable system-level contribution. The integration of LoRA, while not revolutionary, shows awareness of the need for flexibility in LLM applications.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and significant problem: how to efficiently deploy LLMs on resource-constrained edge devices. The focus on BitNet's ternary quantization model is also strategic, as it represents a promising direction for model compression.  The reported improvements in energy efficiency and bit density are substantial and could pave the way for more practical edge LLM deployments.  The detailed evaluation, including post-layout simulations, strengthens the credibility of the results. The work also helps bridge the gap between CiROM, which has demonstrated success with CNNs, and the new challenges of LLMs. The reported reduction in DRAM accesses by using an on-chip KV cache is also quite impactful.

* **Strengths:**
    * Clearly articulates the problem and the limitations of existing approaches.
    * Presents a well-designed architecture with clear explanations of each component.
    * Provides thorough experimental results, including ablation studies and comparisons to prior work.
    * Addresses a timely and relevant problem in the field of AI hardware.
    * Demonstrates a practical implementation in 65nm CMOS.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * While the paper presents impressive results, a direct hardware implementation and measurements on silicon are absent.  The results are based on post-layout simulations which is one step from the actual silicon fabrication and measurements.
    * The specific DR eDRAM used is taken from prior work. It would be better to provide an analysis of the energy and area overhead of DR eDRAM instead of blindly trusting the results from the other sources.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the direction of CiROM research, particularly in the context of LLM acceleration.  The BiROMA and TriMLA architectures could be adopted or adapted by other researchers.  The DR eDRAM approach to KV-cache management is a valuable lesson for future edge LLM accelerator designs. The use of LoRA is also very relevant for practical deployments and can be used by other designs. The success of combining CiROM with BitNet highlights a potentially fruitful line of inquiry.

**Score:** 8

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a strong contribution to the field of AI hardware acceleration, specifically for LLMs. The core architectural innovations (BiROMA, TriMLA, DR eDRAM integration) are novel and well-motivated. The experimental results demonstrate significant performance improvements over existing approaches. While the absence of silicon measurements is a notable limitation, the thorough post-layout simulations and detailed analysis provide convincing evidence of the design's potential. The paper's focus on BitNet and edge deployment makes it highly relevant to current trends in the field. While some parts of the architecture (e.g., DR eDRAM) are re-used from the prior work, overall the paper shows excellent promise, justifying a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.AR
- **Score**: 8/10

### CNN-ViT Hybrid for Pneumonia Detection: Theory and Empiric on Limited Data without Pretraining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08586v1)
- **Authors**: Prashant Singh Basnet, Roshan Chitrakar
- **Abstract**: This research explored the hybridization of CNN and ViT within a training dataset of limited size, and introduced a distinct class imbalance. The training was made from scratch with a mere focus on theoretically and experimentally exploring the architectural strengths of the proposed hybrid model. Experiments were conducted across varied data fractions with balanced and imbalanced training datasets. Comparatively, the hybrid model, complementing the strengths of CNN and ViT, achieved the highest recall of 0.9443 (50% data fraction in balanced) and consistency in F1 score around 0.85, suggesting reliability in diagnosis. Additionally, the model was successful in outperforming CNN and ViT in imbalanced datasets. Despite its complex architecture, it required comparable training time to the transformers in all data fractions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores a CNN-ViT hybrid model for pneumonia detection using chest X-ray images. The core motivation is to address the limitations of both CNNs and Vision Transformers (ViTs) when training data is limited and class imbalance exists. CNNs are good at local feature extraction but struggle with global dependencies, while ViTs require substantial data for effective training. The proposed hybrid combines CNN layers for local feature extraction with a ViT to capture global context. The paper trains the models from scratch, *without* using pretrained weights.  It compares the hybrid model's performance against standalone CNN and ViT models across varying data fractions (50%, 70%, 100%) and under both balanced and imbalanced data settings. Results show the hybrid model generally performs better in terms of recall and F1-score, especially in imbalanced settings, while demonstrating comparable training times. A theoretical analysis is also presented comparing the complexities of the models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The *idea* of hybridizing CNNs and ViTs is *not* entirely novel, as the paper itself cites several other hybrid approaches.  However, the contribution stems from a specific focus:

1.  **Training from Scratch with Data Constraints:** Many hybrid approaches rely on pre-trained models, especially ImageNet. The paper explicitly avoids pretraining and focuses on a low-data setting, making it more relevant for medical imaging where large, labeled datasets are often scarce. *This is a significant contribution.*
2.  **In-depth analysis with Imbalanced Datasets:**  The explicit focus on performance in the presence of class imbalance, a common issue in medical datasets, adds value. Comparing the models in balanced and imbalanced settings provide good insights of model behavior.
3.  **Theoretical Analysis:**  The analysis of model complexity (using the Vapnik-Chervonenkis dimension) and its relation to generalization error gives a reasonable theoretical grounding to the experimental observations. Although this is simplified, it's better than a purely empirical study.
4.  **Comparability of Architecture:** Although the models are trained from scratch, it is not clearly indicated that similar and optimal architectures were employed across all the models. It's unclear whether the training methodology can ensure equal performance to claim that a specific model is superior.
5.  **Clinical validation/integration:** There isn't clinical validation beyond the numbers, which reduces impact, especially to the end users.

**Significance:** The significance lies in providing a practical solution for pneumonia detection in resource-constrained scenarios (limited data, no access to large pretrained models). The results suggest that a hybrid approach offers a robust and consistent solution compared to relying solely on CNNs or ViTs, especially when dealing with imbalanced datasets, a common real-world challenge.
However, the paper would have benefited from demonstrating the scalability of the approach with larger datasets or other medical imaging tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear motivation and problem definition.
*   Well-defined experiments with multiple data fractions and balanced/imbalanced settings.
*   Theoretical analysis of model complexity.
*   Comparison against strong baselines (CNN and ViT).
*   Focus on a practical scenario (limited data, no pretraining).

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited scope of application; needs extension to other diseases/modalities.
*   The absolute performance gains, while consistent, are not revolutionary.
*   Lack of detailed architecture comparison in standalone setups.
*   Lack of clinical validation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable, incremental contribution. While the *idea* of CNN-ViT hybridization isn't brand new, the specific focus on training from scratch, dealing with data limitations, and addressing class imbalance in medical imaging is significant. The theoretical analysis provides a solid foundation for the experimental results. The paper's findings are likely to be of interest to researchers and practitioners working on medical image analysis and deep learning in resource-constrained settings.

However, the limited scope (pneumonia detection, relatively small dataset), lack of scalability analysis, and incremental nature of the performance improvements prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work. This is a very good engineering paper and has some new and valuable results that the wider community can benefit from.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM Ensemble for RAG: Role of Context Length in Zero-Shot Question Answering for BioASQ Challenge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08596v1)
- **Authors**: Dima Galat, Diego Molla-Aliod
- **Abstract**: Biomedical question answering (QA) poses significant challenges due to the need for precise interpretation of specialized knowledge drawn from a vast, complex, and rapidly evolving corpus. In this work, we explore how large language models (LLMs) can be used for information retrieval (IR), and an ensemble of zero-shot models can accomplish state-of-the-art performance on a domain-specific Yes/No QA task. Evaluating our approach on the BioASQ challenge tasks, we show that ensembles can outperform individual LLMs and in some cases rival or surpass domain-tuned systems - all while preserving generalizability and avoiding the need for costly fine-tuning or labeled data. Our method aggregates outputs from multiple LLM variants, including models from Anthropic and Google, to synthesize more accurate and robust answers. Moreover, our investigation highlights a relationship between context length and performance: while expanded contexts are meant to provide valuable evidence, they simultaneously risk information dilution and model disorientation. These findings emphasize IR as a critical foundation in Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approaches for biomedical QA systems. Precise, focused retrieval remains essential for ensuring LLMs operate within relevant information boundaries when generating answers from retrieved documents. Our results establish that ensemble-based zero-shot approaches, when paired with effective RAG pipelines, constitute a practical and scalable alternative to domain-tuned systems for biomedical question answering.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the application of Large Language Models (LLMs) for biomedical question answering (QA), specifically within the BioASQ challenge. The authors present a zero-shot QA ensembling framework that leverages multiple LLMs, combined with a multi-stage Information Retrieval (IR) pipeline incorporating LLM-generated queries, BM25 lexical search, and semantic reranking. The study investigates the impact of context length on QA performance and demonstrates how their system achieves state-of-the-art results on a Yes/No QA task while maintaining generalizability.  The paper highlights that precise IR is critical for effective Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and that ensemble-based zero-shot approaches can rival domain-tuned systems when combined with an effective RAG pipeline.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** While the individual components of the system (LLMs, ensembling, BM25, semantic reranking) are not novel in isolation, the paper's contribution lies in the *integration* of these components into a cohesive pipeline specifically tailored for the BioASQ biomedical QA challenge and demonstrated in an end-to-end fashion in the A+ task. The specific combination of LLM-generated queries with traditional lexical search and subsequent semantic reranking is also a worthy contribution, particularly in the biomedical domain. The focused analysis on the effect of context length and its impact on the performance of different QA methodologies is valuable for the research community.

**Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

*   **Demonstrates strong performance in a challenging domain:** BioASQ is a well-established benchmark, and achieving state-of-the-art results on the Yes/No QA task demonstrates the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
*   **Offers a practical alternative to fine-tuning:** The zero-shot approach avoids the need for costly fine-tuning or large amounts of labeled data, making it a more scalable solution.
*   **Highlights the importance of IR for LLM-based QA:** The paper emphasizes that precise and focused retrieval is crucial for ensuring that LLMs operate within relevant information boundaries, which is particularly important in domains with vast and complex knowledge bases like biomedicine.
*   **Provides insights into context length and performance:** The investigation into the relationship between context length and performance is valuable for understanding the limitations of LLMs and the importance of effective IR.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Evaluation:** While the paper presents results on BioASQ, more comprehensive evaluation on other biomedical QA datasets would further strengthen the claims. A more detailed analysis of where the models perform well and fail, including error analysis, would also be beneficial.
*   **Lack of Ablation Studies:**  The paper would benefit from ablation studies that isolate the impact of individual components in the IR and QA pipelines, to better understand the contribution of each element. For example, evaluating the LLM ensembling approach in isolation without the specialized IR pipeline.
*   **Limited model comparisons:** The model comparisons are limited, more comparisons with other systems in BioASQ will be very valuable.
*   **Relevance to BioASQ Lab at CLEF 2025**: There is some inconsistency as the Introduction mentions BioASQ at CLEF 2024 and the rest of the notebook mentions 2025.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

*   It can encourage researchers to explore ensemble-based zero-shot approaches for domain-specific QA.
*   It can promote the development of more effective IR pipelines for LLM-based QA.
*   It can stimulate further research into the impact of context length on LLM performance and the challenges of information dilution.
*   It can offer a practical and scalable alternative to domain-tuned systems for biomedical QA.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **7.0**.

**Rationale:**

The paper provides a valuable contribution to the field of biomedical QA by demonstrating the effectiveness of a zero-shot LLM ensembling approach combined with a tailored IR pipeline. While the individual components are not entirely novel, the *integration* of these components and the *insights* gained regarding context length and the importance of IR are significant. The successful application of the approach to the challenging BioASQ benchmark further strengthens the paper. The weaknesses, particularly the limited evaluation, and lack of ablation studies, prevent it from scoring higher.

The novelty lies in the end-to-end combination and specific adaptation for the Biomedical domain rather than breakthrough methodological innovation. Given the growing reliance on LLMs, this integration-focused approach provides practical value to researchers in the field. It serves as a useful case study and benchmark.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### Memorization in Large Language Models in Medicine: Prevalence, Characteristics, and Implications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08604v1)
- **Authors**: Anran Li, Lingfei Qian, Mengmeng Du, Yu Yin, Yan Hu, Zihao Sun, Yihang Fu, Erica Stutz, Xuguang Ai, Qianqian Xie, Rui Zhu, Jimin Huang, Yifan Yang, Siru Liu, Yih-Chung Tham, Lucila Ohno-Machado, Hyunghoon Cho, Zhiyong Lu, Hua Xu, Qingyu Chen
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated significant potential in medicine. To date, LLMs have been widely applied to tasks such as diagnostic assistance, medical question answering, and clinical information synthesis. However, a key open question remains: to what extent do LLMs memorize medical training data. In this study, we present the first comprehensive evaluation of memorization of LLMs in medicine, assessing its prevalence (how frequently it occurs), characteristics (what is memorized), volume (how much content is memorized), and potential downstream impacts (how memorization may affect medical applications). We systematically analyze common adaptation scenarios: (1) continued pretraining on medical corpora, (2) fine-tuning on standard medical benchmarks, and (3) fine-tuning on real-world clinical data, including over 13,000 unique inpatient records from Yale New Haven Health System. The results demonstrate that memorization is prevalent across all adaptation scenarios and significantly higher than reported in the general domain. Memorization affects both the development and adoption of LLMs in medicine and can be categorized into three types: beneficial (e.g., accurate recall of clinical guidelines and biomedical references), uninformative (e.g., repeated disclaimers or templated medical document language), and harmful (e.g., regeneration of dataset-specific or sensitive clinical content). Based on these findings, we offer practical recommendations to facilitate beneficial memorization that enhances domain-specific reasoning and factual accuracy, minimize uninformative memorization to promote deeper learning beyond surface-level patterns, and mitigate harmful memorization to prevent the leakage of sensitive or identifiable patient information.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the provided OCR text of the research paper. Here's a summary, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty, significance, and an overall score with justification:

**Summary:**

The paper "Memorization in Large Language Models in Medicine: Prevalence, Characteristics, and Implications" investigates the phenomenon of memorization in large language models (LLMs) that have been adapted for medical applications through continued pretraining or fine-tuning.  The study comprehensively evaluates the prevalence, characteristics, volume, and downstream impact of memorization across various adaptation scenarios, including continued pretraining on medical corpora, fine-tuning on standard medical benchmarks, and fine-tuning on real-world clinical data. The evaluation also covers medical foundation language models and general-purpose LLMs. The findings reveal that memorization is widespread, significantly higher than in general-domain LLMs, and categorized into beneficial, uninformative, and harmful types. The paper further analyzes factors influencing memorization, such as model size, input length, and training stage. Finally, the paper discusses the implications of memorization for method development and adoption of LLMs in medicine and provides practical recommendations for managing memorization to maximize benefits and minimize risks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's main strength is the comprehensiveness of its investigation. While memorization in LLMs has been studied in the general domain, this study is the first, as they mention, to provide a comprehensive evaluation of its prevalence, characteristics, and impacts specifically within the medical domain. Characterizing the types of memorization (beneficial, uninformative, harmful) and linking these to medical applications is novel. Observing that fine-tuning does not necessarily eliminate memorization acquired during pre-training, along with the fact that models still retain the medical knowledge after fine tuning is not only insightful, but also unique. The study's systematic evaluation across diverse adaptation scenarios (continued pretraining, fine-tuning on benchmarks, and fine-tuning on real-world clinical data) makes it unique.

*   **Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the responsible development and deployment of LLMs in medicine. Identifying the risks associated with memorizing sensitive clinical data (patient-specific information, diagnostic errors) and the potential for reduced generalizability highlights the need for careful consideration during model adaptation and deployment. The distinction between beneficial and uninformative memorization provides insights into refining training methodologies. Practical recommendations that encourage deeper learning, improve domain knowledge, facilitate better downstream tasks, and de-identify potentially harmful sensitive patient data help to make the paper actionable. The research addresses a growing challenge as LLMs become more prevalent in healthcare, contributing to a safer and more effective use of these technologies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The study systematically analyzes memorization across different scenarios, models, and metrics.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper provides actionable recommendations for mitigating the risks of memorization.
    *   **Real-world Clinical Data Analysis:** Using clinical data, particularly in the sensitive diagnosis setting, makes the analysis highly relevant.
    *   **Detailed Characterization:** The categorization of memorization types and the identification of influencing factors provide valuable insights.
    *   Clear communication of the risks and benefits for clinical application, providing a more informed approach to LLM.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Generalizability:** Some of the findings might be specific to the models and datasets used in the study. The absence of an analysis of the PMC-LLAMA models might limit the generalizability of these results.
    *   **Focus on Exact Matches:** The primary evaluation measure is based on exact matches, which may underestimate the prevalence of semantic memorization (where the model expresses the same information in different wording).
    *   **Manual Examination:** The PHI detection involved manual reviewing, so its reliability will be subject to that of a human.

*   **Potential Influence:** This research is likely to influence future research in the field by:
    *   **Informing training methodologies:** The insights into beneficial, uninformative, and harmful memorization can guide the development of more effective training strategies.
    *   **Shaping reporting standards:** The call for community efforts to strengthen reporting guidelines for LLMs in medical applications can lead to better transparency and accountability.
    *   **Guiding deployment frameworks:** The emphasis on memorization-related vulnerabilities can inform the development of safer and more compliant deployment frameworks.
    *   **Providing a benchmark for memorization.** The paper provides an accessible source for medical foundations that can inform new LLM designs.

**Overall Score:**

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a substantial and novel contribution to the field by systematically investigating memorization in medical LLMs, an area that has received limited attention. The comprehensive evaluation, practical recommendations, and real-world clinical data analysis make it highly significant and impactful. While the limitations related to generalizability, focus on exact matches, and incomplete PHI detection are important to consider, they do not diminish the overall value of the research. The research is likely to influence future research, development, and deployment of LLMs in medicine, leading to safer and more effective healthcare applications. Thus, it merits a high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### AdsQA: Towards Advertisement Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08621v1)
- **Authors**: Xinwei Long, Kai Tian, Peng Xu, Guoli Jia, Jingxuan Li, Sa Yang, Yihua Shao, Kaiyan Zhang, Che Jiang, Hao Xu, Yang Liu, Jiaheng Ma, Bowen Zhou
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have taken a great step towards AGI. Meanwhile, an increasing number of domain-specific problems such as math and programming boost these general-purpose models to continuously evolve via learning deeper expertise. Now is thus the time further to extend the diversity of specialized applications for knowledgeable LLMs, though collecting high quality data with unexpected and informative tasks is challenging. In this paper, we propose to use advertisement (ad) videos as a challenging test-bed to probe the ability of LLMs in perceiving beyond the objective physical content of common visual domain. Our motivation is to take full advantage of the clue-rich and information-dense ad videos' traits, e.g., marketing logic, persuasive strategies, and audience engagement. Our contribution is three-fold: (1) To our knowledge, this is the first attempt to use ad videos with well-designed tasks to evaluate LLMs. We contribute AdsQA, a challenging ad Video QA benchmark derived from 1,544 ad videos with 10,962 clips, totaling 22.7 hours, providing 5 challenging tasks. (2) We propose ReAd-R, a Deepseek-R1 styled RL model that reflects on questions, and generates answers via reward-driven optimization. (3) We benchmark 14 top-tier LLMs on AdsQA, and our \texttt{ReAd-R}~achieves the state-of-the-art outperforming strong competitors equipped with long-chain reasoning capabilities by a clear margin.
- **Summary**: Okay, here is a concise summary and critical evaluation of the "AdsQA: Towards Advertisement Video Understanding" paper, aiming for rigor and critical analysis.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AdsQA, a novel Video Question Answering (VideoQA) benchmark specifically designed for advertisement (ad) videos. The authors argue that ad videos present a unique and challenging testbed for Large Language Models (LLMs) due to their clue-rich, information-dense nature, including marketing logic, persuasive strategies, and audience engagement cues that go beyond simple physical content understanding.  The AdsQA dataset consists of 1,544 ad videos with 10,962 clips (22.7 hours) and defines five tasks: Visual Concept Understanding, Emotion Recognition, Theme and Core Message Extraction, Persuasion Strategy Mining, and Potential Audience Modeling. The authors also propose ReAd-R, a Deepseek-R1 styled RL model, for this domain and benchmark 14 LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes a commendable contribution by introducing a new domain (advertisement videos) for VideoQA, filling a void in existing benchmarks. It is the first attempt to specifically target the unique characteristics of ad videos and their associated reasoning requirements. The proposed ReAd-R RL model, while inspired by Deepseek-R1, is a valid application of RL within this novel context. *However*, the novelty of the tasks themselves, (Visual Concept Understanding, Emotion Recognition, Theme Extraction, Persuasion Strategy Mining and Audience Modeling) is less strong, as similar tasks exist in other video understanding datasets (albeit in different contexts). The true novelty rests in the *combination* of these tasks within the ad video domain.

*   **Significance/Impact:** The significance stems from several factors. First, it pushes LLMs towards more complex reasoning beyond simple "if A, then B" style logic. Second, the AdsQA dataset is publicly available, enabling further research in this domain. Third, the demonstrated weakness of existing state-of-the-art LLMs on AdsQA highlights a critical gap in their capabilities, prompting future research directions. *However*, the reliance on LLMs (specifically GPT-4) for evaluation raises concerns about potential biases and limitations of these models in assessing nuanced semantic understanding.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Benchmark:** The five tasks are clearly defined and address various aspects of ad understanding.
    *   **Comprehensive Dataset:** The size and diversity of the dataset (spanning different domains, video lengths) are strengths.
    *   **Publicly Available Dataset:** This allows for future research.
    *   **Strong Experimental Evaluation:** The benchmarking of multiple LLMs and the ablation studies provide valuable insights.
    *   **Good benchmark description and analysis**

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Task Overlap:** Some of the tasks may have overlaps, which could potentially lead to redundancy in the benchmark.
    *   **LLM-Based Evaluation:**  Relying solely on LLM-based evaluation, especially when claiming the dataset exposes weaknesses in LLMs, is a potential circularity. Human evaluation should play a stronger role.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Tasks:** As mentioned before, most of the individual tasks, in and of themselves, do not have much innovation.
    *   **Potential annotation bias**

* **Score**
Score: 7
*   **Justification:**
The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by introducing a new, challenging, and publicly available benchmark. While the individual tasks are not entirely novel, their combination and application to advertisement videos provide a unique context for evaluating LLMs. The paper also presents a good evaluation with SOTA LLMs. However, the reliance on LLM-based evaluation and some overlap among the tasks limit the paper's overall impact and score. The potential future contributions would be to see more human evaluation results as well as perhaps a more nuanced or diverse set of tasks.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### LADB: Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges for Semi-Supervised Domain Translation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08628v1)
- **Authors**: Xuqin Wang, Tao Wu, Yanfeng Zhang, Lu Liu, Dong Wang, Mingwei Sun, Yongliang Wang, Niclas Zeller, Daniel Cremers
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models excel at generating high-quality outputs but face challenges in data-scarce domains, where exhaustive retraining or costly paired data are often required. To address these limitations, we propose Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges (LADB), a semi-supervised framework for sample-to-sample translation that effectively bridges domain gaps using partially paired data. By aligning source and target distributions within a shared latent space, LADB seamlessly integrates pretrained source-domain diffusion models with a target-domain Latent Aligned Diffusion Model (LADM), trained on partially paired latent representations. This approach enables deterministic domain mapping without the need for full supervision. Compared to unpaired methods, which often lack controllability, and fully paired approaches that require large, domain-specific datasets, LADB strikes a balance between fidelity and diversity by leveraging a mixture of paired and unpaired latent-target couplings. Our experimental results demonstrate superior performance in depth-to-image translation under partial supervision. Furthermore, we extend LADB to handle multi-source translation (from depth maps and segmentation masks) and multi-target translation in a class-conditioned style transfer task, showcasing its versatility in handling diverse and heterogeneous use cases. Ultimately, we present LADB as a scalable and versatile solution for real-world domain translation, particularly in scenarios where data annotation is costly or incomplete.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "LADB: Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges for Semi-Supervised Domain Translation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Latent Aligned Diffusion Bridges (LADB), a semi-supervised learning framework designed for sample-to-sample domain translation, particularly in data-scarce scenarios. LADB leverages pre-trained source-domain diffusion models and trains a target-domain Latent Aligned Diffusion Model (LADM) on partially paired data.  The key idea is to align the source and target distributions in a shared latent space.  This approach facilitates deterministic domain mapping without full supervision, blending fidelity and diversity using both paired and unpaired data couplings. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of LADB on depth-to-image translation tasks, and extends the framework to handle multi-source and multi-target translation scenarios (e.g., using both depth maps and segmentation masks, or class-conditioned style transfer).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of aligning latent spaces for domain translation isn't entirely new. However, the specific combination of pre-trained source diffusion models, a semi-supervised training approach for a target-domain latent diffusion model using both paired and unpaired data, and the resulting "diffusion bridge" *is* novel. The application to partially paired data settings, which are common in real-world scenarios, is a significant and practical contribution. Furthermore, the extension to multi-source and multi-target settings increases the versatility of the framework.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in the following aspects:
    *   **Data Efficiency:** The ability to perform domain translation with only *partially* paired data addresses a major limitation of fully supervised and unpaired methods. This increases the applicability of diffusion models to domains where labeled data is expensive or scarce.
    *   **Control and Fidelity:**  By using paired data (even a small amount), the method offers a degree of control over the translation process that purely unsupervised methods often lack. The results show improved fidelity compared to baselines in various metrics.
    *   **Versatility:** The extensibility of the framework to multi-source and multi-target scenarios demonstrates its adaptability to more complex, real-world problems. This adds to the framework's broader impact and applicability.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results, demonstrating superior performance in depth-to-image translation compared to existing methods, especially under partial supervision. The quantitative results, supported by qualitative examples, solidify the claims.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-defined problem statement.
    *   Novel combination of techniques to address a practical problem.
    *   Strong empirical validation with comparisons against relevant baselines.
    *   Demonstrated extensibility to more complex scenarios.
    *   Comprehensive set of metrics used for evaluation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper can sometimes feel like an incremental improvement on existing latent diffusion model techniques, even though the combination is novel.
    *   While the experiments cover several scenarios, a more in-depth analysis of the limitations of the method would strengthen the paper. For example, how sensitive is the performance to the *quality* of the paired data? How does the framework handle significant domain gaps?
    *   Computational cost is not explicitly discussed. Training diffusion models, even in the latent space, can be resource-intensive. A discussion of the computational requirements compared to alternative methods would be beneficial.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The LADB framework presents a valuable contribution to the field of domain translation. Its ability to effectively utilize partially paired data, combined with strong empirical results and demonstrated extensibility, makes it a significant advancement over existing methods.  While the core idea builds upon existing concepts in latent diffusion modeling, the specific combination of techniques, the focus on semi-supervision, and the extension to multi-source and multi-target settings justify a high score. I am reducing it slightly because some of the contributions are evolutionary rather than revolutionary.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### BcQLM: Efficient Vision-Language Understanding with Distilled Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08715v1)
- **Authors**: Sike Xiang, Shuang Chen, Amir Atapour-Abarghouei
- **Abstract**: As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) advance, their large-scale architectures pose challenges for deployment in resource-constrained environments. In the age of large models, where energy efficiency, computational scalability and environmental sustainability are paramount, the development of lightweight and high-performance models is critical for real-world applications. As such, we propose a lightweight MLLM framework for end-to-end visual question answering. Our proposed approach centres on BreezeCLIP, a compact yet powerful vision-language encoder optimised for efficient multimodal understanding. With only 1.2 billion parameters overall, our model significantly reduces computational cost while achieving performance comparable to standard-size MLLMs. Experiments conducted on multiple datasets further validate its effectiveness in balancing accuracy and efficiency. The modular and extensible design enables generalisation to broader multimodal tasks. The proposed lightweight vision-language framework is denoted as BcQLM (BreezeCLIP-enhanced Q-Gated Multimodal Language Model). It offers a promising path toward deployable MLLMs under practical hardware constraints. The source code is available at https://github.com/thico0224/BcQLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "BcQLM: Efficient Vision-Language Understanding with Distilled Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion" introduces a lightweight multimodal large language model (MLLM) designed for visual question answering (VQA) in resource-constrained environments. The core innovation is BreezeCLIP, a compact vision-language encoder trained using contrastive learning and knowledge distillation from a larger CLIP model. It replaces BERT and ViT backbones with compact transformer modules.  The architecture also includes a Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion Module (Q-GCAM) to dynamically adjust visual and textual feature contributions.  The model uses a LLaMA-3.2-1B based decoder to generate answers. Experiments on GQA, VQAv2, and VizWiz datasets demonstrate competitive performance compared to larger MLLMs, while requiring significantly fewer parameters.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of existing techniques (knowledge distillation, compact transformer architectures, gated cross-modal attention) into a specific architecture tailored for efficient VQA. The individual components are not entirely novel, but the specific combination and adaptation for this purpose has merit. The BreezeCLIP encoder, while using known techniques, is optimized for the targeted task with a noticeable reduction in parameters. The Q-Gated Cross-Modal Fusion Module, while using gating mechanism which has been used in other architectures, the particular application and configuration provide a new way to dynamically balance the modalities. This can be seen as a good engineering solution.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing the pressing need for MLLMs that can be deployed on resource-constrained devices. Reducing the parameter count while maintaining competitive performance is a substantial contribution that could enable broader adoption of MLLMs in practical applications such as edge computing, healthcare, education, and assistive technologies. While the performance might not significantly outperform the absolute state-of-the-art models, the significant reduction in size combined with competitive performance makes it practical for deployment. The paper clearly demonstrates this with experiments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear problem definition and motivation:**  The paper explicitly addresses the challenge of deploying large MLLMs in resource-limited environments.
    *   **Well-defined architecture:** The architecture is clearly explained, and the rationale behind each component is provided.
    *   **Comprehensive experiments:** The experiments cover multiple VQA datasets and include ablation studies to demonstrate the effectiveness of each component.
    *   **Efficiency analysis:** The paper provides an analysis of inference efficiency, highlighting the speed and memory usage advantages of BcQLM.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental novelty:**  While the combination of techniques is novel, the individual components are not ground-breaking.
    *   **Limited Decoder Fine-tuning:** The decoder is only fine-tuned, this might be a limiting factor in adapting the model to the task.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a practical solution for deploying MLLMs in resource-constrained environments. The architectural choices and the experimental results provide evidence for its effectiveness. The modular approach also has great potential to be deployed in different tasks. However, the relatively incremental nature of its novelty keeps it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution. The choice of evaluation benchmarks also influences the scope of the paper and limits broad understanding of the capabilities of the paper. Furthermore, evaluation to video or audio data is not discussed in the evaluation. While the focus is on VQA, the modular design should be validated by different tasks. A more thorough investigation into the limitations of the model would strengthen it.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Data-driven generative simulation of SDEs using diffusion models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08731v1)
- **Authors**: Xuefeng Gao, Jiale Zha, Xun Yu Zhou
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces a new approach to generating sample paths of unknown stochastic differential equations (SDEs) using diffusion models, a class of generative AI models commonly employed in image and video applications. Unlike the traditional Monte Carlo methods for simulating SDEs, which require explicit specifications of the drift and diffusion coefficients, our method takes a model-free, data-driven approach. Given a finite set of sample paths from an SDE, we utilize conditional diffusion models to generate new, synthetic paths of the same SDE. To demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach, we conduct a simulation experiment to compare our method with alternative benchmark ones including neural SDEs. Furthermore, in an empirical study we leverage these synthetically generated sample paths to enhance the performance of reinforcement learning algorithms for continuous-time mean-variance portfolio selection, hinting promising applications of diffusion models in financial analysis and decision-making.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a data-driven approach for generating sample paths of unknown Stochastic Differential Equations (SDEs) using conditional diffusion models.  Unlike traditional Monte Carlo methods that require explicit knowledge of the drift and diffusion coefficients, this method only needs a finite set of sample paths from the target SDE. The method trains conditional diffusion models to generate stochastic increments in an autoregressive manner, effectively synthesizing new paths that resemble the original data distribution. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of this approach through experiments on various SDEs, comparing it with neural SDEs and a Monte Carlo based diffusion approach.  Furthermore, it showcases a potential application in finance by using the generated paths to enhance reinforcement learning algorithms for continuous-time mean-variance portfolio selection.

**Critical Evaluation of Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution by applying diffusion models to the problem of generating sample paths for unknown SDEs. The approach provides a model-free alternative to traditional methods, which are often limited by the need to explicitly define the SDE.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Model-Free Approach:**  A significant advantage is that the method doesn't require explicit knowledge of the SDE's drift and diffusion terms. This is crucial when the underlying equation is unknown or difficult to specify.
    *   **Diffusion Models:** Leverages the impressive capabilities of diffusion models which have surpassed GANs in many image generation tasks, indicating a robust generative capacity.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper demonstrates the method's effectiveness through simulations on both simple (OU process) and complex (high-dimensional GBM) SDEs. The comparison with existing methods shows superior performance in terms of KL divergence from the true distribution.
    *   **Practical Application:**  The use of generated paths to improve reinforcement learning for portfolio optimization highlights the potential of this method in finance and other fields.
    *   **Incremental advancement to existing research:** The authors highlight a key differentiation from similar work [12] in terms of score estimation.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models can be computationally expensive to train and sample from, which might limit their applicability to real-time or resource-constrained scenarios. This is not explicitly addressed in the paper, although implicitly implied by the use of neural networks in place of monte-carlo estimation of the score function.
    *   **Markov Assumption:** The method heavily relies on the Markov property of SDEs. This may not hold for all real-world time series data, limiting the generalizability of the approach. The paper doesn't discuss how the method would perform if the Markov property is weakly violated.
    *   **Financial Time Series Caveats:** While the application to portfolio optimization is promising, the paper acknowledges the limitations of assuming SDEs accurately represent real-world financial time series. Further research is needed to assess the robustness and performance of the method in realistic financial settings.
    *   **Limited Scope of Experimentation:**  While the paper provides results for KL divergence and portfolio optimization, a more comprehensive set of experiments, potentially involving different SDEs, parameter settings, and evaluation metrics, would strengthen the claims. For example, the authors could include an experiment to test the stability of the method when the time series is non-stationary, or more complex and realistic market simulations.

*   **Novelty:**

    *   While other generative models (GANs, Normalizing Flows) have been applied to SDE simulation, this paper is among the first to leverage the recent advances in conditional diffusion models.
    *   The paper makes a clear advancement from the approach in [12] through the score function approximation.

*   **Significance:**
    * The introduction of a model-free approach opens up new avenues for simulating SDEs in situations where the underlying equation is unknown.
    * The application of this approach to enhancing reinforcement learning in finance has potentially significant implications for improving automated trading and portfolio management strategies.

**Overall Assessment:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by introducing a novel and potentially powerful technique for SDE simulation. The empirical results are promising, and the application to portfolio optimization showcases its practical relevance. However, the computational cost, reliance on the Markov property, and limited scope of experimentation warrant caution. The authors did well in justifying the novelty through the comparisons with existing works.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The score reflects the paper's valuable contribution but also acknowledges its limitations. A score of 7 indicates that the work is a significant advancement over existing techniques, and addresses real-world applications. The paper makes a solid contribution to the field with a novel approach, and while there are limitations with respect to the experimentation scope and some assumptions, the paper provides valuable contributions and justifications.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Calibrating MLLM-as-a-judge via Multimodal Bayesian Prompt Ensembles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08777v1)
- **Authors**: Eric Slyman, Mehrab Tanjim, Kushal Kafle, Stefan Lee
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used to evaluate text-to-image (TTI) generation systems, providing automated judgments based on visual and textual context. However, these "judge" models often suffer from biases, overconfidence, and inconsistent performance across diverse image domains. While prompt ensembling has shown promise for mitigating these issues in unimodal, text-only settings, our experiments reveal that standard ensembling methods fail to generalize effectively for TTI tasks. To address these limitations, we propose a new multimodal-aware method called Multimodal Mixture-of-Bayesian Prompt Ensembles (MMB). Our method uses a Bayesian prompt ensemble approach augmented by image clustering, allowing the judge to dynamically assign prompt weights based on the visual characteristics of each sample. We show that MMB improves accuracy in pairwise preference judgments and greatly enhances calibration, making it easier to gauge the judge's true uncertainty. In evaluations on two TTI benchmarks, HPSv2 and MJBench, MMB outperforms existing baselines in alignment with human annotations and calibration across varied image content. Our findings highlight the importance of multimodal-specific strategies for judge calibration and suggest a promising path forward for reliable large-scale TTI evaluation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of calibrating Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) used as judges for text-to-image (TTI) generation systems.  While MLLMs offer automated evaluation based on visual and textual context, they often exhibit biases, overconfidence, and inconsistent performance.  The authors introduce Multimodal Mixture-of-Bayesian Prompt Ensembles (MMB) to mitigate these issues.  MMB combines a Bayesian prompt ensemble approach with image clustering, dynamically adjusting prompt weights based on the visual characteristics of each image. The results from evaluation on HPSv2 and MJBench show that MMB outperforms existing methods in aligning with human annotations and improving calibration across various image types.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the multimodal-aware prompt ensembling strategy.  While prompt ensembling is not entirely new, its adaptation to multimodal TTI evaluation by incorporating image clustering is a significant contribution. Previous ensembling methods mostly focused on text, failing to adapt weights based on visual data. The Bayesian framework for prompt weighting is also a valuable component borrowed and extended from prior work.
* **Significance:** Improving the reliability and calibration of MLLM judges is crucial for scaling TTI evaluation. Human evaluation is often costly and time-consuming, making automated evaluation desirable. A well-calibrated judge allows for more efficient evaluation pipelines where high-confidence judgments can be automatically accepted, and low-confidence cases can be directed to human reviewers. The demonstrated improvements in accuracy and calibration using MMB address a significant bottleneck in TTI development.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper provides compelling empirical evidence on two widely used benchmarks (HPSv2 and MJBench), demonstrating the superiority of MMB over existing baselines. The thorough experimental setup with statistical significance tests (permutation test, FDR control) strengthens the claims.
    * **Well-Defined Method:** MMB is clearly defined and explained, making it relatively easy to understand and potentially implement. The variational inference framework for learning the prompt weights is well-established.
    * **Thorough Ablation Studies:** The paper includes thorough tests of how different model components impact model performance through experimentation (varying number of clusters, samples, and prompts).

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Pre-trained Embeddings:** The method relies on a pre-trained image embedding function (CLIP).  The choice of this embedding function could affect the clustering results and the overall performance of MMB. More discussion of why CLIP was chosen and the potential impact of using alternative embedding models would be beneficial.
    * **Complexity:** MMB adds some complexity compared to simpler ensembling methods. The need for image clustering and learning group-specific weights introduces additional hyperparameters and computational overhead. The cost of training is mitigated by only requiring a single forward pass to test, however, is still worth keeping in mind.
    * **Limited Generalization Discussion:** Although the results demonstrate the efficacy of MMB on two important datasets, the paper could benefit from more discussion about the generalizability of the approach to other multimodal tasks.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of TTI evaluation and beyond.  Improved MLLM judges can accelerate the development of new TTI models and improve the quality of generated images. The idea of multimodal-aware prompt ensembling can be extended to other tasks and modalities, such as video generation, audio generation, and more general vision-language tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the novelty of the multimodal-aware prompt ensembling strategy, the compelling empirical results on relevant benchmarks, and the potential impact on scaling TTI evaluation and general vision-language tasks, this paper represents a significant contribution. The weaknesses, such as the dependence on pre-trained embeddings and the increased complexity, are relatively minor compared to the overall strengths. However, the model can be improved further by expanding the scope of experiments to more MLLMs, for example, open-source LLMs.

### Do All Autoregressive Transformers Remember Facts the Same Way? A Cross-Architecture Analysis of Recall Mechanisms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.08778v1)
- **Authors**: Minyeong Choe, Haehyun Cho, Changho Seo, Hyunil Kim
- **Abstract**: Understanding how Transformer-based language models store and retrieve factual associations is critical for improving interpretability and enabling targeted model editing. Prior work, primarily on GPT-style models, has identified MLP modules in early layers as key contributors to factual recall. However, it remains unclear whether these findings generalize across different autoregressive architectures. To address this, we conduct a comprehensive evaluation of factual recall across several models -- including GPT, LLaMA, Qwen, and DeepSeek -- analyzing where and how factual information is encoded and accessed. Consequently, we find that Qwen-based models behave differently from previous patterns: attention modules in the earliest layers contribute more to factual recall than MLP modules. Our findings suggest that even within the autoregressive Transformer family, architectural variations can lead to fundamentally different mechanisms of factual recall.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how different autoregressive Transformer models (GPT, LLaMA, Qwen, DeepSeek) store and retrieve factual associations.  Prior work mainly focused on GPT-style models and identified MLP modules in early layers as key to factual recall. This paper examines whether this generalizes across different autoregressive architectures. The authors conduct layer-wise and module-wise (Attention vs. MLP) analysis and find that Qwen-based models exhibit a different pattern: attention modules in the earliest layers contribute more to factual recall than MLP modules.  This suggests that architectural variations within the autoregressive Transformer family can lead to fundamentally different mechanisms for factual recall. The study involves causal tracing, severing interventions, and factual prediction analysis with an improved semantic similarity-based metric.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addressing a significant gap:** The paper tackles an important question about the generalization of factual recall mechanisms across different autoregressive Transformer architectures. Most prior work has been narrowly focused on the GPT family, leaving the mechanisms of other models relatively unexplored.
    *   **Comprehensive analysis:** The study employs a multi-faceted approach, including restoration effects, severing effects, and factual prediction analysis, providing a more complete picture of how different modules contribute to factual recall.
    *   **Improved evaluation metric:** The semantic similarity-based evaluation metric addresses a weakness in previous string matching-based methods, making the evaluation more robust across diverse architectures that can generate semantically equivalent but lexically different outputs.
    *   **Clear identification of architecture-specific differences:** The paper clearly identifies and highlights the differences between GPT-based and Qwen-based models, showing that Qwen models rely more on early Attention layers for factual recall.
    *   **Practical implications:** The findings have implications for model editing, interpretability, and designing knowledge-intensive tasks, especially in choosing which model and method to use.
    *   The use of single vs multi-token analysis brings additional insight.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited explanation of architectural divergences:** While the paper demonstrates that Qwen-based models behave differently, the underlying reasons for these architectural divergences are not fully explained.  The paper proposes tokenization and architecture might be the drivers, but a deeper dive into *why* the different architectures lead to these storage and retrieval differences would strengthen the study. Appendix D tries to address this but is only preliminary analysis.
    *   **Lack of a clear theoretical framework:** The paper could benefit from a more formal theoretical framework for understanding how different architectural choices influence the encoding and retrieval of factual knowledge. This would make the findings more generalizable and less reliant on empirical observations.
    *   **Scalability concerns:** While the study examines several models, they are not the very largest models currently available. Whether the trends observed hold true at even larger scales (e.g., hundreds of billions or trillions of parameters) is not addressed.
    *   **Limited interpretability of knockout:** The knockout experiments, where the outputs of layers are set to zero, can have a number of side effects that are not investigated.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The core finding that Qwen-based models rely more on attention layers for factual storage in early layers compared to the MLP focus of GPT-style models *is* novel and challenges the assumption that factual recall mechanisms are uniform across all autoregressive Transformers. This is a significant contribution and creates opportunities to look into why this occurs.
    * The finding about the impact of the subject token length also adds a useful nuance.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's novelty lies in demonstrating that not all autoregressive Transformers store and recall facts in the same way. The identification of architectural differences in factual recall mechanisms, particularly the contrast between Qwen and GPT models, is a valuable contribution. This contributes to the community's knowledge. However, the lack of complete explanations (more of an exploration) and some limited scope detract from it being truly groundbreaking.
